# Sūrah al-Aʿrāf (Chapter 7) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah of the Heights**

Al-Aʿrāf, "the Heights," is the seventh chapter of the Qur'an, comprising 206 verses, and it stands as one of the great mid-length Makkan sūrahs whose structure resembles a vast architectural plan: a broad statement of the Law, then a series of rooms — narrative suites in which the sacred history of the prophets is told again and again, each time widening the circle of warning. The name comes from the word *al-aʿrāf* in verses 46 and 48, the "heights" from which certain men will look across a partition between the Garden and the Fire, identifying the people of both sides "by their marks." Classical commentators differ on what these heights are: some say a ridge or terrace between Paradise and Hell, others say a level of Paradise from which its inhabitants survey all that is below. Whatever the precise identification, the image gives the sūrah its name and one of its deepest psychological tones: the standing, unresolved middle — the place of those whose outcome is not yet the reward they hoped for, who can see the blessed and the damned, and who must watch. It is a name that keeps the sūrah, and everyone who reads it, out of comfort.

**A Late Makkan Composition with a Subtler Edge**

In the order of revelation al-Aʿrāf is counted among the later Makkan sūrahs, and many scholars place it just before al-Anʿām, with some authorities reporting that verses 163–167 were revealed in Madinah. Its immediate audience is the same as al-Anʿām's: the idolatrous Quraysh who refused to accept Muḥammad ﷺ as a prophet. But where al-Anʿām argues directly with the arguments of its opponents, al-Aʿrāf takes a subtler, less polemical course. It does not meet the Makkans point for point; instead it turns them toward the past and says, in effect: look at what your story has always looked like, from the first of the prophets to the last of the cities. The sūrah's central thesis, stated and restated in many forms, is that the consequences of rejecting the signs of God are not hypothetical; they are historical, and they are repeatable. The chapters of sacred history — Adam, Noah, Hūd, Ṣāli, Lūt, Shuʿayb, Mūsā and Pharaoh — are arranged not as antiquarian material but as a courtroom record, each case closed the same way.

**The Shape of the Sūrah**

The opening verses (1–10) state the case of the Book: it is sent down for the Prophet's breast, for warning, and for the remembrance of the believers; it is followed by the fate of destroyed towns, by the questioning of messengers and their peoples, and by the weighing of deeds. Then the first great narrative begins (11–28): the creation of Adam, the refusal of Iblīs, the temptation in the Garden, the descent, and the clothing that follows. From the clothing the sūrah moves to adornment and temperance (31–33), to the appointed term of every nation (34–39), and then to the famous passage of the Hereafter in which the people of the Garden call out to the people of the Fire, and the men on the Heights call to those they know by their marks (44–50). A brief discourse on the signs of God in creation (52–58) introduces the long procession of the destroyed peoples (59–93): Noah, the people of the well and the palm-grove (Hūd), the she-camel of Ṣāli, the transgression of the people of Lūt, and the commerce and religion of Shuʿayb. The Mūsā narrative (103–156) is the sūrah's longest and most detailed suite: the staff and the white hand, the confrontation with Pharaoh, the magicians, the plagues, the parting of the sea, the drowning, the mountain, the golden calf, and the covenant of the elders. A verse identifies the Prophet as the "unlettered" Muḥammad ﷺ written in the Torah and the Gospel (157). The sūrah then returns to Israel's later history — the breach of the covenant, the punishment of the Sabbath-breakers, the city in the land (161–67) — and closes, after a passage on the character of the Prophet and his mission (158–188), with the great verses of the Covenant of the Looms (172), the story of the two brothers and the field (176–77), the parable of the dog in the Cave (178–81), and the final, serene vision of the angels in the Presence of God, who are not too proud to worship Him, glorifying Him and prostrating to Him (206). The arc of the sūrah — from the letters that open the Book to the angels that close it — is deliberate: it begins with the mystery of Divine speech and ends with the order of Divine worship, and between the two, every human story that could carry the warning is told at length.

**The Method of Its Warning**

What distinguishes al-Aʿrāf from its neighbors is the patience of its method. It does not once say "you Makkans will be destroyed" in so many words in the narrative sections; it simply tells the case files and lets the listener recognize himself. This is a warning designed to bypass the defender's defenses: when a man is told directly that he is wrong, he prepares his answer; when he is told a story of a people he knows, he watches it, and in the watching, he confesses. The sūrah trusts its evidence and its order. It also pairs the narratives with the present address constantly — "O Children of Adam" — so that every story is also a mirror. The result is a sūrah that reads less like a sermon than like a history written by God for the use of His creatures, in which the past is not the past but a live demonstration, and in which the last page, the page of the angels and of the final reckoning, is the only page that is already finished.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:1

> **Alif. Lām. Mīm. Ṣād.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Four Letters, and Only These, Before This Book**

The seventh sūrah of the Qur'an does not begin with a word, a name, a blessing, or a story. It begins with four letters of the Arabic alphabet, recited by their own names in that order: *Alif, Lām, Mīm, Ṣād* — a combination that belongs to this sūrah alone. Of the twenty-nine sūrahs that open with the so-called "separated letters" (*al-ḥurūf al-muqaṭṭaʿāt*), no other carries precisely this quartet. Alif, Lām, Mīm together open the second, third, and twenty-ninth chapters; Mīm alone opens others; and ṣād opens chapter thirty-eight. But the four-letter sequence *ʾalif lām mīm ṣād* stands in the muṣḥaf exactly once, and that once is here, at the gate of al-Aʿrāf. The repetition of the formula across the Book establishes that the letters are not a scribal accident but a standing feature of Divine speech in the Qur'an; the singularity of this particular combination then marks this sūrah with its own signature, as though the Book announced, before a single sentence was spoken, that what follows is a specific and unrepeatable message.

**A Signature of Mystery**

The commentators' first instinct was the same as it was for every set of the separated letters: to acknowledge that the letters are true, real, and weighty, and to acknowledge that their precise meaning is one of the points in which God has concealed knowledge from us. It is transmitted among the early authorities that the meaning of the letters is a *munkarat* — a mystery that the Prophet ﷺ did not explain and that therefore was not to be forced. Some of the later reporters, as the sources gather them, held that the opening letters are a name of God, and that this particular sequence may stand for the "Greatest Name" (*al-Ism al-Aʿẓam*), the name by which God is known to Himself before He is known to creation. Others held that the letters are a name for the Qur'an itself, the Book signing its own opening with the raw material of its own language. Neither opinion is demonstrable; both are preserved in the tradition as reverent conjecture. The soundest course, and the one the older commentators consistently favored, is to hold what is certain: that God opened His Book with these letters, that no meaning has been authentically fixed to them, and that their silence at the threshold is itself a teaching.

**The Challenge Under the Surface**

What can be said with confidence is the function the letters serve in the pattern of the Book. In most of the sūrahs that open with the separated letters, the very next verse mentions the Book or the revelation: *Alif Lām Mīm, this is the Book of which there is no doubt*; *Alif Lām Mīm, a Book We have sent down unto you*; and here, *Alif Lām Mīm Ṣād — [this is] a Book sent down unto thee*. The letters, in other words, are the herald of the Book, the sound the Book makes before it speaks. And there is a second, deeper function that the later commentators drew out at length. The letters are the very letters of the Arab's own tongue. The Qur'an was revealed in the Arabic of the Quraysh, and the Quraysh were the masters of Arabic, the people whose poetry was the measure of eloquence. By opening sūrah after sūrah with the alphabet itself, the revelation performs a quiet demonstration: here are your own letters, your own sounds, your own material, in the hand of your own tongue's Speaker — and from this material, a Book of this order. The explicit challenge, *bring a sūrah like it*, comes elsewhere; here the challenge is implied by the opening itself, the way a master archer lays a bow at the feet of his rivals without saying a word.

**A Pause Before the Storm**

There is also a spiritual function, one the practical commentators of the devotional strain emphasized. A sūrah that opens with isolated letters forces the reciter into a moment of pure voice before meaning: the lips form sounds that do not yet signify, and in that gap the heart is suspended between the letter and the word, between the sound and the sense. For the worshipper this is a small discipline with a large effect. The sūrah that follows these four letters is among the sternest in the Qur'an — it will recount destroyed cities, a fallen devil, a drowned pharaoh, a people cursed in their fields and their sabbaths. To approach it, God has placed a pause: four letters, no meaning, only Presence. The listener is given, in the first second of the sūrah, the posture the entire sūrah demands — the posture of a witness who has not yet heard the verdict. That is the office of these letters at the gate of al-Aʿrāf: to make the reader still, to mark the Book as a Book from above, and to open the page.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:2

> **[This is] a Book sent down unto thee — so let there be no constriction in thy breast because of it — that thou mayest warn thereby, and a Reminder for the believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Book Named at Its Own Gate**

The four letters of the opening are not left hanging in the air. The very next word names what they herald: *This is a Book* (*hādhā kitābun*). Grammarians note the figure in the phrase: the noun *kitāb* is in the state of explanation (*ṣila al-bayān*) to the demonstrative *hādhā*, as though the Book is so near that it is pointed at, so present that it is shown rather than described. And it is immediately qualified with the passive participle *anẓala* — "sent down" — which does two things at once: it declares the Book's origin (it is not produced, compiled, or composed by any hand in this world) and its direction (it descends, it comes from above, it is addressed downward to the human level). The combination — "this is a Book sent down unto thee" — is the Qur'an's own self-introduction in miniature, and it is the first complete sentence of the sūrah. Everything that follows in al-Aʿrāf — the destroyed towns, the prophets, the Hereafter — is the unfolding of a single claim that is made in this verse: that a Book has come from God, and that it has come to one man first, and through him to the rest of the world.

**The Constriction in the Breast**

The address now turns to the Prophet ﷺ, and it turns to the most sensitive place in him: *so let there be no constriction in thy breast because of it*. The word is *ḥaraj* or, in the common reading, the sense of *ḍayq* — a narrowing, a tightening, a closing-in, as when a chest constricts under fear or grief so that the breath grows short. The Qur'an elsewhere places the *ṣadr*, the breast or chest, precisely as the organ of this kind of distress: it is where the word that is too heavy to bear "weighs down" (2:255, where the recitation of the signs is a *ṣadr ḍīq* — "a narrow breast"), and it is where the heart sits. The Qur'an's psychology is bodily before it is abstract: the distress of the messenger is not a thought but a physical tightening, the kind of compression a person feels in the pit of the chest when he sees his own people turn away from the very thing that could save them.

This is not a hypothetical. The history of the first years of the mission is a history of constriction. The Prophet ﷺ had come down from the cave of Ḥirā with a breast that, by his own account to Khadija, was afraid for itself; he had stood before the Quraysh and seen the faces of men he loved harden against him; he had been stoned, insulted, and blocked in the streets of Makkah. The constriction this verse speaks of is not doubt about the Book — that question never troubled him — it is the sorrow and fear of the messenger who carries a warning his people will refuse. And the command "let there be no constriction" is not a command to stop feeling; it is a command to locate the feeling correctly. The Qur'an elsewhere treats exactly this kind of messenger's distress with the same medicine: *do not grieve over them* (16:127), *do not be in distress* (28:21, 56), and the famous prayer taught for the very condition — when a believer's heart tightens, he is taught to say, "O God, Lord of the Throne, Lord of the Might, Lord of the Greatness" — a dhikr whose name in the sources is the *dhikr al-ḍiqq*, the remembrance for constriction. The cure for a constricted breast is not less feeling but the transfer of the feeling to its proper object: the One who owns the constriction and can open it.

**Warning as Purpose, Remembrance as Result**

The verse then states the Book's double office in one sentence. First: *that thou mayest warn thereby*. The root is *n-z-r*, and the word *nadhīr* carries the sense of one who gives notice of a coming harm, the one who stands at the gate and says, a caravan of trouble is coming, and the door is this. Warning is forward-looking: it addresses a future that can still be avoided. The Book was sent down, on this account, so that the Prophet would perform the function of the warners — *nadhīr* is one of his standing titles in the Qur'an, as it is of Noah, Hud, and all the prophets told the same story in this sūrah. Second: *and a Reminder for the believers*. The word is *dhikrā*, from the root *d-h-r*, remembrance. If warning looks forward to the punishment that is avoided, remembrance looks present and inward: it is the keeping of God in the heart, the repeated turning back to the source. The same Book therefore performs two different functions on two different hearers. For the one who has not entered the path, it is a warning: it shows the fate of the destroyed towns and the weighing of deeds. For the one who has entered, it is a remembrance: it keeps the heart warm, the direction steady, and the gratitude fresh. The sentence is a complete map of the Qur'an's relationship to its two audiences, and it is a map that the sūrah will then spend 205 verses walking.

**The Logic of the Command**

There is a logic here worth stating plainly, for it is the logic that holds the whole prophetic enterprise together. The Book was sent down "unto thee" — to one man — and the reason given for not letting his breast constrict is the task itself: *that thou mayest warn*. The messenger must be free of the constriction of fear of his people's answer, because a messenger whose chest is closed by the fear of rejection will deliver a diminished warning, or delay it, or soften it into something his people can swallow. God's command is thus a command about the conditions of effective communication: the message must be carried whole. And the same law, by the pattern of the verse, extends beyond the Prophet to every person who has received the message and carries it. The believer who speaks the truth he has been given and feels his breast constrict at the cost he will pay — the cost of the family's disapproval, the office's disfavor, the crowd's laughter — is addressed by the same verse in the same way: the constriction is real, the feeling is not a sin, and the cure is to place the feeling before the One who sees the breast and opens it. The verse, in other words, is not only a Makkan command to one man; it is a standing statute for every messenger of the Book in every age, including the private age in which the message is carried by an individual rather than a state.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:3

> **Follow that which has been sent down unto you from your Lord, and follow not any protectors apart from Him. Little do you reflect!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Address Widens**

The previous verse spoke to the Prophet in the singular. This verse speaks in the plural, and the shift is deliberate: the command now reaches the community, and beyond the community, all people. *Follow that which has been sent down unto you from your Lord* — the "you" is the collective "you" of every person who has received the Book and stood in the light of it. The object of the command is the revealed Book, the very Book named in the preceding verse, and the verb is *ittabiʿū*, "follow," from the root *ṭ-w-y*, which pictures walking behind someone on a path: to follow is to place oneself on the same track as the one you follow, to let his footfall set your pace. To "follow" a revealed Book is therefore not an abstract approval of its truth but a practical alignment: one makes the Book the route of one's life, the direction of one's choices, the standard by which one measures what to take and what to leave. The Qur'an uses the same verb for the following of the prophets and the following of the law, and the pattern is constant: faith in the Book without following it is like knowing the map and walking a different road.

**Protectors Besides God**

The negative half of the verse is the one that carries the weight: *and follow not any protectors apart from Him*. The word is *awliyāʾ*, the plural of *walī*, from the root *w-l-y*, which means closeness — a wali is literally a close one, and the word has grown in Arabic to cover the whole family of relations: the guardian, the patron, the ally, the protector, the friend who has authority over a person or a cause. To "take" (*ittakhadhū*) protectors apart from God is to place oneself in a relation of closeness, dependence, and loyalty toward something or someone other than God, such that one's security, direction, and love are attached to that other thing. The Qur'an returns to this phrase again and again, and each use clarifies the range of what the "protectors" can be. The disbelievers "took their idols as protectors" (2:257); men "took their satans as protectors apart from God" (18:50, 43:36); and in this very sūrah, a few verses later, the same sentence is repeated with the subject made explicit: *they took satans as their protectors apart from God and deem them rightly guided* (7:30). But the classical commentators broadened the phrase further than the literal, and rightly so. In the early Muslim community the "protectors" apart from God were often not idols at all but human beings — the associates, friends, and kindred who incited the Companions to their old religion, whose approval was the real currency of safety, and whose disapproval was the real punishment. The *walī* in this verse is whatever stands between a person and God in the distribution of his loyalty, whether it is a carved stone, a demon of the unseen, or a living human being whose pleasure is sought as a god is sought.

The Qur'an gives the logic of the error in one of its sharpest parables, in Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt:

> *"The parable of those who take protectors apart from God is that of the spider that makes a house. Truly the frailest of houses is the spider's house, if they but knew."* (29:41)

The image is precise. The spider believes in the strength of its web; the web is the strongest thing in the world to the spider, and the frailest in the world to everything else. The "protectors" apart from God are exactly this: they feel like the whole of security to the person attached to them, and they are the weakest possible thing at the moment of need. When the hour comes in which a protector is tested — the illness, the loss, the death, the hour in which the money cannot be commanded and the status cannot be summoned — the web is cut, and the one who took the spider for a house has no house. The verse's command is therefore not a matter of doctrine but of engineering: attach your security to that which is actually strong.

**The Rebuke of the Unreflecting**

The verse closes with a rebuke that is short and very old in the Qur'an: *Little do you reflect!* The word is *taʿqilūn*, from the root *ʿ-q-l*, the same root as *ʿaql*, the intellect, and the word *taʿqīl* — to reason, to judge, to grasp, to hold a thing in the mind and weigh it. The rebuke is not against the existence of the intellect but against its non-use. The logic of the whole verse is a piece of reasoning: if the Book is from God, and if God is the only protector that is real, then to follow the Book and to take no other protector is the only rationally consistent position; to follow the Book in words while attaching one's life to other protectors is to hold two incompatible directions at once. And the person who does this is not ignorant; he is one who has chosen not to reflect. The Qur'an repeats this exact rebuke in the same short form in several places — "Little do you reflect!" (27:62, 40:58), "Do you not reflect?" (69:42, and dozens of close variants) — and the repetition is itself a teaching: the central failure of the human heart is not a lack of intelligence but a refusal to apply it to the things that matter.

**The Two Loyalties in Modern Form**

The verse's structure — one positive command and one negative prohibition, joined by the logic of exclusive loyalty — is the structure of every real allegiance in human life. A citizen who follows the constitution and also follows a criminal gang is not a citizen in any sense, even if the gang is beloved and the constitution is distant. A person who says he serves God and serves money in the same hour is in the same position, and the Qur'an's phrase for it is the one in this verse: he has taken *awliyāʾ* apart from God. The modern form of the disease is not less real than the Makkah form; it is only better dressed. The "protectors" of a modern life — the career that must not be risked, the reputation that must not be damaged, the crowd that must not be opposed, the money that must not be questioned — are taken in exactly the way the Quraysh took their idols: not as objects of worship in a temple, but as objects of dependence in the heart, the things one actually turns to when the crisis comes. And the cure is the one the verse names: *taʿqīl*, reflection, the simple and brutal test that every real protector fails — *what will this thing do for me in the hour in which I am alone?* The idol, the status, the money, the friend all fail that test; only the One the verse names passes it. To reflect, in the sense this verse requires, is to run that test honestly, and to let the result arrange one's loyalties.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:4

> **How many a town have We destroyed! Our Might came upon them by night, or while they took their ease at midday.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rhetoric of the Many**

The verse opens with a rhetorical plural in the form of a question: *How many a town have We destroyed!* The Arabic word is *kaẓīr*, and the figure is the one the grammarians call the *mabnāʾ li-ḍamīr al-kathīrah* — a form that does not expect an answer, because the answer is not a number but a volume: many, so many that counting has stopped being possible. The construction is deliberate. The Qur'an does not say "I destroyed your town" or "I destroyed one town"; it says *how many*, and the listener's mind is immediately placed in front of a procession of cities, a city after a city, each one with its own name and its own end. This is the sūrah announcing its own method. The long narrative suite that follows — the people of Noah, the people of Hud, the people of Salih, the people of Lot, the people of Shuayb, and then the people of Pharaoh — is the gallery that this single verse opens. The verse is the door of the exhibit: step in, and the walls are lined with the cases of the destroyed towns.

The "town" (*qaryah*) is the unit of the Qur'an's moral history. In the Qur'an, sin is not primarily an individual act in the modern sense; it is a communal condition. A town is destroyed, not a man; a city is struck, not a household. The reason is logical: the corruption that merits destruction is never the corruption of one man but the corruption of a whole order of life — the judges who have sold judgment, the markets that have cheated by measure, the pulpit that has mocked the messenger, the whole civic fabric that has made the false into the normal and the true into the offense. When the Qur'an says *how many a town*, it is naming the scale at which its history is written: the scale of the community, where the moral health of a place is a fact that can be seen from a distance, and where the punishment, when it comes, is the settlement of a communal account.

**The Hour of the Strike**

The verse then names the hour of the destruction, and the naming is one of the most pointed things in the Qur'an: *Our Might came upon them by night, or while they took their ease at midday*. The word for "Might" here is *ʿaẓāb* — punishment, and more than punishment, the punishment that displays a power: it is the word used for the divine strikes that the Qur'an recounts in its narrative suites, the wind that took a whole people in their beds, the flood that rose over the high places, the earthquake that overthrew a city with its dwellings. And the word "came upon them" (*jāʾahum*) is the word of the unexpected: it came *upon* them, not toward them, not as a thing they had arranged for — it fell on them.

The two hours named are the two hours in which a people is furthest from readiness. By night: the hour of rest, the hour when the senses are closed, the hour when no one is watching and no one is on guard. At midday, *while they took their ease* — the Arabic word is *ghināʾ* — the hour of play, of diversion, of a people that has settled into the rhythm of its own comfort and is attending to its own pleasures. The logic of the two hours is the same: it is in the hour of maximum relaxation that the heart is farthest from the awareness of God, and it is in that hour that the account is closed. The Qur'an's teaching is not that God strikes the sleeping or the playing man as a cruelty; it is that the man who has rejected the warning has already chosen the hour of his own account. He has spent his days in the ease that the verse names, and the ease is the opposite of the readiness for which the warning was sent. When the warning is rejected, the man has, in effect, said: I will not be ready. And the destruction, when it comes, comes at the hour that is the true shape of his choice: the hour in which he is not ready.

The Qur'an's own narratives keep the two hours in view. The punishment of Lot's people is spoken of as the striking of the towns *in the night* (15:65); the punishment of the people of Shuayb is the one that "came to them in the morning" (76:6, 46:26); the flood of Noah rose in the hour of their deepest security. The pattern is constant and it is the pattern of this verse: the destruction does not come with a second notice, and it does not come at a convenient hour. It comes when the people is at rest, because the people that has rejected the messenger has already made the rest its religion.

**The Warning of the Hour**

There is a law here that extends beyond the literal destruction of the towns, and the classical commentators drew it out. The law is this: once God has sent the warning and the people has refused it, the man is under no obligation to the man's comfort any longer. The comfort is the man's own, and the man has chosen to spend it in the rejection. The "ease at midday" is therefore not a description of God's method but a description of the man's state. The town that is destroyed at midday is the town that had been at ease at midday for years, and the midday of the destruction is only the last midday of a long noon of ease. The verse, applied to the individual life, is a warning about the hour in which a person feels most at ease with himself — the hour in which the career is secure, the health is good, the friends are pleased, and the question of the account has been pushed to the far side of the calendar. That hour is the hour the verse names, and the verse says the account can close in it, without notice, in the middle of the ease, in the very moment the man is furthest from the preparation. The practical consequence is not fear but readiness: the man who keeps the awareness of the account open does not sleep without the awareness, and he does not play without the awareness, and the destruction that "comes upon" the unready does not come upon him, because he is, in the Qur'an's word, the one who has been *prepared* — and the prepared man is not caught by the hour, in either world.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:5

> **Their plea, when Our Might came upon them, was but to say, “Truly we were wrongdoers.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence That Arrives Too Late**

The fourth verse of the sūrah left the destroyed towns asleep in their beds or resting at midday; this fifth verse reports what they said when the Might of God arrived. The Arabic is *fa-mā kāna daʿwāhum idh jāʾahum baʾsunā illā an qālū innā kunnā ẓālimīn*. The translation "plea" renders *daʿwā*, a noun from the root *d-ʿ-w*, to call out. Its lexical range is instructive: in the market and the courtroom of the Arabs the same word meant a *claim* — the assertion a man entered on his own behalf. What the verse records, then, is not a prayer. It is the last claim the townspeople filed before a court whose session had already ended.

The grammar carries the weight. *Mā kāna … illā* is a restrictive construction: the whole of their speech was *nothing but* that one clause. Not a defence, not a bargain, not an appeal to ancestry or custom — the entire content of their final hour collapsed into four words: *we were wrongdoers*.

**Why the Confession Cannot Help Them**

The word rendered "wrongdoers" is *ẓālimūn*, from *ẓ-l-m*. Its primary sense is not cruelty but displacement: to put a thing where it does not belong. A man is a *ẓālim* in this Qur'anic sense when he installs himself in a station that is not his — when he sits in judgment over what belongs to God, treats his own desire as the standard, and answers for an account that was never his to settle. The confession in the verse is therefore exact. The townspeople do not confess a list of crimes; they confess a mislocation, a life spent in a place that was not theirs.

The tragedy is that the words are true and the time is gone. The Qur'an draws the line between two confessions with unmistakable clarity. Repentance belongs to the living hour: *“repentance is not accepted from those who knowingly persist in sin until they start dying, and then cry, ‘Now I repent!’”* (4:18). Pharaoh made this confession with the water at his throat — *“I believe!”* — and the answer still echoes: *“Now you believe? But you always disobeyed and were one of the corruptors”* (10:90–91). The dead cry out for a second term: *“My Lord! Let me go back, so I may do good in what I left behind”* — and the door answers: *“Never!”* (23:99–100).

Across those scenes the pattern is one anatomy in three parts: the recognition is genuine, the repair is impossible, and the verdict is unchanged. The verse therefore does not describe penitence; it describes penitence's counterfeit — the moment when agreement with God's judgment costs nothing, because nothing can any longer be rescued by it.

**The Clock Inside the Verse**

Read this way, 7:5 stops being a report about vanished nations and becomes a measurement of the reader's own position. The sentence *we were wrongdoers* has two lives. Spoken inside the season of the open door, it is the beginning of the turning; spoken after the closing, it is the final entry in a ledger already sealed. The same words, one hour apart, are two different acts — and the two verses before this one established that no one controls the hour, since the torment came at night or at midday, unannounced.

The Qur'an places the cure beside the diagnosis. *“Whoever does wrong or sins and then seeks forgiveness of God will find God Forgiving and Merciful”* (4:110) is what the townspeople could have said and did not, in the years when saying it would have worked. The Prophet ﷺ described God's reception of such a turning in terms that make this verse's warning land: *“God is more joyful at the repentance of His servant than one of you who finds his camel that he had lost in the desert.”* (Muslim 2747).

The destroyed town and the returning servant say the same sentence and end in opposite places. What separates them is not the sincerity of the tone but their position on the clock of mercy — and the reader of this verse stands, by definition, in the part of the hour where the sentence still works.

**The Ruins the Sūrah Tells Its Readers to Read**

The sūrah does not leave the destroyed towns in the past tense of a report. It tells its audience that the evidence is still lying around them. *“Say: Travel throughout the land and see the fate of the deniers”* (6:11; cf. 16:36; 27:69; 29:20; 30:42), and Sūrat al-Qaṣaṣ makes the same point with the remains in view: *“How many societies We have destroyed that had been spoiled by their comfortable living! Those are their dwellings, hardly inhabited after them”* (28:58).

The traders of Makkah rode north and south every season, and the caravan routes of the peninsula ran through the territories of peoples the Qur'an names as examples: ʿĀd, Thamūd, the people of Lot, the people of the ditch. The sūrah's argument in these first verses is not that God is capable of punishing nations; it is that He already has, in the neighbourhood, and that the ruins are readable to anyone who stops. Verse 96 will state the law that explains the ruins: *“Had the people of those societies been faithful and mindful of Allah, We would have overwhelmed them with blessings from heaven and earth”* (7:96). The verse of the cry, then, is not a piece of ancient history. It is an interpretation of the landscape the sūrah's first listeners crossed on their way to market.


---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:6

> **Then We shall surely question those unto whom Our message was sent, and We shall surely question the messengers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Interrogations, One Order**

The previous verse left the nations with a confession; this one opens the court in which the confession is taken. The emphatic form is deliberate: *la-nasʾalanna* is an oath-future reinforced by *nūn al-tawkīd*, the strongest way Arabic has of saying *this will certainly happen*. The parties are named in a fixed order — first *those unto whom the message was sent*, then *the messengers* themselves.

The order is the argument. The community stands first because the question at issue is what it did with what reached it; the messengers stand second because their testimony closes the door on every plea of ignorance. No one will be able to say that the warning never came, because the same court will call the carrier who brought it.

**What the Messengers Are Asked**

The Qur'an describes the messengers' interrogation, and it is narrow: *“the Day Allah will gather the messengers and say, ‘What response did you receive?’”* (5:109). The question is not whether they were eloquent or successful — success was never theirs to engineer — but whether the message was delivered. The Book states their commission in the same short form again and again: *“your duty is only to deliver the message”* (13:40); *“then your duty is only to deliver the message clearly”* (16:82). A messenger's account is settled by the delivery, and the delivery is what leaves the community without an excuse.

**Why the People Are Questioned at All**

The classical commentators took seriously a difficulty that other verses create: some passages seem to say the guilty will not be questioned. *“On that Day there will be no need for any human or jinn to be asked about their sins”* (55:39). A witness will be brought from every faith-community (4:41; 16:84), the record will be laid open (39:69). How can there be interrogation if there is nothing to ask?

The answer the exegetes gave is the key to this verse: the questioning is not an investigation. God does not ask in order to learn; the account is already complete, and the very next verse says so — *“Then We shall recount unto them with knowledge.”* The questioning of 7:6 is the arraignment: the moment the evidence is put to the person and the person is made to stand inside it. That is why 16:84 ends with the disbelievers allowed neither to plead nor to appeal — the interrogation exposes the excuse; it does not discover the fact.

**The Interrogation That Has No Interpreter**

The Prophet ﷺ described the setting of that encounter with the precision of an eyewitness: *“There is none among you except that Allah will speak to him, with no interpreter between Him and him. He will look to his right and see nothing except what he has sent forth, and to his left nothing except what he has sent forth, and ahead of him nothing but the Fire — so protect yourselves from the Fire even with half a date.”* (al-Bukhārī 1413; Muslim 1016).

The detail that carries this verse's weight is the absence of intermediaries. Human power is screened: secretaries, advocates, patrons, spokesmen stand between the ruler and the ruled. The court of 7:6 has none. The question is put by the Lord directly to the servant, with the witnesses and the messengers present — and every layered defence a person spent a lifetime building (*my father did so*, *my people did so*, *no one told me*) has already been answered by the earlier session of the same court, in which the carriers were asked and the delivery was confirmed.

In two clauses, then, the verse gives the whole architecture of accountability: a message that arrived, carriers who delivered it, and a meeting in which both sides answer — the carriers for the delivery, the receivers for the response, and no one else to blame.

**The Question That Will Be Put to the Messengers**

The interrogation of verse 6 runs in two directions at once, and the Qur'an returns to it at several points in the Book. *“Consider the Day Allah will gather the messengers and say, 'What response did you receive?'”* (5:109). *“And watch for the Day He will call to them, asking, 'What response did you give to the messengers?'”* (28:65). Sūrat al-Naḥl adds that a witness will be produced from every faith-community (16:84), and the Prophet himself is told that he is one of them: *“How will it be, then, when We bring a witness from every community and bring you as a witness against these people?”* (4:41).

The doctrinal consequence is not a technicality about procedure. A message delivered by a messenger creates a record on both sides of the transmission: what the messenger actually said, and what the recipients actually did with it. The second half of the questioning — the messengers themselves — protects the integrity of the whole system, because it establishes that the account being examined is what was in fact delivered, not what the recipients later claimed to have heard.

Any reader who has ever complained that a preacher failed to explain something will find the verse uncomfortable in a useful way. The Book constructs a courtroom in which the accused cannot plead that the message never arrived, and in which the one sent can be asked what he passed on. Both halves of the evidence are on the record, and the record is held by a clerk who was never out of the room.


---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:7

> **Then We shall recount unto them with knowledge, for We were never absent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Recounting That Matches the Fact**

From the question the verse moves to the narration: *fa-la-naquṣṣanna ʿalayhim bi-ʿilm*. The verb is *qaṣṣa*, to follow a thing step by step — the root that also gives *qiṣṣah*, a story, because a story is a tracing of events in their order. To recount in the Arabic of the Qur'an is not to summarize; it is to reproduce the sequence so faithfully that the hearer recognizes his own life inside it. The instrument is named: *with knowledge* — not evidence reconstructed after the fact, not inference from testimony, but the knowledge that was present when the act itself occurred.

The final clause states the ground of that completeness: *wa-mā kunnā ghāʾibīn* — We were never absent. *Ghāʾib* is the one who is elsewhere; the negation removes not merely a lapse but the whole category of distance. The Qur'an expresses the same fact elsewhere in images the imagination can hold: *“We are closer to them than their jugular vein”* (50:16); *“Not even a leaf falls without His knowledge, nor a grain in the darkness of the earth, nor anything fresh or dry which is not in a clear record”* (6:59). The Book does not say that God was informed afterwards. It says that the record was never left unwritten.

**The Reading of the Record**

The sequence of this sūrah — question, recounting, weighing — matches the courtroom imagery the Qur'an uses for the final accounting. The record is brought out and opened: *“And the record will be laid open, and you will see the wicked in fear of what is written in it”* (18:49). Each person is handed his own book: *“We have bound every human's destiny to their neck, and on the Day of Judgment We will bring forth to each person a record which they will find laid open”* (17:13) — and is told: *“Read your record. You alone are sufficient this Day to take account of yourself”* (17:14). Before the prophets and witnesses are brought forward, the earth shines with the light of its Lord and judgment is passed (39:69).

The purpose of that architecture is that a person is not merely judged; he is made to read. The recounting of 7:7 is that reading, and *with knowledge* is why it cannot be contested. No one can plead that the file was mistaken or that a context was missing or that the intention was better than the deed — because the One who recounts was present at the intention as well.

**The Hours No One Saw**

The Prophet ﷺ gave this certainty a name and a set of questions. The feet of the servant will not move on the Day of Judgment, he said, until he is asked about his life and how he spent it, his youth and how he wore it out, his wealth and how he earned it and where he spent it, and his knowledge and what he did with it (al-Tirmidhī 2417, from Abū Barzah; graded ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ; a fourfold version is reported from Muʿādh in al-Dārimī 554). Not one of those four can be answered by reputation. All of them belong to the interior — to hours that no one but God observed.

That is the practical force of *We were never absent*. Human accountability is limited to what can be seen; the divine recounting is not — and this is a mercy before it is a dread. A person who did good that nobody ever learned of has lost nothing at all, and a person who kept an evil behind a locked door has hidden it from everyone except the One who is now doing the recounting. The verse makes secret-keeping as impossible as it already was, and makes integrity a matter of fact rather than of performance: the only kind that survives the reading of the record.

**The Account Written by Observers Who Never Left**

The clause *with sure knowledge* — *ʿalā ʿilm* — rests on a doctrine the sūrah assumes but states elsewhere: the deed was never unobserved. *“He reigns supreme over all of His creation, and sends recording-angels, watching over you”* (6:61). *“Not a word does a person utter without having a vigilant observer ready to write it down”* (50:18). *“While you are certainly observed by vigilant, honourable scribes, who know whatever you do”* (82:10–12). Sūrat al-Zukhruf adds that not even an unspoken thought escapes the record: *“Or do they think that We do not hear their evil thoughts and secret talks? Yes We do! And Our messenger-angels are with them, writing”* (43:80).

The Book's picture of the accounting on the Day incorporates the book itself: the record is laid open before its owner, who reads it without disputing a line — *“and they will say, 'O woe to us! What sort of book is this that leaves out nothing, small or great, without accounting for it?'”* (18:49). The sūrah's *full account* of verse 7 is therefore not a reconstruction made after the fact from fragmentary evidence. It is the presentation of a document.

There is a practical corollary the early Muslims drew from this and repeated in their instruction of children: a person's private life is the part of the record he does not control. Public reputation, in a Book that keeps scribes on both shoulders, is not the file that will be read out.


---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:8

> **And the weighing on that Day is true. So those whose balance is heavy, it is they who shall prosper.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Day That Weighs**

This verse sets the third action of the sūrah's courtroom: after the question (v. 6) and the recounting (v. 7), the weighing. The syntax puts the verdict first: *wa-l-waznu yawmaʾidhin al-ḥaqq* — and the weighing on that Day is the truth. *Ḥaqq* does double duty here. It means the scales are real, not a metaphor that dissolves on inspection; and it means they are true — exact, uncorrupted, incapable of registering a false weight.

The choice of the scale as the instrument of judgment answers the audience of this Makkan sūrah. Al-Aʿrāf speaks to a mercantile city whose life ran on balances: measured grain, weighed silver, a handshake over a pan. The Qur'an takes the instrument of the market and moves it to the last Day, where it is operated by the One who cannot be bribed. The point is sharpened in another sūrah, where woe is pronounced on traders who demand full measure when they buy and short it when they sell (83:1–3). The market's scale is precisely where men learn to weigh for advantage — which is why it is a scale that is set up on the Day of Judgment, and why the verse insists that the measuring there is *ḥaqq*.

**The Scale That Weighs What Scales Cannot**

The Qur'an describes the operation of this balance with mechanical precision: *“We will set up the scales of justice on the Day of Judgment, so no soul will be wronged in the least. And even if a deed is the weight of a mustard seed, We will bring it forth”* (21:47). It will register an atom's weight (4:40; 99:7–8), and its verdicts are given in the very phrasing used here: *“As for those whose scale is heavy, it is they who will be successful. But those whose scale is light, they will have doomed themselves”* (23:102–103); and in Sūrat al-Qāriʿah, *“So as for those whose scale is heavy, they will be in a life of bliss”* (101:6–7).

What, then, is weighed? The Prophet ﷺ answered with an image no arithmetic can hold. A man is brought before God with ninety-nine scrolls of sins, each stretching as far as the eye can see, and a single card bearing the witness *there is no god but God*. The scrolls are placed on one pan and the card on the other: the scrolls rise, the card descends — *“nothing is heavier than the Name of God”* (al-Tirmidhī 2639, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr; graded ḥasan; Ibn Mājah records it as well).

That image is this verse's commentary. The scales of the hereafter do not weigh quantity, so that whoever piles up the most acts wins; they weigh reality — the actual worth of what a life was aimed at. A mountain of activity performed for display can rise like chaff, while one sincere confession of the One God can descend with the weight of a lifetime. This is why the Day can be called *the truth*: it is the moment when things are measured as they are, and not as they were advertised.

**The Prosperity of the Heavy**

The verse closes with the outcome for those whose pan descends: *fa-ulāʾika humu al-mufliḥūn*. *Mufliḥūn* comes from *f-l-ḥ*, the root of the farmer — *fallāḥ* — who splits the earth, plants, and waits. *Filāḥ*, prosperity, is the same word as cultivation, because in the Qur'an's vocabulary success is not what a man extracts from the world but what grows out of what he has planted in it. The Arabic quietly answers the mercantile image: the real trade is not the one that closes when the caravan departs, but the one whose harvest is delivered on the Day the scales are set.

Heaviness, in that grammar, is not a burden. It is the weight of the things that survive — a confession guarded in secret, a charity no one recorded, an injustice refused at the moment when it would have paid. All of it descends in the pan, in the sight of no one except the One who was never absent.

**What Is Placed on the Scale**

The scale of verse 8 is the Book's standard instrument for the reckoning and it appears in three other sūrahs with the same terminology: *“As for those whose scale is heavy with good deeds, it is they who will be successful. But those whose scale is light will have doomed themselves, staying in Hell forever”* (23:102–103); *“So as for those whose scale is heavy with good deeds, they will be in a life of bliss; and as for those whose scale is light, their home will be the abyss”* (101:6–9).

The Book states elsewhere the arithmetic that gives the weighing its relief: *“Whoever comes with a good deed will be rewarded tenfold, but whoever comes with a bad deed will be punished for only one”* (6:160), and the atom of good and evil each appears on the record of the one who did it (99:7–8). But the sūrah's wording is not that deeds are counted; it is that they are *weighed*. A thing weighed has a quality as well as a quantity — sincerity, intention, the occasion, the cost — and the tradition drew precisely that conclusion. The Prophet ﷺ said of two short phrases that *they are light on the tongue, heavy in the balance, and dear to the Most Merciful: Subḥān Allāh wa biḥamdih, Subḥān Allāh al-ʿAẓīm* (al-Bukhārī 6406, from Abū Hurayrah).

For the reader of the sūrah, the scale's existence changes the size of small things. The history of the sūrah's audience is full of gestures that looked trivial at the time — a cup of water given, a harsh word withheld, a rumour passed on — and the verse tells him that each of them has been placed on an instrument that will be read out with exactness, on a Day whose justice the sūrah has already called *ḥaqq*.


---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:9

> **And as for those whose balance is light, it is they who have ruined their souls by having treated Our signs wrongfully.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Lightness as a Verdict**

The verse completes the pair begun in 7:8 with an exact mirror: the heavy scale and the light. What makes a scale light is not always the absence of deeds — the scrolls and the card have already shown that volume counts for nothing — but the absence of weight. The measure of the hereafter is not the number of acts but their worth, and the worth of an act is set by the aim to which it was offered.

The fate is then stated in commercial Arabic: *alladhīna khasirū anfusahum* — those who have lost themselves. *Khusrān* is the merchant's word for a loss, and the Qur'anic usage is exact: this is not the loss of profit but the loss of capital. The person in the verse leaves the market of his life having traded away the very self he was trading with. The same ledger appears in another sūrah: those who reject the meeting with their Lord have *“rendered their deeds void, so We will not give their deeds any weight on Judgment Day”* (18:105). A deed with no weight to give is a loss no accounting can repair.

**The Cause: Wronging the Signs**

The verse then names the cause, and it is not the one a reader might expect. Lightness is not diagnosed here as weakness or as ignorance. It is *an ẓalamū bi-āyātinā ẓulm* — that they wronged Our signs. The noun is the same one the townspeople used of themselves in 7:5, and the echo ties the sūrah's opening to its weighing: the destruction of the first verses and the light scale here are one offense at two moments of the story. Wronging a sign (*ẓulm*) is, in the Qur'an's usage, putting it out of its place — denying it outright, or acknowledging the words while treating them as though they carried no claim, or bending their meaning toward a preferred outcome. The imperfect *yaẓlimūn* adds what "having treated wrongfully" can only hint at: this was not a single lapse but a practice.

The seriousness follows from what a sign is. The signs — the revelation, the messengers, the world itself with its dawns and its marks of design — do not merely inform; they oblige. To meet one and shrug is not neutrality; it is a decision about the One who sent it. That is why the wronging of signs is counted among the gravest acts, and why the lightness of the scale is its natural consequence: a person who spends a lifetime refusing weight arrives at the judgment with none.

**The Exactness That Spares No One**

The verses around this one guard the verdict from despair. *“We will set up the scales of justice on the Day of Judgment, so no soul will be wronged in the least”* (21:47). *“Indeed, Allah never wrongs anyone — even by an atom's weight”* (4:40). *“So whoever does an atom's weight of good will see it, and whoever does an atom's weight of evil will see it”* (99:7–8). The lightness of this verse is therefore no accident of arithmetic, and no one will be condemned for a gram that was never his to supply: the same Book that weighs a mustard seed also declares that God does not require of any soul more than it can bear (2:286).

What follows in the sūrah — the settlement of humanity on the earth, its brief tenure, and the return (7:10 onward) — reads as the practical conclusion of this weighing. If the scale is real, then the days in which a pan may still be loaded with something of weight are exactly the days being lived now. Verse 9 closes the ledger with a warning whose force lies in its tense: those who *were* wronging Our signs made their own lightness, one deliberate and unremarkable moment at a time.

**"They Have Doomed Themselves"**

*Khasirū anfusahum* — they have lost themselves. The phrase is one of the Qur'an's most concentrated statements of what sin does, and the Book repeats it with a variety of emphasis. *“And by the soul and the One Who fashioned it... doomed is the one who corrupts it”* (91:7–10). *“The true losers are those who will lose themselves and their families on the Day of Resurrection”* (39:15), a sentence Sūrat al-Shūrā echoes for the wrongdoers exposed to the Fire (42:45). Sūrat al-Baqarah uses the same vocabulary for the small-scale version of the crime in this world: the hypocrites think they are deceiving God and the believers, *“yet they only deceive themselves, but they fail to perceive it”* (2:9).

The word *khasira* is drawn from trade: it is what happens to a merchant's capital. The sūrah's audience knew the sensation exactly — loss of a caravan, of a year's profit, of a stake in a market — and the Book uses their own vocabulary of catastrophe for something they did not price at all. The warning implicit in the choice is that the life of a human being is a capital sum placed in his hands at birth, and that what is done with it has the shape of an investment: it either returns or it does not.

The sūrah itself returns to this ending later, in the mouths of the condemned: *“They have surely lost their souls, and all that they fabricated has failed them”* (7:53). The two verses frame the sūrah's whole account of the last things with the same verdict, and the verb is the same in both. The difference is that the men of verse 9 could still read the sentence while it was in manuscript.


---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:10

> **And We have indeed established you upon the earth and placed means of livelihood for you therein. Little do you give thanks!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the Courtroom Back to the Earth**

The three verses before this one stood inside the final court: the question, the recounting, the weighing. Verse 10 returns the reader to the ground he is standing on. The transition is deliberate. Before the sūrah names the first refusal in the universe (vv. 11–18), it reminds the human being of the position he occupies: he has been *settled*, and he has been *provided for*. The two facts together form the premise of every command that follows, and the pivot of the verse is the last clause, which turns provision into a question about the recipient: *Little do you give thanks!*

**makkannākum: The Verb of Being Settled**

The verb is *makkanā*, from *m-k-n*, the root of place and of power. *Tamkīn* is not merely to drop someone somewhere; it is to give him a firm place, the ability to act from it, and the security to remain. The Qur'an uses the same root for the establishment of the believers in the land (22:41; 24:55) and for the power given to Joseph in Egypt (12:56). Here it is granted to humanity as such, without distinction of nation or creed.

The Qur'an describes the earth with the vocabulary of a house prepared for a guest: *“Who has made the earth a place of settlement for you and the sky a canopy”* (2:22); *“It is Allah Who made the earth a place of settlement for you”* (40:64); *“He is the One Who smoothed out the earth for you, so move about in its regions and eat from His provisions”* (67:15). The last of these is the closest twin to this verse: the earth is *smooth*, *tamed*, *made traversable* — a planet whose gravity holds instead of crushing, whose atmosphere filters instead of burning, whose soil yields food when a seed is placed in it. None of that was arranged by the person who benefits from it.

**maʿāyish: Livelihood in the Plural**

Provision is named with a plural that is significant: *maʿāyish*, from *ʿ-y-sh*, to live. Not one livelihood but many, distributed across soils and climates and temperaments. The commentators drew the practical consequences. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the plural shows the variety of ways God has opened for the creature to eat — agriculture, trade, craft, pasture — so that no one is forced into a single channel and no one may claim that the earth offered him nothing. Al-Qurṭubī adds the juridical consequence: since the livelihoods are *placed* by God, they are not owed to any person by right of cleverness; they are received, and receiving creates the duty of thanks.

The verse also quietly settles a question that occupies the sūrah elsewhere. If provision is placed by God, then striving is a means, not a guarantee; effort is required, but the outcome is not owned by the effort. That single adjustment disarms both envy and arrogance at once: envy, because another's plenty is not a theft from my share; arrogance, because my plenty is not a proof of my superiority.

**The Ingratitude Clause**

The verse closes with *qalīlan mā tashkurūn* — “little is it that you give thanks.” The construction (*qalīlan mā* + imperfect) is one of the Arabic forms of emphatic rebuke, and the translation's “Little do you give thanks!” carries it. The wording is careful: the verse does not say that human beings are never grateful. It says that gratitude is *little* — occasional, partial, easily displaced by complaint. The Qur'an returns to this diagnosis repeatedly: *“If you tried to count Allah's blessings, you would never be able to number them”* (14:34; 16:18), with 14:34 ending in the same verdict — *“Indeed, humankind is truly ungrateful.”*

The Qur'an also states the law that governs the relationship: *“If you are grateful, I will certainly give you more. But if you are ungrateful, surely My punishment is severe”* (14:7). The logic deserves to be stated plainly, because it is not a bargain in the manner of a merchant. Gratitude is not a payment that purchases increase; it is a *seeing* that makes increase bearable and fruitful. The person who acknowledges the Giver receives the same gift and is enlarged by it; the person who credits himself receives it and is narrowed by it, because everything he has becomes a monument to himself — and monuments have to be defended forever.

**Gratitude as a Discipline of the Eye**

Islam treats *shukr* as a practice, not a mood. The Prophet ﷺ trained his companions to compare downward rather than upward in the things of this world: *“Look at those below you and not those above you, for that is more likely to keep you from belittling the favour of God upon you.”* (Muslim 2963). It is a remedy against precisely the disease this verse names. Ingratitude rarely announces itself as denial of God; it appears as a habitual focus on the one thing missing, in a life that has received a thousand things.

The circle of gratitude is also horizontal before it is vertical. *“Whoever is not grateful to the people, he is not grateful to Allah.”* (al-Tirmidhī 1954, from Abū Hurayrah). A person who cannot bring himself to thank a teacher, a parent, a driver, a colleague has already lost the reflex by which the Creator is thanked, because both are acts of the same faculty: the willingness to admit that what I have came through someone other than me.

Read in the sūrah's sequence, then, verse 10 is not a pleasant interlude before the story of Iblīs. It is the foundation of that story. The earth is prepared, the livelihoods are placed, the human being is settled in dignity — and the next thing the sūrah shows is a creature who had been given a station and lost it by believing the station was his own achievement.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:11

> **Indeed, We created you, then We formed you, then We said unto the angels, “Prostrate yourselves before Adam.” And they all prostrated, save Iblīs; he was not among those who prostrated.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Why the Story of Adam Comes Here**

This verse begins the sūrah's longest narrative, the account of Adam, Iblīs, and the first disobedience — a story that will run to verse 25 and will be told again, from different angles, in al-Baqarah, al-Ḥijr, al-Isrāʾ, Ṣād, and Ṭā-Hā. Its placement here answers the verse before it. Verse 10 described a creature settled on a prepared earth and provided for; verse 11 shows what that creature *is* — the being for whom the angels were commanded to bow. The dignity is stated before the failure, so that the failure may be read correctly: not as evidence that humanity is contemptible, but as the collapse of something noble.

**Two Verbs: Created, Then Formed**

*Khalaqnākum thumma ṣawwarnākum* — We created you, then We formed you. The two verbs are not synonyms. *Khalaqa* names the bringing into being of what was not; *ṣawwara* names the giving of shape — proportion, features, the arrangement that makes a thing recognizable as itself. The Qur'an uses the second verb for the human form with evident pleasure: *“He shaped you in the womb, perfecting your form”* (40:64); *“He created the heavens and the earth for a purpose. He shaped you, perfecting your form”* (64:3); *“Who has perfected everything He created”* (32:7); and the summary statement of Sūrat al-Tīn, *“Indeed, We created humans in the best form”* (95:4).

The plural address — *you* — has been explained in two ways. Al-Ṭabarī prefers that it addresses humanity as a whole in Adam: you were created in him, and decreed in him, and then brought into existence from him. Others read the sequence as stages of the species: created as matter, formed as a body, then animated. Either reading carries the same theological point, which the next clauses make explicit: the human being did not design himself, and his form is not an accident of chemistry but the product of an intention.

**The Prostration and What It Confers**

Then God addressed the angels: *“Prostrate before Adam.”* The angels obeyed, and the Qur'an states their obedience without qualification. The command requires theological care, because prostration appears to be an act of worship and worship belongs to God alone. The classical commentators resolve this by distinguishing the *act* from its *object of worship*: the prostration was commanded by God, performed for God, and directed at Adam as a *qiblah* of honor — a salute, in the manner of a subject greeting a king. Al-Qurṭubī cites the analogy of Jacob and his sons prostrating before Joseph (12:100), which the Qur'an records without censure: the prostration of greeting was a known courtly form, and its permissibility or prohibition is set by the divine command. What the angels performed was obedience to God expressed in the direction He chose.

That the direction was a human being is the astonishing part. The angels are described elsewhere as creatures of uninterrupted worship — *“they do not disobey God in what He commands them, and they do exactly what they are commanded”* (66:6) — and here they are told to bow to dust that has been shaped and animated by God. The honor of humanity, in this scene, is not intrinsic but *conferred*, and it is conferred by the very act that Iblīs will refuse. Dignity, in the Qur'an's account, is always a grant; the moment it is treated as a personal possession, it becomes the occasion of a fall.

**Iblīs and the Grammar of the Exception**

*Illā Iblīs* — except Iblīs. The verse's final clause is unusual: it does not say that he refused, but that *he was not among those who prostrated*. The Qur'an distributes the facets of his act across sūrahs: he *refused and was arrogant* (2:34); he *was one of the jinn, but he rebelled* (18:50); he *protested* (17:61); he *said, “It is not for me to prostrate to a human You created from sounding clay”* (15:33). Each wording adds a detail. From 18:50 we learn that he was not an angel at all but a jinn, whose free will was the very test the command was designed to exercise. From 2:34 we learn that the refusal was accompanied by *istikbār* — the attribution of greatness to oneself. And from this verse we learn that his non-prostration was a settled state: he did not merely miss the moment; he placed himself outside the ranks of the obedient and remained there.

**The Warning Stored in the Honor**

The sūrah's first audience was a proud merchant leadership, and this narrative was addressed to them. The first rebellion in creation was not caused by disbelief in God's existence; Iblīs believed, and even invoked God's act of creation as his proof. It was caused by a ranking instinct — the conviction that a position undeserved by another belonged properly to oneself. The Prophet ﷺ stated the danger in a single hadith: *“No one who has an atom's weight of arrogance in his heart will enter Paradise.”* When a man asked how one could like his clothes and his shoes to be good, the Prophet ﷺ clarified the definition: *“Arrogance is rejecting the truth and belittling people.”* (Muslim 91, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd). Both halves of that definition are present in the verses that follow — and both were present in the court of the angels, where a creature with free will was shown what his freedom was for.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:12

> **He said, “What prevented thee from prostrating when I commanded thee?” He said, “I am better than he. Thou hast created me from fire, while Thou hast created him from clay.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Put to the Accused**

The scene turns to the interrogation, and its form repeats the pattern established in verses 6 and 7: God asks, and the answer exposes. The question is *mā manaʿaka* — literally, *what prevented you?* The verb *manaʿa* is the vocabulary of withholding and of barriers; a *māniʿ* is a thing that stands in the way. The question is therefore not a request for information. It puts before the accused the one thing he must now name: the obstacle. And since the command had come from God and nothing external had blocked the way, the only possible answer was the barrier he had built inside himself. The Qur'an's question strips away every third-party explanation before Iblīs speaks a word.

His answer does not name that barrier. It supplies, instead, an argument.

**The First Analogy in the Tradition's Telling**

*“I am better than him: You created me from fire and him from clay.”* The classical scholars read these words as the first act of reasoning by analogy (*qiyās*) in the history of the creation, and they examined its form with the care of logicians. Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī set out the objections in sequence.

First, the premise is asserted, not established. Iblīs claims a nobility of fire above clay as though the proposition were self-evident. It is not; it is a claim about material substances whose ranking Iblīs had no revelation to support. The Qur'an, for its part, treats the composition of both as dust-bound and God-given: the jinn are *“created from a smokeless flame of fire”* (55:15), and the human being from *“sounding clay moulded from black mud”* (15:26). Arranging these ingredients in a hierarchy of superiority is a human projection, not a scriptural datum.

Second, even a true premise could not have produced that conclusion. The value of a thing before God is not set by what it is made of, but by its obedience and its reverence: *“Indeed, the most noble of you in the sight of Allah is the most righteous of you”* (49:13). Iblīs reasoned from origin to rank, and the Qur'an's entire moral architecture refuses that move. A creature of fire and a creature of clay stand equally as creatures; only the command of God differentiates their duties.

Third, and decisively, the argument was raised against a direct command. Theologians of every school drew the line here: no demonstration — even a sound one — abrogates an explicit divine obligation. A person's reasoning is authorized to conclude that God is one, that the Messenger speaks for Him, that the prayer is due; it is not authorized to override what is thus concluded. To reason so is not to reason, but to use reasoning as an alibi.

**When Intelligence Goes to Work for Desire**

The most instructive feature of the answer is that it is *articulate*. Iblīs does not say “I did not want to.” He constructs a case. The Qur'an thus presents, at the first moral crisis of the created order, the psychology that will recur in every subsequent one: the mind's capacity to convert a preferred outcome into a reasoned position. Al-Rāzī notes that this is why the lesson of the verse is not confined to Iblīs. Any argument can be assembled backward from the conclusion one is unwilling to surrender; and the sharper the intelligence, the more respectable the assembly looks.

The criterion that separates sound reasoning from this corruption is stated by the angels in the parallel scene of al-Baqarah. When God announced the creation of a deputy on earth and the angels asked their question, the answer given for them — and given by them — was: *“Glory be to You! We have no knowledge except what You have taught us. You are truly the All-Knowing, All-Wise”* (2:32). The angels also reasoned — but they closed their reasoning with submission, whereas Iblīs closed his with a verdict. That is the whole difference, and the sūrah intends it to be remembered.

**The Lie in the Phrase “I Am Better”**

The comparative carries its own refutation. Iblīs accepted God's act of creation and even cited it as an argument; what he refused was God's authority to command. He was, in effect, a theist in metaphysics and an atheist in ethics, dividing lordship into departments so that the department that inconvenienced him could be reserved for himself. The Qur'an will answer the posture directly in another sūrah: *“Call upon Me, I will respond to you. Surely those who are too proud to worship Me will enter Hell, fully humbled”* (40:60).

And the final irony is the criterion he chose. *“I am better than he”* — better in substance, by a material comparison. The Book's answer is that the creature he despised would receive what he lost: *“We have indeed honoured the children of Adam”* (17:70). Rank in the divine court is not a property of matter; it is a matter of response. It is the one measurement Iblīs never applied, because applying it would have cost him the argument — and it is the same measurement this sūrah put before its readers four verses earlier, when it asked who among the settled, provided, and shaped would give thanks.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:13

> **He said, “Get down from it! It is not for thee to wax arrogant here. So go forth! Thou art surely among those who are humbled.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict and Its Exact Fit**

Iblīs had offered an argument; the verse answers with a sentence. *Fa-hbiṭ* — get down. The command is abrupt, and its brevity is part of its meaning: the case is closed, and no further exchange is permitted. What precedes it in the sūrah is now visible as a whole: a command was given, a creature measured himself against another, a rationale was produced, and the answer is a two-word eviction.

The proportionality is worth pausing over. The punishment is not an arbitrary penalty attached to a rule; it is the *reversal* of the thing he did. He placed himself above his station, so his station is lowered. He judged himself great, so he is told he is small — *min al-ṣāghirīn*, of the humbled, the reduced. The Qur'an states this correspondence elsewhere as a settled law: *“Is Hell not a fitting home for the arrogant?”* (39:60), and *“Surely those who are too proud to worship Me will enter Hell, fully humbled”* (40:60). The grammar of the hereafter is the grammar of a mirror.

**A Dwelling That Cannot Hold Arrogance**

The verse states the reason for the expulsion with precision: *lā yakūnu laka an tatakabbara fīhā* — it is not for you to be arrogant *in it*. The pronoun has been discussed by the commentators: the Garden, or the exalted assembly, or the presence in which the command was given. Whichever is intended, the clause carries a principle that runs through the whole Book. There are places and states that exclude self-exaltation not by legislation but by nature. Paradise is one of them: it is the house of those who submitted. The Prophet ﷺ described its inhabitants as *“the humble”* and said that its gates are entered by the one who acknowledges; in another report, the poor and the meek enter it before the wealthy and the proud.

This is why the verse says *it is not for you* rather than merely *you are not allowed*. The wording is ontological before it is legal: arrogance is not an offense that may be committed in that house and then repented of there; it is a condition incompatible with residence. The person who insists on being above others is not being denied a room; he is being told that he is asking for the wrong building.

**Two Expulsions: ihbiṭ and ukhruj**

The sūrah contains two removal commands, and the commentators distinguished them. The first is here — *ihbiṭ*, get down — and the second comes at verse 18: *ukhruj*, go forth, with the added description *madhʾūman madḥūran*, disgraced and banished. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr read the two as stages of a single sentence: the first removes him from the place in which the transgression occurred; the second confirms the state of ruin, the permanent expulsion from mercy. Others observe that the first descent precedes his request for respite (vv. 14–15) and his vow to mislead (vv. 16–17), and that the second expulsion follows those words — the sentence deepening with each further act of defiance. On either reading, the structure teaches something the reader of this sūrah needs: the fall was not a single slip followed by a settled punishment, but a path he continued to walk, one hardened statement at a time.

**ṣāghirīn: The Weight of Smallness**

The final word of the verse, *al-ṣāghirīn*, comes from *ṣ-gh-r*, the root of smallness and of willing submission. It is the direct antonym of *kibr* — the very thing that caused the fall. Arabic makes the reversal audible: the one who claimed *kabīr* (great) is made *ṣāghir* (small). The Qur'an does not use the language of size casually. In its idiom, greatness belongs to God alone — *al-Kabīr*, *al-Mutakabbir* — and any creature who appropriates the attribute contracts an absurdity that the moral order must eventually correct. The correction is rarely instantaneous in this life, which is precisely what makes this verse's placement instructive: the correction in the story is immediate, before the eyes of the angels, while in the reader's experience it is usually deferred, and the deferral is the space in which repentance is still possible.

That last point is the mercy hidden inside a verse of punishment. Iblīs' sentence was pronounced and stood; there was no door of return opened for him, because his refusal was not a lapse but a position. For human beings the Qur'an keeps the door open to the very end, and it does so explicitly: the same Adam who will slip in the verses that follow receives words from his Lord and is forgiven (2:37). The sūrah's first fall is therefore a warning and not a template. It teaches what arrogance costs and how it is answered, and it leaves the reader standing where Iblīs no longer stood — in the time when the correction can still be made by choice rather than by sentence.

**The Vocabulary of the Expulsion**

The two words that close the sentence — *madhʾūman madḥūran*, disgraced and rejected — are a fixed pair in the Qur'an, and the reader who notices where else the Book uses them will hear the sūrah's alarm more clearly. *Dh-ʾ-m* is the root of blame and disgrace; *d-ḥ-r* is the root of driving a thing away from oneself, of putting it out of reach. The pair describes a condition in which both the judgment and the separation are final.

The expulsion itself is narrated three times in the Book with the same elements, and each version adds something. Sūrat al-Ḥijr and Sūrat Ṣād record the command to leave and the curse that follows the creature *until the Day of Judgment* (15:34–35; 38:77–78). This sūrah's own account returns to the scene later and gives the sentence its final form: *“Get out of Paradise! You are disgraced and rejected! I will certainly fill up Hell with you and your followers all together”* (7:18). And Sūrat al-Isrāʾ applies the same two words to a human being for the first time in the Book's usage, in a verse that could be describing the sūrah's own audience: *“Whoever desires this fleeting world alone, We hasten in it whatever We please to whoever We will; then We destine them for Hell, where they will burn, condemned and rejected”* (17:18).

That last verse is the one that gives 7:13 its pastoral weight. What happened to Iblīs is not presented as a unique event in the biography of a jinn; it is the template of a verdict that human beings can reach by the same route. The sūrah has already shown its readers the mechanism — the refusal to bow, performed as a matter of dignity — and it will spend the next forty verses showing the human versions of the same posture, ending with the gates that do not open.
