The verse is consistent with the statement of the other sūrah (6:103): sight comprehends Him not, but He comprehends all sight. The desire of Moses to see God is engendered by the state of the intimacy that he experienced with God upon the mountain, where his Lord spoke unto him without intermediary, and, according to a legendary report, God was so close that Moses could hear the scratching of the pen across the tablets as they were being written upon by God. With this closeness and the sweetness of the speaking of God to him, Moses was overcome with the spiritual ecstasy, and yearned to be yet nearer to God, and was emboldened to ask the request of the seeing. The verb of the "show me" of the verse might also be translated "cause me to see," that is, "grant me the ability to see," so that Moses might look upon God and attain the vision he desires.

Some of the commentators argued that Moses, who surely knew that God transcended all the form and the corporeality and thus could not be seen physically, asked the request of the seeing only to satisfy the Israelites, who declare in the other sūrah (2:55) that they will not believe Moses till they see God openly. The other commentators suggest that Moses was not asking for the physical vision, but rather for such the complete spiritual knowledge of God that it would be as if he were able to see Him directly.

**The Mountain That Crumbled**

The verse states the reply of God to the request of Moses, and the reply is the reply that is the two statements: *Thou shalt not see Me; but look upon the mountain: if it remains firm in its place, then thou wilt see Me*. The reply of God to the request of the seeing is understood by some of the commentators to mean that God is not seen in this world, but may be in the next, sometimes invoking the other verse (75:23), which speaks of the righteous gazing upon their Lord in the Hereafter, and the Sufi writers speak of the ability to see God inwardly, with the eye of the heart. The other commentators argue that the response of the verse is stated in the emphatic form, indicating that God will not be seen, even in the next world. In the Biblical account, Moses is told that none can see the Face of God "and live" (Exodus 33:20), and some of the commentators mention this as well, or note that, had Moses not looked at the mountain when God manifested Himself, he would have died. According to one report, Moses responded that he would rather see God and die than live without seeing Him. The Sufi tradition connects this with the idea that the vision of God in this life is only possible after the "death of the ego," when one has completely "died" to the passions and the desires of the soul.

That the mountain crumbled after God said of it that it should remain firm in its place indicates that the seeing of God with the physical eye is as impossible as the mountain being able to withstand the self-manifestation of God, and the statement demonstrates the annihilating power of that vision, since even the mountain, so much larger and stronger than Moses himself, was incapable of bearing it. The word for "crumble to dust" might also be translated "level to the ground." Elsewhere in the Qur'an, the mountains are awed or moved by the power of God (33:72, where the mountains fear accepting the trust of God; 19:90–91, where the earth would be rent asunder and the mountains destroyed by the claim that God has a son). The annihilating power of the self-manifestation of God is similarly suggested in the saying attributed to the Prophet ﷺ, that the veil of God is light, and were He to remove it, the glory of His Face would burn up everything His Sight reached.

**The Swoon and the First of the Believers**

The verse states the act of Moses after the manifestation of God to the mountain, and the act is the act that is the swoon: *and Moses fell down in a swoon*. Moses fell down in a swoon out of the sheer awe, or as the result of being passed over by one of the angels. The word for "swoon" might also be translated "thunderstruck," from the same root as the thunderbolt that is said to have struck the Israelites for asking the similar question in the other sūrah (2:55). Some of the commentators indicate that Moses actually died in this moment and was brought back to the life, although the others argue that the word for "recovered" connotes the arousal from the state of the unconsciousness, rather than from the physical death. Upon recovering, Moses "turned in repentance," repenting of having asked to see God. The assertion of Moses that he is "the first of the believers" may mean that he was the first among the Israelites of his time to believe, or that he was the first to believe that God could not be seen physically, or that he was the foremost believer of his time. The Sufi tradition speaks of those who seek, and sometimes receive, the blessing of "seeing," or "witnessing" God, or receiving the inward vision of God. The commentary of al-Sulamī on this verse indicates that "nothing can withstand the witnessing of God save the hearts of the gnostics," which God has adorned with the spiritual knowledge of Himself and illuminated with His Light, and that this "witnessing" really describes the witnessing or the seeing of Himself by God, "for the Real is witnessed by none but Himself." The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the request of the seeing and the reply of God and the swoon of Moses, and the statement of the request of the seeing and the reply of God and the swoon of Moses are the statement of the request of the seeing and the reply of God and the swoon of Moses that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the choice of God in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:144

> **He said, “O Moses! Verily I have chosen thee above mankind through My messages and My speaking [unto thee]. So take that which I have given thee, and be among the thankful.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Choice Above Mankind**

According to some of the commentators, this verse recounts the consolation that was given to Moses after he was denied the vision of God in verse 143 of the sūrah, and it indicates the great blessing he had been given, for which he should be among the thankful: *O Moses! Verily I have chosen thee above mankind through My messages and My speaking [unto thee]*. That Moses was "chosen . . . above mankind" indicates his status among the people of his time, on the form of the teaching of the other verse (20:13), in which Moses is again called chosen, and the other verse (3:42), in which Mary, the mother of Jesus, is described as chosen above the women of the worlds. The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ also has the name al-Muṣṭafā, the "Chosen One," from the same root as the verb "I have chosen thee." The logic of the teaching of the verse is the logic that is worth stating: the prophet who is chosen above mankind is the prophet who is chosen by the grace of God rather than by any right of the prophet, and the prophet who is chosen by the grace of God is the prophet who is the object of the consolation of the verse, and the object of the consolation is the object that is in the state of the great blessing for which he should be among the thankful.

**The Messages and the Speaking of God**

Moses is distinguished by the two causes of the choice of the verse, and the two causes are the messages he received from God, meaning the Torah, and God's having spoken directly to him (4:164). In the other books of the Scripture, Moses is similarly distinguished by having spoken to God "face-to-face" (Exodus 33:11; Deuteronomy 5:4; 34:10). Because of this distinction, Moses is given the honorific title in the Islamic tradition of Kalīm Allāh, "he with whom God spoke." The distinction of the direct speaking of God to Moses is the distinction that is worth relating to the state of the Prophet ﷺ, for the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is also said to have spoken to God without intermediary at the lote tree of the boundary (53:14) during his Night Journey and Ascension, and the state of the Prophet ﷺ at the lote tree of the boundary is the state that is the object of the teaching of the other verse (53:14–15). The two distinctions of the verse are the two distinctions that are in the form of the teaching that the choice of the prophet is the choice that is by the message and by the speaking, and the choice by the message and by the speaking is the choice that is in the form of the state of the highest rank of the prophets.

**The Command of the Taking and the Gratitude**

The verse closes with the two commands of God to Moses, and the two commands are the two commands that are the taking of that which God has given, and the being among the thankful: *So take that which I have given thee, and be among the thankful*. The command of the taking of that which God has given is the command that is the receiving of the revelation, and the command of the being among the thankful is the command that is the gratitude for the choice of God. The logic of the two commands is the logic that is worth stating: the prophet who is the object of the two commands is the prophet who receives the revelation in the state of the gratitude, and the prophet who receives the revelation in the state of the gratitude is the prophet who is the example of the state of the one who receives the grace of God in the state of the gratitude for the grace of God, rather than the sorrow of the denial of the vision, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 143 of the sūrah. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the choice of God and the two commands, and the statement of the choice of God and the two commands are the statement of the choice of God and the two commands that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the revelation of the Torah in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:145

> **And We wrote for him upon the Tablets an exhortation concerning all things, and an elaboration of all things. “Take hold of them with strength, and command thy people to hold to the best of them. Soon I shall show thee the abode of the iniquitous.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Writing of the Torah Upon the Tablets**

The verse states the act of God after the choice of Moses, and the act is the act that is the writing of the Torah upon the tablets: *And We wrote for him upon the Tablets an exhortation concerning all things, and an elaboration of all things*. The writing of the Torah upon the tablets indicates that the Torah was originally the written revelation, and the writing of the Torah is on the form of the statement of the Biblical verse (Exodus 31:18), in which the tablets were "written with the Finger of God." The commentators generally consider there to have been the two tablets, as in the Biblical narrative, although the fact that the word for "tablets" is grammatically plural rather than dual has led to the speculation that there could have been more than two, some saying seven or ten. The tradition gives the details of the tablets, and the details are the details that are worth stating: various reports indicate that the tablets were made of the emerald, the ruby, or the wood, and that they were brought to Moses by the Archangel Gabriel, while the other reports say that they were made of the "stone" cut by Moses himself, which is similar to the Biblical account. The elaboration of all the things of the tablets means that they contained all that the people needed to know for the purposes of their religion, or that, like the Qur'an, they were revealed as the clarification of all things (16:89).

**The Command of the Strength and the Best**

The verse states the two commands of God to Moses, and the two commands are the two commands that are the taking hold of the tablets with the strength, and the command of the people to hold to the best of them: *Take hold of them with strength, and command thy people to hold to the best of them*. The command of the taking hold of the tablets with the strength is the command that is understood to mean the obeying in earnest of the commands and the prohibitions given in the tablets. Although Moses himself is instructed to take hold of the tablets with the strength, without qualification, the Israelites are told to hold to the best of them, which might also be translated "hold to the most beautiful of them." This may mean simply that the Israelites should perform the good and the virtuous actions commanded in the Torah, or that they should try to inculcate the virtues, such as the temperance, the modesty, and the patience, enjoined in it. In the other sūrah (39:55), the Prophet ﷺ is similarly instructed to enjoin his people to follow the most beautiful of that which has been sent down unto them from their Lord. The logic of the difference between the two commands is the logic that is worth stating: the prophet is commanded to take hold of the tablets with the strength without qualification, and the people are commanded to hold to the best of the tablets, and the difference between the two commands is the difference that is the teaching that the prophet is the one who is in the complete obedience, and the people are the ones who are in the state of the one who are the objects of the command of the holding to the best, on the form of the teaching of the wisdom of God in the state of the capacity of the people.

**The Abode of the Iniquitous**

The verse closes with the statement of God, and the statement is the statement that is the showing of the abode of the iniquitous: *Soon I shall show thee the abode of the iniquitous*. The "abode of the iniquitous" of the verse may refer to the place of the sinful in the Hereafter, to the land of Canaan, or to the land of Pharaoh. The three readings of the commentators are the three readings that are the teaching that the abode of the iniquitous is the abode that is the abode of the iniquity, and the abode of the iniquity is the abode that is the land of the iniquity or the place of the iniquity in the Hereafter. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the writing of the Torah and the two commands and the statement of the abode of the iniquitous, and the statement of the writing of the Torah and the two commands and the statement of the abode of the iniquitous are the statement of the writing of the Torah and the two commands and the statement of the abode of the iniquitous that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the turning away of the arrogant in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:146

> **I shall turn away from My signs those who wax arrogant upon the earth without right. Even if they were to see every sign, they would not believe in them. And if they were to see the way of sound judgment, they would not take it as a way, but if they were to see the way of error, they would take it as a way. That is because they denied Our signs and were heedless of them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turning Away of the Arrogant**

The turning away of God from His signs of the arrogant may mean that He will prevent them from understanding the revelation given to them, perhaps by "sealing their hearts" so that they do not understand, or by allowing them to be distracted by their worldly desires. The "signs" of the verse may indicate all of the signs and the proofs of God, including both those present in nature and those found in the human soul (41:53), in addition to the scriptural revelation, and the turning away of God from the signs of the arrogant thus means that He prevents them from contemplating them or benefitting from them, since some signs benefit only those whose hearts are already in a state of belief.

**The Arrogance Without Right**

The word for "wax arrogant" of the verse means, literally, to make oneself out to be grand and, by implication, to belittle the others. God is the Proud, on the same root, in the other sūrah (59:23), but all the others wax arrogant without right, since God alone has the right to assert His Greatness and His Superiority over all the beings. The word for "Proud" is the word that is positive when applied to God, but often negative when applied to the human beings or the other creatures. Alternately, the waxing arrogant without right might refer to asserting one's superiority over the others when it is not merited, on the form of the teaching of the other verses (7:12–13), in which the assertion of the superiority of Iblīs over Adam is dismissed as the "waxing arrogant." The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating: the man who makes himself out to be grand without right is the man who is in the one who belittles the others, and the man who belittles the others is the man who is in the one who is the object of the turning away of God from His signs. The theological discussion of the verse is the discussion that is worth stating: the Muslims who subscribed to the school of theology appealed to this verse to support their view that God might prevent certain people from believing, while the other school interpreted the turning away of God of the people from His signs as the punishment for their having freely chosen to wax arrogant and to "deny" the signs of God beforehand. The two readings of the verse are the two readings that are the teaching that the turning away of God from the signs of the arrogant is the turning away that is the consequence of the arrogance of the people, on the form of the teaching of the consequence of the denial in the commentary of verse 100 of the sūrah.

**The Reversal of the Way**

The verse states the three statements of the reversal, and the three statements are the three statements that are the seeing of every sign without the belief, and the seeing of the way of sound judgment without the taking, and the seeing of the way of error with the taking. The word for "the way of sound judgment" of the verse means the path of the guidance, the truth, and the correct knowledge and action, leading to the felicity in this world and in the Hereafter. The people who wax arrogant are the people who are in the state of the one who see every sign and do not believe in them, and the people who see every sign and do not believe in them are the people who are in the state of the one who see the way of sound judgment and do not take it as a way, and the people who see the way of sound judgment and do not take it as a way are the people who are in the state of the one who see the way of error and take it as a way. The three statements of the reversal are the three statements that are the teaching that the arrogance is the arrogance that is the reversal of the perception of the people, and the reversal of the perception of the people is the reversal that is the making of the truth the falsehood and the making of the falsehood the truth in the eyes of the people. The statement of the "that is because they denied Our signs and were heedless of them" of the verse is the statement that is the cause of the reversal, and the cause of the reversal is the cause that is the denial of the signs and the heedlessness of the signs, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 132 of the sūrah.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:147

> **As for those who deny Our signs and the meeting of the Hereafter, their deeds have come to naught. Are they recompensed for aught save that which they used to do?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Denials Named**

The verse identifies the people it concerns by two denials, and both are required. *As for those who deny Our signs and the meeting of the Hereafter.* The apparatus glosses the second: the meeting of the Hereafter refers to the Day of Resurrection and Judgment.

The pairing is not redundant. Denial of the signs is a claim about evidence — that what has been shown does not establish what it was shown to establish. Denial of the meeting is a claim about accountability — that there is no occasion on which the matter will be reviewed. The first can be held by someone who still expects to answer for himself; the second removes that expectation entirely.

The Qur'an treats the two together elsewhere and 18:105 is the closest parallel: *"They are those who disbelieve in the signs of their Lord, and in the meeting with Him. Thus their deeds have come to naught, and on the Day of Resurrection We shall assign them no weight."* The same two denials, the same consequence, and the addition of a phrase — no weight — that explains what coming to naught means in the arithmetic of that Day.

That the consequence follows both denials jointly is what makes the verse a statement about belief rather than about behaviour. It is not listing sins. It is naming the two convictions whose absence empties everything else of value.

**Their Deeds Have Come to Naught**

The consequence is stated in a single clause: *their deeds have come to naught*. The apparatus unpacks it in three parts, and the three are distinct rather than interchangeable. They will not benefit from their deeds. They will not receive reward for them. Nor will their deeds, however seemingly good, be able to shield them from punishment for their denial and disbelief.

The three answer three different hopes a person might place in a good deed — that it will advance him, that it will be credited to him, that it will protect him. The verse closes all three at once. What is left is a deed that happened and produced nothing.

Elsewhere it is said that disbelievers, idolaters, hypocrites, and those who persecute the prophets and the righteous will have their deeds come to naught, and the note collects the references: 2:217, 3:22, 5:5 and 5:53, 9:17, and 18:105. The list is deliberately mixed. It includes the outright disbeliever and the hypocrite who professes belief, the idolater and the persecutor of the righteous — four different positions, one outcome.

That mixture is the point of the recurrence. The nullification is not attached to a particular kind of wrong but to a particular kind of absence, and the absence is the one named in the first half of the verse. A hypocrite's deeds come to naught for the same reason an idolater's do.

**However Seemingly Good**

The apparatus's phrase *however seemingly good* is doing careful work and deserves to be held onto. The verse does not say that the deeds of these people were bad deeds. It says their deeds have come to naught, and the gloss insists the nullification applies even where the deed appeared good and even where it was good in its outward form.

This is the hardest implication of the verse and it is not softened anywhere in the note. A person may have fed the hungry, honoured a guest, freed a captive, kept a trust — and the verse says none of it will benefit him, be rewarded, or shield him. The deeds are not described as false; they are described as void.

The distinction between false and void matters. A false deed was never done. A void deed was done and counted for nothing, and the voidness attaches to the person rather than to the act. That is why the verse opens with who these people are — those who deny the signs and the meeting — before saying what happens to their deeds. The condition of the doer determines the value of the doing.

18:105 states the same conclusion in the language of weighing: *on the Day of Resurrection We shall assign them no weight*. The image is a scale, and the verse is not saying the deeds are light. It is saying they are not placed on it.

**Are They Recompensed for Aught Save What They Used to Do?**

The verse closes with a rhetorical question, and its function is to deny injustice rather than to threaten. *Are they recompensed for aught save that which they used to do?* The expected answer is no, and the no is exculpatory of God.

The formula is quoted elsewhere in the same words. At 10:52 it is put to the wrongdoers on the Day itself: *"Then it shall be said unto the wrongdoers: Taste the punishment everlasting. Are you recompensed for aught save that which you used to earn?"* At 27:90 the same question recurs. Its repetition across three sūrahs marks it as a settled formulation rather than a local turn of phrase.

The question does two things at once. It asserts the exactness of the recompense — nothing is added to what they did, nothing arbitrary is inflicted. And it asserts the identity between the deed and its outcome, so that the punishment is not something imposed on their history but their history arriving at its term.

Read against the first half of the verse the closing question completes an argument that could otherwise seem merely punitive. Their deeds have come to naught; they are recompensed only for what they used to do. The two clauses together say that the nullification is not a subtraction from what they earned but the shape of what they earned. Denial of the meeting with God is a deed like any other, and this verse reports its wage.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:148

> **And while he was away, the people of Moses took a calf [made] from their ornaments—a body that lowed. Did they not consider that it spoke not unto them, nor guided them to any way? They took it up, and they were wrongdoers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Making of the Calf**

The "while he was away" of the verse means that the people of Moses took the calf while Moses was still speaking with God on the mountain. The people fashioned from the precious metals, usually understood to be the gold, an idol in the form of the calf and made it the object of worship, saying, "This is our god and the god of Moses" (20:88). Although Aaron was left in charge, the commentators indicate that Aaron did not participate in making or worshipping the calf, which was directed by another Israelite referred to as al-Sāmirī, the "Samaritan," as indicated in the other sūrah (20:87, 95–97), and rather, Aaron tried to stop this action (20:90). The verse fulfills the foreshadowing of verse 138 of the sūrah, in which the people whom the Israelites came upon had the idols in the form of the calf, and the foreshadowing of the verse is the foreshadowing that is realized in the act of the taking of the calf of the verse.

**The Body That Lowed**

The verse describes the calf as the body that lowed, that is, the body that emitted the sound of the live calf, and the description of the body that lowed leads some of the commentators to think that the calf possessed the supernatural power, although the other sūrah (20:95–96) indicates that the lowing sound was the result of a kind of sorcery on the part of al-Sāmirī. The other commentators suggest that the calf was fashioned with the carefully placed holes and hollows, and when the wind blew through them, it generated the lowing sound. Despite the lowing sound, the calf spoke not unto the people, nor guided them to any way, and the statement of the non-speaking and the non-guiding of the calf is the statement that is the test of the idol: the idol that does not speak and does not guide is the idol that is the idol that is not the god. The Qur'anic narrative of this event offers the further reflection on the nature of the prophetic guidance and the miracles as contrasted with the sorcery, indicating that the unusual feats are not themselves the indications of the spiritual power or worthy of worship and are no substitute for the divine revelation. The true prophethood and its signs can be distinguished from the mere conjuring of a sorcerer, because the former bring the guidance and the clarity, while the latter, like the feats of the sorcerers in verse 116 of the sūrah, are ultimately powerless and illusory. The Sufi commentators understand the calf and the willingness of the Israelites to worship it, despite the tremendous favor of God toward them, to be indicative of a tendency in the human beings to allow the worldly phenomena to distract them from the worship of God, and the tendency is the reminder that none are free of this temptation toward the idolatry save those who have "slain their souls" by conquering its worldly passions. The people of the verse are the wrongdoers, in that they "wronged themselves" by taking the powerless idol as the object of worship.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:149

> **And when they wrung their hands and saw that they had gone astray, they said, “If our Lord does not have mercy upon us and forgive us, we shall surely be among the losers!”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Remorse Fell Into Their Hands**

The verse's opening phrase is an idiom rather than a description, and the commentators read it three ways. Literally it says that remorse *fell into their hands* — and al-Rāzī explains that the falling is an inward event: remorse settling into the heart, which the hand is made to stand for because the hand is where a person feels what he cannot otherwise express.

Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī both record a second reading, more physical. The remorseful bite their hands, as though attacking the site of the feeling. On this account the idiom is not a metaphor for interiority at all but a compressed report of a gesture — the same gesture the Qur'an describes elsewhere for the Day of Judgment, when the wrongdoer bites his hands.

The third reading turns the hand from an image of feeling into an instrument of repair. Remorse fell into their hands in the sense that the responsibility now lay in their own hands: it was for them to make amends and set matters right, and no one else could do it.

The three are not competing so much as cumulative. Remorse arrives as a feeling, expresses itself as a gesture, and — if it is genuine — becomes an acknowledgement that the fixing belongs to the one who broke it. The verse needs all three, because what follows is not a feeling but a sentence spoken.

**Seeing Before Speaking**

Between the remorse and the prayer the verse places an act of sight: *and they saw that they had gone astray*. The sequence is deliberate and it is the whole psychology of the verse. Remorse came first and was not sufficient; what produced the prayer was the seeing.

That distinction matters. The people had not stopped wanting the calf. They had seen what they were. Al-Rāzī's first reading — remorse settling into the heart — describes the arrival of the feeling, and this clause describes the moment the feeling became knowledge, which is the only point at which it can issue in speech.

The Qur'an marks the same order elsewhere when it describes the disbeliever on the Day of Judgment seeing his record and wishing he had not been given it. Sight precedes the wish as it precedes the prayer here. What a person says about himself after seeing is different in kind from what he says before.

**Contrition Without an Excuse**

What they say is short, and its shortness is the point. *If our Lord does not have mercy upon us and forgive us, we shall surely be among the losers.* There is no explanation, no mitigating circumstance, no reference to the samirī or to Moses's absence or to the calf's apparent power. The sentence contains a condition and a consequence and nothing between them.

The source note describes it as immediate and utter contrition, a casting of themselves upon the mercy of God rather than an offering of excuses, and identifies two exact parallels. The first is in this sūrah, at verse 23, where Adam and his spouse say:

> *"Our Lord! We have wronged ourselves. If Thou dost not forgive us and have Mercy upon us, we shall surely be among the losers."* (7:23)

The second is Noah's, at 11:47: *"My Lord! Truly I seek refuge in Thee from questioning Thee concerning that whereof I have no knowledge. If Thou dost not forgive me and have Mercy upon me, I shall surely be among the losers."*

Three speakers, three different failures, one formula — and the formula is identical down to its closing words. That is not coincidence but instruction. The Qur'an is showing what repentance sounds like when it is real, and it sounds the same whether it is spoken by the first human beings, by a prophet who asked a question he should not have asked, or by a people who built a calf. The structure is: we did this, we have no defence, everything now depends on you.

**Among the Losers**

The word translated *losers* is *khāsirūn*, from *kh-s-r*, to lose, to suffer diminution, to come out of a transaction worse than one went in. It is commercial language applied to a spiritual state, and this sūrah uses it repeatedly — at verse 90 of those who disbelieved and turned others from the path, at verse 92 of the people of Shuʿayb, whose own trading idiom is turned back on them, and at verse 99 of those who feel secure from God's plan.

The recurrence is not mere repetition. Each use names the same thing from a different direction: the disbeliever loses, the fraudulent trader loses, the secure lose. What makes verse 149's use different is who is speaking. In the earlier instances the word is God's description of them; here it is their description of themselves.

That is the last step the verse records, and it is the one that matters. A person who can say *we shall be among the losers* about himself, without qualification and without an excuse attached, has already taken the position that mercy requires of him. The calf-worshippers are not saved by their remorse; they are saved by having arrived, through it, at an accurate sentence about themselves.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:150

> **And when Moses returned unto his people angry and aggrieved, he said, “How evil is the course you have followed after me! Would you hasten the Command of your Lord?” And he cast down the Tablets and seized his brother by the head, dragging him toward himself. He said, “Son of my mother! Truly the people deemed me weak, and they were about to kill me. So let not the enemies rejoice in my misfortune, and place me not with the wrongdoing people.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Return of the Anger and the Grief**

Moses returned to his people already angry and aggrieved, even before seeing them, because God made him aware of their having gone astray while he was still on the mountain (20:83–85). The word for "aggrieved" of the verse may connote the sadness, but also the intense anger. The anger of Moses was at his people, but also perhaps at himself for having left them, while his grief was both for his people's grave error and for his having to leave the intimate discourse with God, since the narrative of this event in the other sūrah (20:83–85) suggests that Moses had to cut short his time on the mountain to address the matter.

**The Statement of the Evil Course**

The verse states the statement of Moses to his people, and the statement is the statement that is the two parts: *How evil is the course you have followed after me! Would you hasten the Command of your Lord?* The statement of the evil course may have been directed at the Israelites generally or at the leaders among them, including Aaron, and the phrase "after me" may mean while Moses was away or after Moses had set the example of the true monotheism. The statement of the "would you hasten the command of your Lord" may pertain to the command that the people wait for the return of Moses, and thus addresses their impatience concerning—and their ultimate hastening of—the return of Moses from his appointed meeting with God, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 142 of the sūrah, in which the forty-day stay of Moses upon the mountain was reportedly ten days longer than his people had expected. The statement may also refer to their hastening the judgment of God upon them through their actions, on the form of the repeated Quranic warning to the disbelievers (6:57–58; 10:50–51). More broadly, the question might also be meant to chastise them for their impatience regarding the fulfillment of the promise of God to them, on the form of the statement of the other sūrah (20:86).

**The Casting Down of the Tablets and the Plea of Aaron**

The verse states the acts of Moses after the statement, and the acts are the acts that are the two parts: the casting down of the tablets, and the seizing of the brother. Moses threw down the tablets in anger and shock upon seeing his people's idolatrous worship, breaking them, as in the Biblical account (Exodus 32:19). He then seized his brother, Aaron, in anger for his having failed in his charge of preserving the Israelites in righteousness, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 142 of the sūrah. Aaron pleaded with Moses, calling him "son of my mother," rather than just "my brother," in a bid to evoke the tenderness and the mercy of Moses toward him. Aaron excused his own actions by arguing that the people deemed him weak, meaning that they no longer recognized his authority, as suggested by their having followed instead the directives of al-Sāmirī, while ignoring the pleas of Aaron for them to desist, and that they were moreover prepared to slay him, on the form of the teaching of the other sūrah (20:94), in which Aaron argues that he feared he would be blamed for causing the discord among the Israelites, had he been more forceful with them. Aaron further asked Moses not to let their enemies rejoice in his misfortune, that is, to gloat over his further humiliation at the hands of Moses, and not to place him with the wrongdoing people, that is, not to consider him guilty of the idolatry and so punish him along with the others.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:151

> **He said, “My Lord, forgive me and my brother and bring us into Thy Mercy, for Thou art the most Merciful of the merciful.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prayer of the Forgiveness**

The verse states the prayer of Moses after the plea of Aaron, and the prayer is the prayer that is the request of the forgiveness: *My Lord, forgive me and my brother and bring us into Thy Mercy, for Thou art the most Merciful of the merciful*. The request of the forgiveness of Moses for himself and his brother is considered by the commentators to be an indication of their complete dedication to God and their desire to draw near to Him, rather than an acknowledgment of the sin or the wrongdoing on their part, since the prophets are believed to be immune from the sin according to the Islamic doctrine of the inerrancy of the prophets. The other commentators suggest that Moses asked God to forgive him for his anger and to forgive Aaron for not being forceful enough with the Israelites, neither of which may be understood as rising to the level of the "serious sin."

**The Holy Anger of the Prophet**

The anger of Moses is said by some of the commentators to have been not the personal anger, but the "holy anger" for the sake of God, motivated by his intense desire to preserve the worship for God alone, and the holy anger is the anger that was not the anger that reflected the lack of control of the passions, but was rather the anger manifested outwardly, in the form of the holy wrath for the sake of the religion, while the inner state of Moses remained characterized by the calm and the mercy. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating in full: the prophet who is in the state of the holy anger is the prophet who is in the one who manifests the anger outwardly the holy wrath, and the prophet who manifests the anger outwardly in the form of the holy wrath is the prophet who is in the one whose inner state remains characterized by the calm and the mercy. The two states of the prophet are the two states that are the teaching that the anger of the prophet is the anger that is the anger for the sake of the religion, and the anger for the sake of the religion is the anger that is the anger that is not the anger of the passion, and the anger that is not the anger of the passion is the anger that is the anger that is the state of the prophet. The logic of the prayer of the verse is the logic that is worth stating: the prophet who prays for the forgiveness of himself and his brother is the prophet who is in the one who is the object of the mercy of God, and the prophet who is the object of the mercy of God is the prophet who is in the one who brings his brother into the mercy of God, and the prophet who brings his brother into the mercy of God is the prophet who is in the one who is the example of the brotherhood of the prophets. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the prayer of the forgiveness, and the statement of the prayer of the forgiveness is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the punishment of the worshippers of the calf in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:152

> **As for those who took up the calf, anger from their Lord shall seize them, and abasement in the life of this world. Thus do We recompense those who fabricate.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Anger and the Abasement**

The verse states the punishment of the people who took up the calf, and the punishment is the punishment that is the two parts: *anger from their Lord shall seize them, and abasement in the life of this world*. That the anger from their Lord shall seize the Israelites who worshipped the calf is thought by some of the commentators to be a reference to the command that they atone for their sins by "slaying their own" in the other verse (2:54), which some of the commentators understand to mean that the Israelites were to slay those among their own people who were most guilty for the calf incident, and the other commentators understand as a form of the collective punishment. Some of the commentators understand both the anger of God and the abasement of the Israelites in the life of this world as the references to the later hardships that were to be suffered by the Israelites and their descendants, or suggest that the anger of God refers to His punishment of them in the Hereafter, while the abasement refers to their future adversity in this world. The other commentators argue that the repentance and the atonement of the Israelites meant that their punishment would be only in this world and not in the next. The two readings of the commentators are the two readings that are the teaching that the punishment of the people who worship the calf is the punishment that is the punishment in this world or the punishment in the Hereafter, and the punishment in this world or the punishment in the Hereafter is the punishment that is the recompense of the state of the people in the state of the worship of the calf.

**Those Who Fabricate**

The verse closes with the statement of the recompense, and the statement of the recompense is the statement that is the recompense of those who fabricate: *Thus do We recompense those who fabricate*. The "those who fabricate" of the verse refers to those who initiate the false religious beliefs or practices, on the form of the teaching of the other verses (4:48–50; 5:103; 6:93; 10:59–60; 16:56).

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:153

> **But as for those who commit evil deeds and then repent thereafter and believe, surely, thereafter, thy Lord is Forgiving, Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Mercy for the Repentant**

The verse states the reminder of the mercy of God for the people who commit the evil deeds and who repent thereafter and believe, and the reminder is the reminder that is the two names of God: *surely, thereafter, thy Lord is Forgiving, Merciful*. The reminder that God forgives those who are truly repentant and who mend their ways is found throughout the Qur'an (2:160; 3:89; 5:39; 16:119). In the other verse (4:17), the Qur'an states that God only accepts the repentance of those who do evil in ignorance and then turn quickly in repentance, and in verse 149 of the sūrah it is clear that the repentance of the Israelites is the quick and the unconditional repentance. The logic of the reminder is the logic that is worth stating in full: the verse of the mercy of the repentant is the verse that follows the verse of the punishment of the worshippers of the calf in verse 152 of the sūrah, and the order of the two verses is the order that is the teaching that the punishment of God is the punishment that is the punishment for the people who do not repent, and the mercy of God is the mercy that is the mercy for the people who repent. The people who commit the evil deeds and who repent thereafter and believe are the people who are in the state of the one who are the objects of the mercy of God, and the people who are the objects of the mercy of God are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the examples of the teaching that the mercy of God is the mercy that is the mercy that is the object of the repentance. The man who commits the evil deed and who repents is the man who is in the one who is the object of the next section of the sūrah, in which the verse states the taking up of the tablets. The logic of the balance of the two verses is the logic that is worth stating: the verse of the punishment and the verse of the mercy are the two verses that are the teaching that the state of the man is the state that is the punishment or the state that is the mercy, and the punishment and the mercy are the two states that are the two responses of the state of the man in the state of the repentance. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the mercy of God for the repentant, and the statement of the mercy of God for the repentant is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the taking up of the tablets in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:154

> **And when the anger abated from Moses, he took up the Tablets; and in their inscription lay a guidance and a mercy for those who are in awe of their Lord.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**When the Anger Abated**

The verse begins with a subsiding rather than an action, and the order matters. *And when the anger abated from Moses, he took up the Tablets.* The anger came first and it went away on its own terms; what Moses did followed the abatement and not the anger.

The anger itself is described at verse 150, where Moses returns to his people angry and aggrieved, casts down the Tablets and seizes his brother by the head. The commentators note there that his anger was at his people but perhaps also at himself for having left them, and that his grief was both for their grave error and for having to cut short the intimate discourse with God. That is a compound state, and this verse reports its end.

It is worth attending to what the verse does not say. It does not say Moses mastered his anger, or repented of it, or was rebuked for it. It says the anger abated — a verb of weather, describing something that passes. The Prophetic anger at idolatry is not treated here as a fault requiring correction but as a condition with a duration.

What the verse does establish is that nothing is done inside that duration. The Tablets lie where they were cast. Moses waits, or rather the narrative waits, until the state has passed; and the first act recorded after it is an act of retrieval.

**He Took Up the Tablets**

The retrieval is stated in a few words and the verse draws no attention to it, which is itself significant. Moses had cast the Tablets down in front of his people, in anger, at the moment of their worst failure. The gesture was public and it was destructive in appearance. Nothing in the intervening verses reports what became of them.

And now he takes them up. The verse does not say they were broken, does not say they were intact, does not say he gathered fragments. It says he took up the Tablets, using the same object as before, and then immediately speaks of what is in them — *in their inscription lay a guidance and a mercy* — which requires that the inscription still be legible.

The silence is best read as deliberate. The narrative has no interest in the physical fate of the Tablets because its interest is in what they contain, and the content is stated in the same sentence as the retrieval. Whatever happened when they fell, what is picked up is a text.

There is also a correction implied in the gesture, though the verse does not name it. The Tablets were cast down at the people. They are taken up for the people — for *those who are in awe of their Lord*, as the second half of the verse specifies. The same object moves from being an expression of anger to being an instrument of mercy, and the movement is marked only by the verb.

**In Their Inscription**

The word translated *inscription* is *nuskhah*, and the apparatus treats it at length because it is not a simple term. It generally denotes a written copy of something, but its root meaning relates to substituting one thing for another, particularly as regards a text. Both senses are live here and the commentators pursue both.

Al-Qushayrī and al-Rāzī record the first consequence of the root meaning. On their reading *nuskhah* refers to new Tablets given to Moses, which contained a copy of what had been inscribed on the originals. The substitution is then literal: the first Tablets were replaced by second ones, and the second carry the same text.

Al-Qushayrī records a second and broader reading, in which *nuskhah* refers to the notion that guidance and mercy were inscribed upon — or copied onto — the Torah Tablets from the heavenly Preserved Tablet, from which all Divine Revelation derives. See 85:22, where the Qur'an itself is described as *upon a Preserved Tablet*.

On this reading the substitution runs the other way. The Tablets in Moses's hands are the copy, and the original is in heaven. Every revelation is a *nuskhah* in that sense, and the word does the same work here that it does in the technical vocabulary of the commentators when they speak of one text superseding another.

The two readings are not mutually exclusive and the verse does not require a choice between them. What both preserve is that the Tablets are not self-originating. Their authority is derived — from an earlier set, on the first reading, or from a heavenly original, on the second.

**A Guidance and a Mercy**

What the inscription contains is named in two words, and the pairing is a formula rather than a description. *A guidance and a mercy* — the same two nouns, in the same order, are used of the Torah and of the Qur'an alike, and the apparatus collects the instances: 6:154, 7:52, 16:64, 28:43, and 31:2–3.

Two of these are close enough to quote. 6:154 speaks of the Book given to Moses: *"Then We gave unto Moses the Book, complete for those who would be virtuous, as an exposition of all things, and as a guidance and a mercy."* And 7:52, earlier in this same sūrah, speaks of the Qur'an: *"We have indeed brought them a Book, which We have expounded with knowledge, as a guidance and a mercy for a people who believe."*

The agreement of the two descriptions is the exegetical point. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī take the phrase simply as indicating that the contents of the Tablets — the Torah — contained guidance and mercy, and the fact that the Qur'an describes itself in the identical terms means the verse is making a claim about revelation generally, not about one book.

The two nouns are not synonyms and their order is not arbitrary. Guidance directs; mercy receives. A text that guided without mercy would be a demand, and a text that showed mercy without guidance would be a comfort with no direction in it. The pairing describes what a revelation must be to be of use.

The verse then restricts the benefit. Guidance and mercy lie in the inscription *for those who are in awe of their Lord* — the same qualification 7:52 makes in the words *for a people who believe*. The restriction is not a withholding. It is a statement about reception: the Tablets contain what they contain for everyone, and it reaches only the one who is in a condition to receive it.

That condition is named here as awe, and it connects this verse to the one that follows in the narrative and to the sūrah's larger argument. Verse 157 will describe those who follow the unlettered Prophet they find written in the Torah and the Gospel. The Tablets are not a closed document; they are a guidance and a mercy that point onward, and the awe required to read them is the same awe required to follow what they point to.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:155

> **And Moses chose seventy men from his people for Our meeting. And when the earthquake seized them, he said, “My Lord! Hadst Thou willed, Thou wouldst have destroyed them and me beforehand. Wilt Thou destroy us for that which the fools among us have done? It is naught but Thy trial, whereby Thou leadest astray whomsoever Thou wilt, and guidest whomsoever Thou wilt. Thou art our Protector, so forgive us and have mercy upon us, and Thou art the best of forgivers!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Choice of the Seventy Men**

Most of the commentators indicate that this meeting with the elders followed upon the calf incident and was connected with the attempt of the Israelites to atone for their sin. The seventy men chosen by Moses, at the command of God, were said to be among those elders who had not directly participated in making or worshipping the calf. Some of the reports say that Moses chose six men from each of the twelve tribes for a total of seventy-two men, and two of those, Joshua and Caleb, were chosen to remain behind and supervise the Israelites in the camp. The other seventy men were instructed to fast and purify themselves and their clothing in preparation for the meeting. When they approached the mountain, the earthquake seized them, and the word for "earthquake" is the same word that was used to denote the physical event that destroyed the Thamūd in verse 78 of the sūrah and the people of Midian in verse 91 of the sūrah. The earthquake was meant as the punishment for the seventy men, who had not worshipped the calf, but who nonetheless had not done enough to stop those who did. Some of the commentators indicate that the elders were, in fact, killed by the quake, but then revived at the plea of Moses, while the other commentators say that the quake shook them violently, but relented before they died.

**The Common Narrative of the Cloud**

A common narrative associated with this verse connects it with the incident in which the Israelites ask to see God openly (2:55). According to this account, as Moses and the seventy elders approached the mountain, a cloud descended and enveloped the mountain, and Moses entered the cloud alone, but the elders followed. As they entered, they were cast down prostrate, but could hear Moses speaking with God. Eventually the cloud lifted, and they approached Moses and asked to see God directly, at which the earthquake seized them for their insolence.

**The Statement of Moses and the Trial**

The verse states the statement of Moses when the earthquake seized the seventy men, and the statement is the statement that is the four parts: the statement of the will of God, the question of the destruction for the fools, the statement of the trial, and the request of the forgiveness. The statement of the "Hadst Thou willed, Thou wouldst have destroyed them and me beforehand" of the verse is said to indicate that Moses would have preferred that, if God intended to kill them, He would have done so while they were still in the camp, for he feared that if God killed the elders on the mountain, the Israelites would blame Moses for their deaths. The question of the "Wilt Thou destroy us for that which the fools among us have done" of the verse refers to the fools who are those Israelites who directly participated in the calf worship. According to al-Rāzī, Moses knew that God would not punish some for the sins of the others, and his question was a way of indicating that he was, in fact, certain that God would not do this, as if to say, "I know Thou wouldst not destroy us for the wrongdoing of others." The statement of the "It is naught but Thy trial" of the verse is the statement that is the teaching that the trial of God is the trial that is the trial that leads astray whomsoever God wills, and guides whomsoever God wills, on the form of the teaching of the dichotomous effect of the divine revelation on the different souls (2:26; 9:124–25). The theological discussion of the verse is the discussion that is worth stating: that God may both guide and lead astray is found throughout the Qur'an (6:39; 7:178; 13:27; 14:4; 16:93; 17:97; 18:17; 35:8), and some of the Muslim theologians argue that such statements indicate that all the faith or the disbelief is determined by God, while the other theologians argue that God leads the people astray only as the punishment for the previous wrongdoing or iniquity, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 146 of the sūrah. The request of the forgiveness of Moses is the request that is the statement of the protection of God and the mercy of God and the best of the forgivers, and the request of the forgiveness is the request that is the continuation of the supplication in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:156

> **And prescribe good for us in the life of this world, and in the Hereafter; truly we have turned unto Thee.” He said, “I cause My Punishment to smite whomsoever I will, though My Mercy encompasses all things. I shall prescribe it for those who are reverent, and give alms, and those who believe in Our signs,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Supplication for the Good of the Two Worlds**

The verse continues the supplication of Moses, and the continuation is the continuation that is the request of the good: *And prescribe good for us in the life of this world, and in the Hereafter; truly we have turned unto Thee*. The supplication of Moses beseeching God for the good in the life of this world and in the Hereafter is an example of the proper mode of supplication for the believers (2:201). The "good in the life of this world" of the verse may mean the physical goods, such as the health and the physical well-being, but may also mean the moral integrity and the success in obeying the commandments of God. The "good in the Hereafter" of the verse means the forgiveness for one's sins and the reward in Paradise for one's good deeds. The logic of the supplication is the logic that is worth stating: the one who prays for the good only in this life is the one who is in the sign of the worldliness, and the one who prays for the good only in the Hereafter is the one who is in the sign of the failure to recognize one's dependence on God in this life and His ability or willingness to intervene in the worldly matters. The one who prays for the good in the two worlds is the one who is in the one who is the example of the proper mode of the supplication. The statement of the "truly we have turned unto Thee" of the verse is the statement that means that the people turn in repentance to God, and the turning of the people in repentance to God is the turning that is the repentance and the humility of verse 155 of the sūrah.

**The Punishment and the Mercy That Encompasses All Things**

The verse states the response of God to the supplication of Moses, and the response is the response that is the two statements: the statement of the punishment, and the statement of the mercy. The statement of the punishment is the statement that begins with the warning: *I cause My Punishment to smite whomsoever I will*. Some of the commentators see this phrase as the specific reference to the earthquake that seized the Israelites in verse 155 of the sūrah, or to the atonement ordained by God in the other verse (2:54) that they should "slay their own," thus indicating that Moses has no power to deflect the punishment that God has decreed. Although the line asserts the freedom and the power of God to punish as He wills, the commentators often qualify this to mean that He punishes only those deserving of it, in accordance with His Wisdom. The other reading of the line is the reading that it means "I cause My Punishment to smite whosoever does evil." The statement of the mercy is the statement that is the "My Mercy encompasses all things," and the statement of the mercy that encompasses all things is the statement that has the great metaphysical importance in the Islamic thought, and is similar to the statement of the other verse (40:7), in which those who bear the Divine Throne say that God encompasses all things in Mercy and Knowledge. God's Knowledge is the only other Divine Attribute described in such the all-embracing manner in the Qur'an (6:80; 7:89; 20:98; 65:12). The preeminence of God's Mercy is expressed in the well-known sacred tradition in which God states, "My Mercy precedes My Wrath," and in the other verses God is said to have prescribed Mercy for Himself (6:12–13; 6:54).

**The Two Kinds of the Mercy**

The logic of the teaching of the verse is the logic that is worth stating in full. Some of the commentators understand the verses, including the present one, to indicate that God's Mercy is the infinite mercy, and that it is tantamount to His Will for the good in and for all the created beings. The commentators assert that the all-embracing Mercy of God is the source of every mercy between the human beings, and even between the animals, who manifest the Mercy of God in their tenderness and attachment to their young. God's Mercy, as manifested in His Forgiveness, is said to be so vast that even Iblīs will stretch forth his neck on the Day of Resurrection, hoping to be touched by it, and some of the commentators observe that Iblīs too is embraced by God's Mercy insofar as he exists. This alludes to the more profound point made by some of the commentators and the Islamic metaphysicians that there is a fundamental connection between the Divine Mercy and the existence itself, and the word for "Mercy" is from the same root as the word for "womb," and the Mercy of God thus embraces the creation as the womb embraces the unborn child and brings it into the world. For the Sufi thinkers, the existence itself is none other than the Divine Mercy, and the "Breath of the Compassionate" is the very substance of the universe through which all things are brought into being and sustained and to which all things return. Some of the commentators maintain that the Divine Mercy, while described here as "embracing all things," is nonetheless meant specifically for the believers. The other Islamic thinkers delineate the two kinds of the Divine Mercy: one is the universal mercy, the infinite mercy, bestowed upon all the creatures in this world, and metaphysically identical to the existence itself; and the other is the particular and the specific mercy, bestowed upon those who have earned it through the belief and the righteousness. It is this specific mercy that is alluded to in the subsequent statement of the verse, and the specific mercy is the mercy that is prescribed for those who are reverent, and who give alms, and who believe in the signs of God. The reverent here indicates those who are marked by the piety and who avoid the idolatry and the major sins, and those who give alms may refer to those who give the alms to the poor, but may also refer to those who engage in the purification of the souls. The Israelites who followed Moses, as well as the pious among the People of the Book in general, may be described by these qualities and hence are the recipients of this specific Divine Mercy. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the supplication of Moses and the response of God, and the statement of the supplication of Moses and the response of God are the statement of the supplication of Moses and the response of God that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the qualities of the recipients of the mercy in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:157

> **those who follow the Messenger, the unlettered Prophet, whom they find inscribed in the Torah and the Gospel that is with them, who enjoins upon them what is right, and forbids them what is wrong, and makes good things lawful for them, and forbids them bad things, and relieves them of their burden and the shackles that were upon them. Thus those who believe in him, honor him, help him, and follow the light that has been sent down with him; it is they who shall prosper.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Unlettered Prophet**

The verse continues the list of the qualities that describe those for whom God will prescribe His Mercy, and the continuation is the continuation that is the following of the Messenger, the unlettered Prophet. The description of the Prophet ﷺ as the unlettered is traditionally understood to mean that he was unable to read and write, a fact that is affirmed in various ways in the biographical literature concerning him. That the Prophet ﷺ was unable to read and write serves, in the Islamic tradition, as a fundamental proof of the miraculous nature of the Qur'an and the purity of the soul of the Prophet who was the recipient of it, and of its Divine provenance, since it would be otherwise impossible for a man who had not "studied" earlier works to produce the eloquent verses containing the knowledge of the past peoples and prophets. That the Prophet ﷺ was unlettered is understood to mean that his soul was not defiled by the profane knowledge and that it was the pure slate upon which the Divine Word could be "inscribed" in its purest form, untainted by the humanly acquired knowledge and learning. The word for "unlettered" is also related directly to the word for "mother," and the Sufi master Ibn ʿArabī understands the word as a kind of spiritual "infancy," connoting the state devoid of the external, discursive knowledge, for God brought the people forth from the bellies of their mothers, knowing naught, but also in the state of the innocence, the purity, and the utter receptivity. The word when used in relation to the Prophet ﷺ also seems to be related to his place in the sacred history in that he was not of the Jewish origin, but rather hailed from the Arab people, who were largely "illiterate" at this time and who had not read or studied the Torah, in particular. In the other verse (7:169), by contrast, those who were given the covenant of the Book, meaning the People of the Book, are described as those who have studied what is in it. Some have added that the word can also be related to Makkah, insofar as Makkah is described as the Mother of Cities in the other verse (42:7).

**The Inscription in the Torah and the Gospel**

The verse states the inscription of the Prophet ﷺ in the Torah and the Gospel, and the statement is the statement that means, for most of the commentators, that the qualities that identify and describe him as a prophet are mentioned in the Torah and the Gospel. In the other verse (61:6), Jesus says that he brings the glad tidings of a Messenger to come after him whose name is Aḥmad, and Aḥmad is one of the most often used names of the Prophet ﷺ in the Islamic tradition. According to the report, the Prophet ﷺ was described in the Torah with some of the same qualities attributed to him in the Qur'an, including that he is a giver of glad tidings and a warner, that he does not do evil to those who do evil to him, that he is kind and forgiving, that he is not rude or loud in the marketplace, and that he is a guardian for the unlettered. The report goes on to say that he will cause even those who are astray to utter, "There is no god but God," and hence open the eyes, the ears, and the hearts. Some of the commentators mention particular statements in the Torah and the Gospel that were interpreted by the Muslims as the references to the coming of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, including the prophecies about the descendants of Ishmael in the Bible, and the reference of Jesus to a spiritual "comforter" who would come after him.

**The Qualities of the Messenger and the Prosperity**

The verse states the qualities of the Prophet ﷺ, and the qualities are the qualities that are the enjoining of what is right and the forbidding of what is wrong, and the making of the good things lawful and the forbidding of the bad things, and the relieving of the burden and the shackles. The enjoining of what is right and the forbidding of what is wrong is the moral requirement and the characteristic of every believer and of the believing community mentioned in several verses (3:110, 114; 9:67, 71; 22:41; 31:17). The making of the good things lawful means that through the prophethood of the Prophet ﷺ the ritualistic restrictions that the pagan Arabs had placed on themselves, without the divine sanction, were abolished, and the forbidding of the bad things is the forbidding of the things such as the consumption of swine flesh and the practice of usury. The relieving of the burden and the shackles that were upon them refers to some of the difficult and the onerous commands and prohibitions in the Torah, which the Jews were specifically obligated to follow as part of the covenant they made with God, but which were abrogated by the Qur'an. The light that has been sent down with him refers to the Qur'an, which along with the other scriptures is described as being or containing the light (5:15; 3:184; 6:91). The verse closes with the statement of the prosperity, and the statement of the prosperity is the statement that is the teaching that the people who believe in the Messenger, and who honor him, and who help him, and who follow the light that has been sent down with him, it is they who shall prosper. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating: the people who believe in the Messenger and who follow the light are the people who are in the state of the one who are the objects of the prosperity, and the people who are the objects of the prosperity are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the recipients of the specific mercy of God, on the form of the teaching of the previous verse. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the qualities of the recipients of the mercy, and the statement of the qualities of the recipients of the mercy is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the message to the mankind in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:158

> **Say, “O mankind! Truly I am the Messenger of God unto you all—Him to Whom belongs Sovereignty over the heavens and the earth. There is no god but He. He gives life and causes death. So believe in God and His Messenger, the unlettered Prophet, who believes in God and His Words; and follow him, that haply you may be guided.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Message to the Mankind**

The verse interrupts the account of Moses and his people to issue the command to the Prophet ﷺ to identify himself as the Messenger of God unto you all, and the interruption of the verse is the interruption that is the teaching that the story of the prophets is the story that is the introduction of the message of the final prophet. The statement of the verse, situated at the end of a series of accounts about the previous prophets, is understood by some of the commentators to mean that the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is distinguished from the earlier prophets in that each of those prophets was a prophet to his people or the people of his region, but Muhammad ﷺ is a prophet for all of the humanity. In the report, the Prophet ﷺ states that his having been sent to all the humanity is one of the five distinctions given specifically to him by God. The claim of such a distinction may be based upon the fact that the mission of the Prophet ﷺ was to both the People of the Book and the "unlettered," that is, the Arabs or those without a scripture (3:20).

**The Sovereignty and the Words of God**

The verse states the two declarations of God, and the two declarations are the two declarations that are the sovereignty over the heavens and the earth, and the giving of the life and the causing of the death. The statement of the sovereignty of God over the heavens and the earth is the statement that is the teaching that the sovereignty of God is the sovereignty that is the sovereignty that is the object of the worship. The statement of the giving of the life and the causing of the death is the statement that is found throughout the Qur'an (2:258; 3:156; 9:116; 10:56; 23:80; 40:68; 44:8; 53:44; 57:2) and is reflected in the Divine Names al-Muḥyī, the One who gives life, and al-Mumīt, the One who causes death. The statement of the "His Words" of the verse, which the unlettered Prophet believes in, refers to all of the signs of God, or to the three Abrahamic scriptures, the Torah, the Gospel, and the Qur'an, and by implication to all the sacred scriptures. According to some of the reports, the "His Words" refers specifically to Jesus, the prophet who most closely precedes Muhammad ﷺ and who is referred to as a Word from God (3:45), although such an interpretation seems less plausible in this context. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating: the prophet who believes in God and His Words is the prophet who is in the one who is the object of the following of the people, and the prophet who is the object of the following of the people is the prophet who is in the one who is the object of the guidance of the people, on the form of the statement of the "that haply you may be guided" of the verse. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the message to the mankind, and the statement of the message to the mankind is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the community of the people of Moses in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:159

> **And among the people of Moses is a community that guides by the truth and does justice thereby.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Community That Guides by the Truth**

The verse states the statement of the community among the people of Moses, and the statement is the statement that is the guiding by the truth and the doing of the justice: *And among the people of Moses is a community that guides by the truth and does justice thereby*. The commentators differed regarding the identity of the community among the followers of Moses that guides by the truth and does justice, that is, renders the judgment according to the principles of truth, and the same description is given to a community among those God has created in verse 181 of the sūrah. Despite the clear statement of the verse that among the people of Moses, that is, the Jews, are those who are just and rightly guided, the commentators have often sought to limit the application of this statement. According to some of the legendary accounts, the community in the present verse refers to a group or a tribe of the Israelites who separated themselves from the rest, when the latter began to go astray, and then traveled eastward beyond China, and some say that they continue to live according to the law brought by Moses, while the others say they became the Muslims. The other commentators assert that this is a reference to those Jews in Madinah in the time of the Prophet ﷺ who became the Muslims, although this seems inconsistent with the description of this group as a community, which often connotes an independent religious community or at least a sizable group of people, and the Jews who became the Muslim were thereafter considered part of the Muslim community, rather than a separate community unto themselves.

**The Plain Sense of the Verse**

These various attempts to qualify the reference to this righteous community among the people of Moses contradict the plain sense of the verse, which indicates that a group among the people of Moses, in the sense of being the followers of the Jewish Law and ritual, continue to live and worship righteously according to the teachings of the Torah. The other verses support the literal reading as well, including verse 168 of the sūrah, which indicates that there were the communities of the righteous Jews still existing at the time of the revelation of the Qur'an (3:113–15, 199). The commentators do not usually support the most literal and the obvious meaning of this verse, perhaps because it seems inconsistent with the common interpretation of verse 157 of the sūrah as meaning that all the human beings should recognize and follow the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ as their prophet.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:160

> **And We divided them into twelve tribes, communities. And We revealed to Moses, when his people asked him for water, “Strike the rock with thy staff.” Then twelve springs gushed forth from it: all the people knew their drinking place. And We shaded them with clouds, and sent down manna and quails upon them, “Eat of the good things We have provided you.” And they wronged Us not, but themselves did they wrong.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Twelve Tribes and the Twelve Springs**

The verse states the acts of God with the people of Moses, and the acts are the acts that are the division of the tribes, and the striking of the rock, and the shading with the clouds, and the sending down of the manna and the quails. The division of the Israelites into the twelve tribes is on the form of the statement of the other verse (5:12), in which God made the covenant with the Children of Israel and raised among them the twelve chieftains. The striking of the rock with the staff of Moses is on the form of the statement of the other verse (2:60), and the Bible, in the verse of Exodus, mentions the Israelites coming upon a place in the desert called Elim, where there were the twelve wells, and recounts Moses being instructed by God to strike a rock with his staff to produce the water for his people, although no mention is made of the twelve springs specifically. The twelve springs that gushed forth from the rock are the twelve springs that are the provision of God for the twelve tribes, and the provision of God for the twelve tribes is the provision that is the teaching that each of the tribes knew its drinking place, and the knowledge of the drinking place of each of the tribes is the knowledge that is the teaching that the provision of God is the provision that is the provision that is the order of God.

**The Shading and the Manna and the Quails**

The verse states the acts of the shading with the clouds and the sending down of the manna and the quails, and the acts are on the form of the statement of the nearly identical passage in the other verse (2:57), and the other sūrah (20:80–81). The Qur'an repeatedly exhorts the people to eat the wholesome and the lawful food provided by God, and the other examples not exclusively addressed to the Israelites are in the other verses (2:168, 172; 6:141–42; 16:69, 114; 23:51; 67:15). The verse closes with the statement that is the teaching that the people did not wrong God, but wronged themselves, and the statement is the statement that is the teaching that the error of the people is the error that is the wrong of the people upon themselves rather than the wrong of the people upon God.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:161

> **And when it was said unto them, “Settle in this town, and eat of that which is therein wheresoever you will, and say, ‘Remove the burden!’ And enter the gate prostrating, that We may forgive you your iniquities. We shall increase the virtuous.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command of the Settling and the Prostration**

The verse states the command of God to the people of Moses, and the command is the command that is the three parts: the settling in the town, the eating of that which is in the town, and the uttering of the word and the entering of the gate prostrating.
