The command of the "settle in this town" of the verse is on the form of the statement of the other verse (2:58) and the brief allusion to it in the other verse (4:154). The town here is said by some of the commentators to be Jerusalem, and is said by the other commentators to be the land of Syria or Palestine generally. The command of the eating of that which is in the town wheresoever they will is the command that is the provision of God for the people in the town, and the provision of God for the people in the town is the provision that is the abundance of the town.

**The Word of the Removal of the Burden**

The command of the uttering of the word of the verse is the command that is the word of the repentance: *and say, "Remove the burden!"* The word of the "remove the burden" of the verse is an exclamation that the Israelites are told to utter, indicating their desire to be forgiven for their sins. The logic of the command of the word is the logic that is worth stating in full: the command of the word is the command that is the teaching that the word of the repentance is the word that is the desire of the forgiveness, and the word of the repentance is the word that is the condition of the forgiveness of God, and the condition of the forgiveness of God is the condition that is the uttering of the word of the repentance in the state of the sincerity. The command of the entering of the gate prostrating is the command that is the teaching that the prostration of the people at the gate is the prostration that is the humility of the people before God, and the humility of the people before God is the humility that is the condition of the forgiveness of God, on the form of the statement of the "that We may forgive you your iniquities" of the verse. The promise of the "We shall increase the virtuous" of the verse is the promise that is the increase of the virtue of God for the people who are the virtuous, and the increase of the virtue of God for the people who are the virtuous is the increase that is the teaching that the virtue is the virtue that is the increase by God. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the command of the settling and the word and the prostration, and the statement of the command of the settling and the word and the prostration is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the substitution of the word in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:162

> **Then those among them who did wrong substituted a word other than that which had been said unto them. So We sent down upon them a torment from heaven for the wrong they used to do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**What Was Commanded and What Was Said**

The verse follows directly on the instruction at 7:161, which is unusually generous in what it gives and unusually precise in what it asks. The people are told to settle in the town, to eat of it wherever they wish, to say one word, and to enter the gate prostrating — and the forgiveness of their iniquities and an increase for the virtuous are attached to the doing of it.

The word they were to say is *ḥiṭṭah*, rendered here as *remove the burden*. It is a single term and the command is to utter it. What the wrongdoers among them did was substitute another word for it, and the verse records the substitution without yet naming the substitute.

The act is named in the commentary tradition *tabdīl* — substitution, replacement — and it is treated as a distinct offence rather than as a degree of ordinary disobedience. Refusing to do a commanded thing is *maʿṣiyah*: the command stands and the person fails it. Substituting is different in kind, because it asserts that something else will serve. The word is not declined; it is exchanged.

**Ḥiṭṭah into Ḥinṭah**

Al-Zamakhsharī supplies the substitute. Rather than saying *ḥiṭṭah* and prostrating, he reports, they said *ḥinṭah* — wheat — and entered the gate on their backsides, in mockery. The two words differ by a single letter, and the change converts a plea for the removal of a burden into the name of a grain.

The reading is famous and it is worth stating the objection the source note itself raises, because the objection is better than the wordplay. These words would not have been spoken in Arabic. The exchange depends on a phonetic near-identity that belongs to Arabic and not to the language the Israelites were speaking, so as an account of what was actually said it does not convince.

What survives the objection is the shape of the act rather than the syllables. The report is doing exegetical work on the kind of substitution intended: a change small enough to be passed off as compliance, and derisive enough to undo it. Entering the gate on their backsides is the same gesture performed with the body instead of the tongue — the form of the command kept and its direction reversed.

**Why Substitution Is the Graver Thing**

The commentators read the gate episode alongside the accusations of *taḥrīf*, distortion, levelled later in Sūrah al-Baqarah (2:75–79), and the connection is not incidental. If a received word may be exchanged for another, the exchange does not stop at that word. Ibn ʿĀshūr's formulation in the later tradition puts the stakes plainly: the sin of *tabdīl* is the declaration that the revelation's precise wording does not bind the hearer.

That is why the punishment in this verse is not delayed to a later generation or generalised into a warning. It falls on the substitution itself. The verse is the first recorded instance in the Qur'an's Israel narrative of a received word exchanged for a substitute, and it is also the first instance of that exchange answered directly from heaven.

The moral generalises without strain. Most departures from a command are not refusals. They are substitutions — a thing done in place of the thing asked for, close enough to be described as obedience by the person doing it. The verse treats that as the more serious case, not the more excusable one.

**Rijz: The Torment From Heaven**

The word for what is sent down is *rijz*. In classical Arabic it denotes foulness, defilement, loathsome filth, and the Qur'an uses it of idolatry itself — 22:30: *"So shun the filth of idols, and shun false speech"* — and of wine, gambling, idols and the divining arrows together at 5:90, *"a means of defilement, of Satan's doing"*. In the account of Badr at 8:11 it names what the water sent from the sky removes: the defilement of Satan from the believers' hearts.

The word is therefore doing double work here. Al-Ṭabarī and the commentators understand it in this verse as a punishment or an act of wrath, and the same term recurs in this sūrah at 7:134–35 for the plagues sent on Pharaoh's people and lifted again — and see also 29:34 and 34:5. But a punishment called *filth* is not merely a penalty. It is described as what it is: a defilement falling on people who had just defiled a command.

> *"So We sent down upon them a torment from heaven for the wrong they used to do."*

Two features of the clause are worth marking. The torment comes *from heaven*, which answers the direction of the offence — the word was changed on the ground and the reply descends. And the reason given is *bi-mā kānū yaẓlimūn*, on account of the wrong they *used to* do: the imperfect with *kānū* describes a settled habit rather than a single lapse. The substitution was not an incident. It was their way of dealing with commands.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:163

> **And ask them about the town that was by the sea, when they transgressed the Sabbath. Their fish would come to them, surfacing on the day of their Sabbath, but on the day when they did not observe the Sabbath, they would not come to them. Thus did We try them for their having been iniquitous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Town by the Sea**

The verse states the command of the asking about the town that was by the sea, and the command is the command that is on the form of the command of the asking about the stories of the sūrah, and the town that was by the sea is the town that is the town that the commentators speculate about. Some of the reports identify the town as a town called Aylah, between Midian and Sinai, while the other reports say that it refers to Tiberias or Midian itself. The people of the town transgressed the Sabbath by performing the acts, such as the working, that God had forbidden on the day that was reserved for the worship alone. Although in Islam there is no "Sabbath" in the same sense that one practices it in Judaism, with its extensive set of the ritual practices and proscriptions, the Qur'an nonetheless understands the observation of the Sabbath to be the requirement established in the Torah for the Children of Israel and the Jews in particular (2:65; 4:47, 154; 16:124).

**The Fish of the Sabbath**

The verse states the state of the fish of the town, and the state is the state that is the surfacing of the fish on the day of the Sabbath: *Their fish would come to them, surfacing on the day of their Sabbath, but on the day when they did not observe the Sabbath, they would not come to them*. On the day of their Sabbath, the people of the town would fish, although this was against the rules pertaining to the Sabbath, and the fish would appear all around them on the surface of the water. But on the other days of the week, when they did not observe the Sabbath, and so the hunting and the fishing were permitted, the fish would remain hidden below the surface. The logic of the state of the fish is the logic that is worth stating in full: the state of the fish that surfaces on the day of the Sabbath is the state that is the temptation of the people to fish on the Sabbath, since the fishing was especially easy and fruitful on that day, and the temptation of the people to fish on the Sabbath is the temptation that is the way for God to try the people for their having been iniquitous. According to one report, the question of the fishing on the Sabbath divided the Israelites in the town: a third of them wanted to fish, a third admonished against it, and a third neither fished themselves, nor admonished the others. The three groups of the people are the three groups that are the teaching that the state of the people is the state that is the three responses to the command of God: the response of the transgression, the response of the admonition, and the response of the silence. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the town by the sea and the state of the fish of the Sabbath, and the statement of the town by the sea and the state of the fish of the Sabbath are the statement of the town by the sea and the state of the fish of the Sabbath that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the three groups of the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:164

> **And when a community among them said, “Why do you admonish a people whom God is about to destroy or punish with a severe punishment?” They said, “As an excuse before your Lord, and that haply they may be reverent.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Group in the Town**

The Sabbath narrative in this sūrah divides the town into three groups, and this verse gives voice to the middle one. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī identify *a community among them* as those Israelites who did not fish on the Sabbath but also did not admonish those who did. They were not violators; they were bystanders, and this verse records their argument.

What they ask the admonishers is why anyone would keep warning a people already marked out for destruction or severe punishment. The question is not hostile in tone and it is not frivolous in content. It is a cost-benefit calculation: why spend effort where the outcome is settled? The reasoning is coherent, and that is exactly why the verse bothers to answer it. A bad argument does not need a reply; a plausible one does.

The answer given is itself two-part — an exoneration before God, and a hope that the warned might yet become reverent — and the verse sets out both rather than collapsing them into a single motive.

**An Excuse Before Your Lord**

The admonishers give two reasons, and the order is significant. The first is *an excuse before your Lord*. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī explain that they consider themselves obligated to forbid wrong, and having done so they have discharged a moral duty. The discharge does not depend on the outcome.

This separates the obligation from its efficacy, which is the direct answer to the bystanders' calculation. The bystanders measured the value of admonition by its expected success rate. The admonishers measure it by whether the duty was performed. On the second measure a warning delivered to someone who ignores it is not wasted; on the first it is pure loss.

The phrase *maʿdhirah* — excuse, or ground of exoneration — is doing careful work. It is not a claim that the warning will succeed, and it is not a performance for the sinners' benefit. It is an account to be rendered to God, and the admonisher's own standing before Him is what it protects.

**And That Haply They May Be Reverent**

The second reason is *and that haply they may be reverent* — the admonishers also hoped their warnings might bring the Sabbath-violators back to reverence for God and for the Mosaic Law. Hope is named alongside duty, and the two are not merged.

The particle *laʿalla* ("haply") keeps the hope honest. The admonishers do not claim their intervention will work; they say it may. Holding duty and uncertain hope together in one sentence is what allows them to keep working without either despairing when the warning fails or inflating its expected effect to make the effort feel worthwhile.

The narrative's outcome vindicates the ordering. The sūrah goes on to record that God saved those who forbade wrong and seized the wrongdoers with a severe punishment (7:165). The bystanders' predicted destruction happened; so did the deliverance of the people who thought the effort pointless to make.

**Why the Argument Still Persuades**

The bystander position survives because it looks like realism. Facing entrenched wrongdoing, it is genuinely tempting to conclude that speaking is futile and that the energy would be better spent elsewhere. The verse takes that temptation seriously enough to stage it as dialogue rather than dismiss it.

Its answer is structural rather than motivational. It does not promise that admonition works. It relocates the reason for admonishing from results to accountability, and then adds hope as a secondary motive that can bear uncertainty. A person who needs the second motive alone will stop when the results fail to appear; a person who has the first can continue.

The application is not confined to religious warning. Any situation where a person must decide whether to object to a wrong they almost certainly cannot prevent — in a family, an institution, a profession — turns on the same question, and the verse supplies the same answer: the objection is owed whether or not it lands.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:165

> **When those who were violating the Sabbath forgot that whereof they had been reminded by those who admonished them, God punished the violators and saved those who forbade evil—that is, by exhorting their fellow Israelites to observe the Sabbath.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Punishment of the Violators and the Saving of the Admonishers**

The verse states the act of God after the forgetting of the violators of the Sabbath, and the act is the act that is the two parts: the punishment of the violators, and the saving of the admonishers: *God punished the violators and saved those who forbade evil*. The "those who forbade evil" of the verse refers to those who exhorted their fellow Israelites to observe the Sabbath, and the saving of the admonishers is the saving that is the reward of the duty of the admonition, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of the previous verse. The commentators are divided on the fate of those who abstained both from the fishing on the Sabbath and from forbidding the others to do so, and some of the commentators consider them to be among the saved. The logic of the division of the commentators is the logic that is worth stating: the people who abstained from the fishing and from the forbidding are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who did not transgress, but who also did not fulfill the duty of the admonition, and the people who did not transgress, but who also did not fulfill the duty of the admonition are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the division of the commentators, and the division of the commentators is the division that is the teaching that the silence is the state that is the state between the state of the transgression and the state of the admonition. The logic of the teaching of the verse is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who are punished are the people who are in the state of the one who are the violators of the Sabbath, and the people who are saved are the people who are in the state of the one who are the admonishers of the violators, and the people who are punished and the people who are saved are the two groups that are the teaching that the state of the people is the state that is the punishment or the state that is the saving, and the punishment and the saving are the two states that are the two responses of the state of the people in the state of the transgression and the admonition. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the punishment of the violators and the saving of the admonishers, and the statement of the punishment of the violators and the saving of the admonishers are the statement of the punishment of the violators and the saving of the admonishers that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the transformation of the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:166

> **Be you apes, outcast,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Transformation of the Violators**

The verse states the act of God upon the violators of the Sabbath, and the act is the act that is the transformation: *Be you apes, outcast*. When the violators of the Sabbath continued to be insolent concerning the Sabbath, despite the warnings and the reminders they had been given, they were punished severely. The statement of the verse is understood by many of the commentators to mean that the violators were physically transformed into the apes, since the divine imperative here may be taken to be the command that brings the named thing into being. The other commentators reject the literal reading and suggest, rather, that the hearts of the violators of the Sabbath were transformed to be like those of the apes, but not their physical form, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of the other verse (2:65). The statement of the verse may also indicate that God is leaving the violators to indulge their baser instincts, which can lead only to their humiliation and their being outcast in their community, and the latter reading seems consistent with the Biblical statement that one who works on the Sabbath shall be "cut off from among the people" (Exodus 31:14). The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who are the objects of the transformation of the verse are the people who are in the state of the one who are the violators of the Sabbath, and the people who are the objects of the transformation of the verse are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the examples of the teaching that the insolence concerning the command of God is the insolence that is the transformation of the people, and the transformation of the people is the transformation that is the transformation of the physical form or the transformation of the heart, and the transformation of the physical form or the transformation of the heart is the transformation that is the outcast of the people from the community. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the transformation of the violators, and the statement of the transformation of the violators is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the proclamation of the Lord in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:167

> **and Thy Lord proclaimed that He will send against them, until the Day of Resurrection, evil announcers. He will pour His Wrath over their heads; He will make them apes and pigs, and cast them into error. And he changed those who had wronged themselves, and He changed not that whereof they used to be.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Proclamation of the Lord**

The verse continues the statement of the punishment of the violators of the Sabbath, and the continuation is the continuation that is the proclamation of the Lord: *and Thy Lord proclaimed that He will send against them, until the Day of Resurrection, evil announcers*. The statement of the proclamation of the Lord indicates that God decreed, commanded, or swore. The statement of the "He will send against them, until the Day of Resurrection, evil announcers" of the verse may mean that God will grant other people the authority over the people, and some of the commentators mention that prior to the coming of Islam the Persian peoples as well as the Greeks, the Romans, and the Christians had gained the authority over the Jewish community. The people whom God "sends against them" could also refer to the Arabs, who assumed the political authority over much of the Jewish population after the early Islamic conquests. The other verses that speak of the Israelites as having been struck with the abasement for their transgressions are on the form of the statement of the other verses (2:61; 3:112), understood by some of the commentators as indicating a state of the weakness for them relative to the other peoples, and the other verse (17:5–8) mentions the destruction of the homes and the land of the Jews as well as their Temple by the others possessed of the great might.

**The Transgenerational Punishment**

The logic of the teaching of the verse is the logic that is worth stating in full. All of the statements of the commentators suggest that the punishment the Jews received for their transgressions occurred in this world and was inherited by the later generations of their descendants, which is similar to the perspective found in the Hebrew scriptures, although from the Islamic perspective this does not exclude the punishment in the Hereafter. Unlike the people of Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot, and Shuʿayb, the Israelites were not destroyed as a people, but their transgenerational punishment in this world extends the point made by those earlier accounts that the divine punishment can be meted out in this life as well as in the Hereafter (2:84). God is the swift in the retribution against those who invite His Punishment through their disobedience and their lack of faith, but He is the Forgiving and the Merciful toward the repentant, on the form of the teaching of the other verse (6:165).

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:168

> **And We divided them in this land into communities. Among them are the righteous, and among them are otherwise. And We tried them with good things and evil things, that perhaps they might turn [to Us].**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Division into the Communities**

The statement of the division of the Israelites into the communities on the earth is taken as the reference to the Diaspora, the existence of the smaller communities of the Jews in the various locations throughout the world.

**The Righteous and the Otherwise**

The verse states the statement of the two groups of the people, and the statement is the statement that is the righteous and the otherwise: *Among them are the righteous, and among them are otherwise*. The statement of the "some of them are righteous" of the verse indicates that a life of the righteousness continues to be possible for those who follow the Jewish Law and ritual as prescribed in the Torah, and the statement is on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 159 of the sūrah. The statement of the "others" of the verse refers to those who do not follow the law, although they continue to be the members of this community. Many of the commentators tend to see the Quranic affirmation of the righteousness among some of the Jews to pertain to the Jewish community of the past, before the coming of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ or even before the coming of Jesus, or else to the Jews in Madinah who became the Muslim. Such interpretations are open to the question, however, because the verse is clearly in the present tense, indicating that it pertains to the present and the indefinite future, and the reference is seemingly to those who have remained the Jews, not those who have joined the Islamic community. The verse that is in the present tense is the verse that is the statement of the present and the indefinite future, and the statement of the present and the indefinite future is the statement that is the teaching that the righteousness is the righteousness that is the possibility of the people of the law in the state of the present and the future.

**The Trial of the Good and the Evil**

The verse closes with the statement of the trial, and the statement of the trial is the statement that is the trial with the good things and the evil things: *And We tried them with good things and evil things, that perhaps they might turn [to Us]*. The "good things" with which God tries the people include the worldly provision and the ease, and the "evil things" are the various forms of the worldly adversity. The trial by both the good and the bad fortune is not particular to the Jewish population, but is meant in the more general sense, on the form of the teaching of the other verse (21:35), in which God tries the people with the evil and with the good, as a test, and unto God shall they be returned.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:169

> **Then We succeeded them with another generation. And the generation who succeeded them grasped the ephemeralities of this lower world and said, “We shall surely be forgiven.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Later Generation and the Ephemeralities**

And the generation who succeeded them grasped the ephemeralities of this lower world*. The generation who succeeded the followers of Moses is understood by the commentators to refer to some later Jews, and the Jewish religious leaders in particular, who brought about a certain corruption in the teachings and the Law of Moses by allowing some of the commands and the prohibitions to be neglected. Some of the commentators suggest that certain of the Jewish leaders took the bribery in exchange for easing the requirements of the law for certain people, on the form of the teaching of the other verse (19:59), regarding the descendants of Abraham and Israel: that they were succeeded by a generation who neglected the prayer and followed the base desires. The corrupt among this later generation grasp the ephemeralities of this lower world, meaning that they take whatever they desire of the fleeting, worldly things, whether they are lawful or unlawful, or even sinful, and assume they will be forgiven for it. The other Quranic passages suggest that certain of the Jews were overly confident about the leniency of God in the judgment toward them, on the form of the statement of the other verses (2:80; 3:24), in which they claim that the Fire will not touch them save for days numbered.

**The Statement of the Forgiveness**

The verse states the statement of the generation, and the statement is the statement that is the assumption of the forgiveness: *and said, "We shall surely be forgiven."* The statement of the generation is the statement that is the teaching that the generation who grasp the ephemeralities and who say that they will surely be forgiven are the generation who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are not truly repentant, and the generation who are not truly repentant are the generation who are in the state of the one who are the ones whose intention is to persist in their acts of the disobedience. The man who says that he will surely be forgiven is the man who is in the one who is the object of the teaching of the other verse (16:119), in which the verse states that the Lord is the Forgiving and the Merciful for those who commit the evil in ignorance, then later repent and make amends, and the man who is the object of the teaching of the other verse is the man who is in the one who is taught that the forgiveness of God is the forgiveness that is the forgiveness for those who are truly repentant and who mend their ways, and not the forgiveness for those who persist in their errors.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:170

> **As for those who cling to the Book and perform the prayer—surely We neglect not the reward of the workers of righteousness.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception After the Indictment**

The verse follows directly upon the indictment of v. 169, which described a generation that inherited the Book, grasped the ephemeralities of this lower world, and assumed they would be forgiven. Verse 170 turns to a different group within the same community.

The commentators read the contrast as deliberate. The previous verse condemned those who corrupted the teaching for worldly gain; this verse affirms those who did not. The two together describe a community divided by its response to the Book it inherited.

The opening phrase *wa-lladhīna yamassikūna bi-l-kitāb* — "and those who cling to the Book" — uses a verb of holding fast. Al-Qushayrī and al-Ṭabarī explain that clinging to the Book means continuing to adhere to the teachings, commands, and prohibitions established in the Torah.

The specification matters. The verse is not praising attachment to scripture in the abstract; it names the Torah and its law. The commentators read this as identifying a concrete group — those among the Jews who continued to observe the Mosaic law.

**"And Perform the Prayer" — the Single Act Named**

The verse pairs clinging to the Book with the performance of prayer: *wa-aqāmu ṣ-ṣalāh*. The commentators note that prayer is singled out from among the Torah's obligations.

The selection is significant given the context. Verse 169 described a generation that grasped worldly ephemeralities; the prayer is the act most directly opposed to that orientation, since it is performed at fixed times regardless of worldly advantage.

The commentators connect this to 19:59, cited in the commentary on v. 169: *Then they were succeeded by a generation who neglected prayer and followed base desires.* The two verses form a pair — the neglect of prayer and the pursuit of base desire on one side, the establishment of prayer and the clinging to the Book on the other.

The verb *aqāma* — to establish, to set upright — is stronger than mere performance. The commentators read it as indicating prayer maintained as a settled practice rather than performed occasionally.

**"We Neglect Not the Reward" — the Assurance and Its Parallels**

The verse closes with an assurance: *innā lā nuḍīʿu ajra l-muṣliḥīn* — "surely We neglect not the reward of the workers of righteousness." The commentators explain that this means God will not fail to reward them for their deeds.

The commentators supply an extensive set of parallels, and the recurrence is what gives the phrase its weight: 3:171, 9:120, 11:115, 12:56, 12:90, and 18:30 all use the same formulation.

The verb *aḍāʿa* denotes losing something through neglect. The commentators read the negation as addressing a specific anxiety — that deeds performed without recognition, or within a community under condemnation, might simply go unreckoned.

The word *muṣliḥūn* — workers of righteousness, or those who set things right — is broader than the prayer just mentioned. The commentators read it as encompassing the whole of the conduct described: clinging, establishing, and reforming.

**What the Verse Says About the Torah's Observers**

The commentators are explicit about the verse's implication, and it is worth stating plainly. Al-Qushayrī and al-Ṭabarī's reading indicates that those who are Jews and continue to follow the Torah and Mosaic Law will be rewarded for their good deeds.

The commentators connect the verse to 2:62, which states that Jews, Sabaeans, and Christians who believe and work righteousness shall have their reward with their Lord, and to the parallel at 5:69.

This reading places the verse in tension with the condemnation of v. 169, and the commentators hold both. The same community contains those who corrupted the teaching for gain and those who clung to it faithfully; the verse distinguishes them rather than collapsing them.

It should be recorded that the later tradition has read 2:62 and its parallels in various ways, and that the relationship between this verse and the Qur'an's other statements about the People of the Book has been extensively discussed. The verse itself states a reward for those who cling and establish; the systematic conclusions drawn from it are the tradition's work, not the text's.

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Argument**

The placement of the verse is instructive. It comes between the indictment of the corrupt generation at v. 169 and the account of the covenant at Sinai at v. 171, where the mountain is lifted above the Israelites.

The commentators read the sequence as moving from corruption to faithfulness to covenant. The verse in the middle establishes that the community is not uniformly condemned — there were those who held, and their reward is assured.

This matters for the sūrah's broader purpose. Sūrat al-Aʿrāf repeatedly addresses the People of the Book, and its treatment is consistently differentiated. The verse supplies the positive counterpart to the criticism that surrounds it.

The assurance also does pastoral work for the sūrah's own audience. If the reward of those who clung to an earlier Book is not neglected, the reward of those who cling to this one is not in doubt. The verse's logic extends beyond its immediate subject.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:171

> **And when We made the Mount rise above them as a threat, and We said unto them, “Take hold of that which We have given you with strength, and remember it that perhaps you may become reverent.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Mountain Raised Above Them**

The statement of the raising of the mountain above them is similar to the description of the other verses (2:63, 93; 4:154), and in all the cases the statement refers to the making of the covenant at Sinai. The word for "we made the mountain rise" of the verse means literally to pluck something out from its roots, and suggests the removing of the mountain physically from its earthly base and causing it to hover above them as if it were a canopy. Some of the commentators indicate that this was done as a means of threatening the Israelites to accept the covenant of the Torah, as some of them had hesitated because of the difficult nature of the obligations the covenant imposed upon them. The mountain that is raised above the people is the mountain that is the threat of God, and the threat of God is the threat that is the acceptance of the covenant of the people, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 138 of the sūrah, in which the people came upon the people of the idols and the verse states the command of the setting of the matters aright.

**The Command of the Taking Hold and the Remembrance**

The verse states the command of God to the people, and the command is the command that is the two parts: the taking hold of that which God has given, and the remembrance: *Take hold of that which We have given you with strength, and remember it that perhaps you may become reverent*. The command of the taking hold of that which God has given refers to their covenantal obligation to obey the commands and the prohibitions in the Torah, and the command of the taking hold of the Torah is the command that is on the form of the command of the taking hold of the tablets with the strength in verse 145 of the sūrah. The command of the remembrance of the command is the command that is the teaching that the remembrance of the command of God is the remembrance that is the reverent of the people, on the form of the statement of the "that perhaps you may become reverent" of the verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:172

> **And when your Lord brought forth from the children of Adam, from their loins, their descendants, and made them bear witness of themselves—“Am I not your Lord?” They said, “Yes, we testify”—so that you may not say on the Day of Resurrection, “Indeed, we were unaware of this.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Cornerstone of the Sacred History**

The verse is the verse that is in many ways the cornerstone of the Islamic sacred history and the anthropology, and the verse establishes that the fundamental relationship between God and all the human beings is premised upon the simple, the unmediated recognition of His Lordship at the moment of their pretemporal creation. The event recounted in this verse is widely referred to as the pretemporal covenant, or the pact, although these terms do not appear in the verse itself, and the covenant is the covenant made by God with all of the humanity prior to their earthly existence. The covenant of the verse is connected by the commentators with the pretemporal covenant between God and the prophets (3:81; 33:7), and is considered by some to be subsequent to it.

The Qur'an mentions the covenants or the pacts between God and the believers generally (5:1, 7; 6:152; 13:20, 25; 16:91, 95; 57:8), as well as the covenants with Abraham (2:124–25), the Israelites (2:40, 63, 83–84, 93; 4:154; 5:70), the Christians (5:14), and the People of the Book collectively (3:187). But the making of the covenant described in the present verse is unmediated and universal, contracted directly between God and all the humanity, and can thus be interpreted as the basis of all the later and the specific covenants mediated by the prophets. The logic of the universality of the covenant is the logic that is worth stating in full: the covenant that is unmediated and universal is the covenant that is the covenant of all the humanity, and the covenant of all the humanity is the covenant that is the basis of all the later and the specific covenants, and the basis of all the later and the specific covenants is the basis that is the teaching that the covenant of the prophets is the covenant that is the renewal of the covenant of the pretemporal witnessing.

**The Primordial Nature and the Imprint**

The verse is connected with the Quranic notion of the primordial nature (30:30), with which all the human beings were originally endowed, indicating that the innate recognition of the Oneness of God constitutes the essence of being human. Even though the human beings do not remember the pretemporal covenant, their testimony to the Lordship of God is understood to have left an indelible imprint upon their souls and to have established the moral responsibility for them. When these souls are engendered in the earthly bodies and reach the age of the moral understanding and the accountability, the innate knowledge is reawakened in those who believe by their encounter with the prophetic teachings, the scriptures, and the divine laws, which serve as the reminder and the renewal of their initial covenant with God. Therefore, if a child dies before reaching the age of the moral and the religious responsibility, he or she is considered to have died according to the first covenant and the original primordial nature, and so in the state of the moral purity. However, for those who fail to affirm the original covenant by rejecting the later covenant, that is, by denying the messages brought to them by their prophets and scriptures, their initial covenant will not benefit them.

**The Progeny and the Witnessing**

The verse states the act of God with the progeny of Adam, and the act is the act that is the bringing forth of the progeny from the loins. Some of the commentators describe this pretemporal event as the one in which God brought forth all the progeny of Adam from his loins, specifically, and some argue that this was done immediately after God had blown His Spirit into Adam (15:29; 32:9; 38:72), and the others debate whether it occurred before or after his fall to the earth. Many observe, however, that God takes the progeny not from Adam, but from the children of Adam and from their loins, in the plural, indicating simply that He brought forth all the future generations that have appeared and will appear until the end of time. The word for "progeny" of the verse derives from the root that in its most literal sense denotes the small particles, the atoms, or the seeds that are scattered, but also connotes the "progeny" or the "offspring" in over a dozen Quranic verses (2:124; 3:34; 4:9). Some of the commentators indicate that the progeny were drawn forth in the form of the "particles" or the "seeds," suggesting the physically unformed state of the children of Adam during this encounter, and some questioned how it was possible for the progeny as the "particles" to hear and respond to God's question, and because the intellect was not considered to manifest itself in the human beings until years after the birth, they also questioned how these "particles" knew what they were saying or could be held accountable for it. Given these issues, some of the commentators consider this verse the symbolic description of an innate covenant, or the symbolic description of the temporal process by which the human beings realize the divine Oneness, arguing that as the individuals grow in the intellect and contemplate the world around them, the existence of a single God becomes innately clear to them, and they witness to this truth inwardly, since they bear this knowledge already in their primordial nature. The other commentators reject this interpretation as against the plain meaning of the text, and argue that God temporarily bestowed the faculties of the intellect, the hearing, and the speech on the "particles," or the human "seed," just as He did when He caused the heavens and the earth to respond to Him (41:11; 41:21).

God causes the children of Adam to bear witness concerning themselves, or "against themselves," and the Qur'an mentions the human beings bearing witness against themselves, in both this world and the Hereafter, through their deeds and their very bodies (6:130; 7:37; 24:24; 36:65; 41:20–22). In the present verse, their witnessing takes the form of the response to God's question, "Am I not your Lord?", understood to be a declarative statement in the form of a question, requiring the hearers to bear witness to its truth. Poetically, some of the Muslim authors, such as Rūmī, have described the event in this verse as the "Day of Alast," referring to the first part of the question, and the "Day of Alast" therefore refers in the Islamic thought to a day beyond all the days reckoned in time. Their response is "Yes, we testify," and some of the reports consider the "we testify" to have been spoken by the other witnesses to their testimony, identified variously as the angels, Adam, the heavens and the earth, or God Himself, although most consider this a less likely reading.

**The Purpose of the Covenant and the Proof Against the Disbelievers**

The verse ends by explaining that the purpose of this questioning and witnessing was so that the human beings could not come on the Day of Resurrection claiming to have been unaware of God's Lordship or their duty to worship Him. One may question how one can be responsible, during the earthly life, for a testimony one cannot remember having made prior to coming into this world, and some of the commentators have argued that it is precisely this human inability to remember the event described in this verse that points to its symbolic nature and thus reject the idea that the pretemporal covenant, of which most of the human beings are not conscious and that they cannot recall, could be the basis of a responsibility that is binding upon them. When this verse is read in the wider Quranic context and juxtaposed with the Quranic prophetic history, however, this pretemporal recognition of God's Lordship can be understood as creating an innate disposition in the human beings toward recognizing and worshipping God during the earthly life and toward accepting the prophets and the messages they bring as the "reminders" of what the human beings already know inwardly, but have merely forgotten. Those who reject the prophets, therefore, are considered willfully ignorant, denying the truths that should, in any sincere and morally uncorrupted soul, resonate with a deep but forgotten knowledge of God that nevertheless still exists within them. In this sense, those who deny and reject the prophetic messages sent to them are described as the disbelievers, a word whose etymological meaning signifies the "covering over" of something, which in the religious sense refers to covering over the innate awareness of the truth of God's Lordship and Oneness that they bear within themselves. The pretemporal covenant, then, in conjunction with God's sending of messengers to all the people serves as a "proof" against the disbelievers who capriciously or cynically deny the prophetic messages that they know deep within themselves to be true.

The verse is also the basis for the more elaborate narrations, some of which are attributed to the Prophet ﷺ, that connect the pretemporal covenant with the predestinarian notions. Although the literal reading of this verse suggests that all the human beings have made the same covenant recognizing God's Lordship and therefore have the same possibility for realizing it in the earthly life, several of the reports suggest that the human moral destiny is linked to the distinctions made among the covenanters on the occasion of this pretemporal covenant. Some of the reports assert that God removed some of the progeny from the right side of Adam or with His Right Hand, and these human beings were destined for the Garden, while the others were removed from the left side of Adam or with the other Hand of God and were thus destined for the Hell. Still other reports indicate that some of the covenanters bore witness only reluctantly or deceptively, although this interpretation is rejected by other authorities. Some of the early traditions indicate that the covenanters on this day were also asked to bear witness to the prophethood of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, but that only some accepted it. Similarly, some of the early Sufi authors referred to the pretemporal covenant as the moment in which the spiritual elect were distinguished from the common people. Given the great importance in the Islamic thought of this verse and the event it describes, it was natural for some of the thinkers to link it with various notions of a spiritual hierarchy among the human beings and with the concept of predestination. Yet, although this pretemporal covenant may indeed be linked to the human moral destiny in a foundational way, because it created in the human beings an innate ability to recognize the religious truth, the plain meaning of the verse more plausibly indicates the universal potential in all the human beings for the moral and the spiritual attainment and the acceptance of the revelation. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the covenant of the pretemporal witnessing, and the statement of the covenant of the pretemporal witnessing is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the rejection of the excuse of the fathers in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:173

> **Or lest you should say, “[It is] only that our fathers ascribed partners unto God beforehand, and we were their progeny after them. Wilt Thou destroy us for that which the falsifiers have done?”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rejection of the Excuse of the Fathers**

The verse states the purpose of the covenant of the pretemporal witnessing, and the purpose is the purpose that is the rejection of the excuse of the fathers: *Or lest you should say, "[It is] only that our fathers ascribed partners unto God beforehand, and we were their progeny after them."* The verse indicates that the pretemporal covenant was also made with all, and not some, human beings, so that they could not offer the excuse, on the Day of Judgment, that they were merely following their fathers' practices of idolatry or "ascribing partners unto God." In several Quranic accounts, the people reject the prophets' warnings against the idolatry and the sin by arguing that they are following the practices of their fathers (2:170; 5:104; 7:28; 11:62, 87; 14:10; 21:53–54; 26:74; 31:21; 34:43; 43:22–24). This excuse is dismissed and mocked in the Qur'an (2:170; 5:104; 31:21; 43:24), and the present verse makes it clear that it can never be accepted, since God has endowed all the human beings, by virtue of the pretemporal covenant made with Adam and all his progeny in verse 172 of the sūrah, with an innate understanding of the religious truth and of His sole Lordship. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who offer the excuse of the fathers are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the rejection of the excuse by the covenant of the pretemporal witnessing, and the people who are the objects of the rejection of the excuse by the covenant of the pretemporal witnessing are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are taught that the excuse of the fathers is the excuse that is the excuse that can never be accepted, and the excuse that can never be accepted is the excuse that is the excuse of the following of the fathers in the state of the ignorance of the covenant of the pretemporal witnessing. The man who offers the excuse of the fathers is the man who is in the one who is the object of the teaching of the sūrah, and the man who is the object of the teaching of the sūrah is the man who is in the one who is taught that the covenant of the pretemporal witnessing is the covenant that is the rejection of the excuse of the fathers, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 28 and verse 70 of the sūrah.

**The Rejection of the Blind Imitation**

The basic Quranic teaching of the verse is connected to the widespread disparaging in the Islamic tradition of the "blind imitation," or taqlīd, of the religious views of the others. The blind imitation is considered permissible regarding the matters of the religious Law that are not based necessarily upon an innate moral or spiritual understanding, such as the time of day for the canonical prayers or the specific physical movements required in the prayer, and with regard to following the practice of the Prophet ﷺ, but the mere blind imitation is not allowed in the matters of the fundamental religious beliefs and principles, which one must accept with one's own intelligence to the extent possible. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating: the people who are the objects of the teaching of the blind imitation are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are taught that the acceptance of the matters of the fundamental religious beliefs and principles is the acceptance that is the acceptance with one's own intelligence, and the acceptance with one's own intelligence is the acceptance that is the renewal of the covenant of the pretemporal witnessing in the state of the earthly life.

**The Plea of the Destruction for the Falsifiers**

The verse closes with the statement of the plea, and the statement of the plea is the statement that is on the form of the statement of the previous verse: *Wilt Thou destroy us for that which the falsifiers have done?* The plea of the verse is similar in form to the plea of Moses to God, "Wilt Thou destroy us for that which the fools among us have done?" in verse 155 of the sūrah, and the similarity of the two pleas is the similarity that is the teaching that the plea of the people who are the objects of the destruction for the falsifiers is the plea that is the plea that is rejected by the justice of God, and the plea that is rejected by the justice of God is the plea that is the teaching that the destruction of God is the destruction that is the destruction for the deeds of the people rather than the destruction for the deeds of the others. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the rejection of the excuse of the fathers and the statement of the plea, and the statement of the rejection of the excuse of the fathers and the statement of the plea are the statement of the rejection of the excuse of the fathers and the statement of the plea that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the expounding of the signs in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:174

> **Thus do We expound the signs, that haply they may return.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Expounding of the Signs**

The verse states the act of God with the signs, and the act is the act that is the expounding of the signs: *Thus do We expound the signs, that haply they may return*. The statement of the expounding of the signs is on the form of the statement of the other verses (6:55, 97–98, 126; 7:32, 52; 9:11; 10:5, 24; 11:1; 13:2; 30:28), and the statement is the statement that serves also as the basis for the title of the other sūrah (41:3), and the title of the sūrah is the title that means the "expounded" or the "made distinct." The expounding of the signs means that God provides a variety of the arguments and the indications for His Oneness and Lordship, and the variety of the arguments and the indications is the variety that is the teaching that the signs of God are the signs that are the signs that are expounded the signs that are the objects of the return of the people.

**The Return of the People**

The verse states the purpose of the expounding of the signs, and the purpose is the purpose that is the return of the people: *that haply they may return*. The word for "return" of the verse is the word that carries the sense of the turning back, the one who turns back to God, and the turning back to God of the people is the turning back that is the repentance of the people, and the repentance of the people is the repentance that is the object of the mercy of God, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 153 of the sūrah. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who are the objects of the expounding of the signs are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the return, and the people who are the objects of the return are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the teaching that the expounding of the signs is the expounding that is the means of the return of the people, and the means of the return of the people is the means that is the teaching that the sign that is expounded is the sign that is the sign that benefits the one who is in the turning back to God, and the sign that is expounded is the sign that is the sign that does not benefit the one who is in the hardness of the heart, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 146 of the sūrah. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the expounding of the signs and the statement of the return of the people, and the statement of the expounding of the signs and the statement of the return of the people are the statement of the expounding of the signs and the statement of the return of the people that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the account of the one who cast off the signs in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:175

> **And recite unto them the account of the one to whom We gave Our signs, but he cast them off. So Satan made him his follower, and he became one of the deviant.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The One Who Cast Off the Signs**

The verse states the command of the recitation of the account of the one to whom God gave His signs, and the command is the command that is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ: *And recite unto them the account of the one to whom We gave Our signs, but he cast them off*. The verses that come at the end of several narratives about the people who rejected their prophets and the messages and the signs they brought may be read as the general warning about the moral fate of those who "cast off" and deny the signs that God has sent them, and the general reading is the reading that is preferred by al-Rāzī. Many of the early commentators considered the one to whom God gave His signs to refer to Balʿām Bāʿūrāʾ, the Balaam of Beor, a spiritually gifted man, capable of receiving certain divine messages and having his supplications answered by God, who was compelled or bribed to utter the imprecations against Moses. The various narratives of Balaam provided in the Quranic commentaries have many details in common with the Biblical narrative of this same figure (Numbers 22–24), but they also differ, significantly, from it in that the Biblical Balaam, though rebuked by God, refused to curse Moses and his people and instead conveyed God's blessing upon them, while in most of the Quranic commentary accounts, Balaam is similarly reluctant to curse the Israelites, but in the end is persuaded to do so. Also, the reports included in some of the commentaries identify Balaam as an Israelite, while in the Biblical account he is a prophetic figure from a different people, and despite the widespread association of these verses with Balaam, the connection seems tenuous.

The other reports identify the one to whom God gave His signs as an Arab named Umayyah ibn Abī Ṣalt, who had studied the Jewish and the Christian scriptures and hoped he would be the prophet that he believed these sources had been predicting, and unlike many of the Arabs of his time, he is said to have believed in the Oneness of God and in the Hereafter. But when the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ arose as a prophet, Umayyah was resentful and refused to accept him or the Quranic message. The "Our signs" of the verse is glossed by the commentators as referring to either the "prophethood" of the one in question generally, or his reported knowledge of the "greatest Name of God," although it more likely indicates the knowledge of the scriptures, which would apply in all the interpretations of the verse. This knowledge was then cast off, "like a snake shedding its skin," when he abandoned and denied the signs. The word for "cast them off" of the verse is the word that carries the sense of the shaking off, the one who shakes off the sign from himself, and the shaking off of the sign from oneself is the shaking off that is the one who abandons and denies the signs.

**The Following of Satan**

The verse states the result of the casting off of the signs, and the result is the result that is the following of Satan: *So Satan made him his follower, and he became one of the deviant*. The statement of the following of Satan may also be interpreted to mean "Satan overtook him," as one overtakes another on a path. In either case, the verse suggests that rejecting the signs of God leaves one vulnerable to the influence of Satan, on the form of the teaching of the other verse (15:42), which says that Satan does not have authority over the faithful servants of God.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:176

> **Had We willed, We would surely have elevated him thereby, but he inclined toward the earth and followed his caprice. Thus his parable is that of a dog: if you attack him, he lolls out his tongue, and if you leave him alone, he lolls out his tongue. That is the likeness of the people who deny Our signs. So recount the stories, that haply they may reflect.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Elevation and the Inclining Toward the Earth**

The verse states the statement of God about the one who cast off the signs, and the statement is the statement that is the two parts: the statement of the elevation, and the statement of the inclining toward the earth. The statement of the elevation is the statement that is the elevation by virtue of the signs that were given to the one: *Had We willed, We would surely have elevated him thereby*. Some of the commentators understand this statement to mean that God did not will him to be spiritually ennobled in this way and thus caused or allowed him to turn his back on these signs, and the other commentators interpret, by contrast, to mean that had God willed, He could have compelled him to remain faithful to the signs that he had been given, but allowed him instead to make freely his own moral choice. The two readings of the statement are the two readings that are the teaching that the elevation of God is the elevation that is the elevation by virtue of the signs, and the elevation by virtue of the signs is the elevation that is the elevation that is the object of the choice of the one who is given the signs. The statement of the inclining toward the earth is the statement that means that the one became enamored of the worldly goods and pursued them, and the inclining toward the earth and the following of the caprice of the one is the inclining toward the earth and the following of the caprice that are the one who prefers the worldly goods to the elevation by virtue of the signs.

**The Parable of the Dog**

The verse states the parable of the one who cast off the signs, and the parable is the parable that is the dog: *Thus his parable is that of a dog: if you attack him, he lolls out his tongue, and if you leave him alone, he lolls out his tongue*. The comparison of those who deny the signs of God to a dog, a ritually unclean animal in many interpretations of the Islamic Law, which "lolls out its tongue" or "pants" whether "attacked" or "left alone," suggests that such people are devoid of the spiritual discernment to know what is good or harmful for them and are thus impervious to the guidance. The parable of the dog is similar to the idea expressed in the other verse (2:6), in which it is the same for the disbelievers whether the prophet warns them or warns them not, and they do not believe. The other verses compare the human beings to the cattle (7:179) and the frightened asses (74:49–51) to indicate their lack of the spiritual discernment. The logic of the parable is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who are the objects of the parable of the dog are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who deny the signs of God, and the people who deny the signs of God are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the parable of the dog, and the parable of the dog is the parable that is the same state in the two conditions, and the same state in the two conditions is the same state that is the lolling out of the tongue in the state of the attack and the lolling out of the tongue in the state of the leaving alone, and the lolling out of the tongue in the two states is the lolling out of the tongue that is the teaching that the denial of the signs of God is the denial that is the state that does not change in the two conditions of the warning and the non-warning.

**The Command of the Recounting of the Stories**

The verse closes with the command of the recounting of the stories, and the command is the command that is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ: *So recount the stories, that haply they may reflect*. The "stories" of the command refers to the accounts of the past communities mentioned in this sūrah, and the command of the recounting of the stories is the command that is on the form of the command of the expounding of the signs in verse 174 of the sūrah. For al-Rāzī, these verses serve as a warning to all the people of learning that the spiritual knowledge they have been given by God can be removed if they incline toward the base desires and the worldly concerns, citing the report in which the Prophet ﷺ states that whoever increases in knowledge, but does not increase in guidance, increases in naught but distance from God. The logic of the warning is the logic that is worth stating: the people of learning who incline toward the base desires and the worldly concerns are the people of learning who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the warning of the verse, and the people of learning who are the objects of the warning of the verse are the people of learning who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are taught that the spiritual knowledge is the spiritual knowledge that is the knowledge that is the object of the elevation if it is kept in the state of the guidance, and the spiritual knowledge is the spiritual knowledge that is the knowledge that is the object of the removal if it is inclined toward the base desires and the worldly concerns. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the parable of the dog and the statement of the command of the recounting of the stories, and the statement of the parable of the dog and the statement of the command of the recounting of the stories are the statement of the parable of the dog and the statement of the command of the recounting of the stories that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the evil parable in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:177

> **Evil is the parable of the people who denied Our signs and wronged themselves.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Evil Parable of the Deniers**

The verse states the statement of the evil parable of the people who denied the signs of God and wronged themselves, and the statement is the statement that is the general reading of the previous verses: *Evil is the parable of the people who denied Our signs and wronged themselves*. The verse suggests that the foregoing discussion of the one to whom God gave His signs, but he cast them off, in verse 175 of the sūrah, is meant to refer in a general way to all those who reject the signs given to them, and the general reading of the verse is the reading that is the extension of the teaching of the parable of the dog to all the people who deny the signs of God. The statement of the "wronged themselves" of the verse is the statement that is on the form of the statement of the other verses (9:70; 16:33; 29:40; 30:9), and the statement is the statement that is the teaching that the wrong of the people is the wrong that is the wrong of the people upon themselves rather than the wrong of the people upon God, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 160 of the sūrah. The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating: the people who deny the signs of God and who wrong themselves are the people who are in the state of the one who are the objects of the evil parable, and the people who are the objects of the evil parable are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are taught that the parable of the people is the parable that is the evil parable if it is the parable of the one who denies the signs of God and wrongs himself, and the parable of the people is the parable that is the good parable if it is the parable of the one who believes in the signs of God and obeys them, on the form of the teaching of the other verse (14:5? 24:35) of the sūrah, in which the parable of the light of God is the parable of the good parable. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the evil parable of the people who deny the signs of God, and the statement of the evil parable of the people who deny the signs of God is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the guidance and the leading astray in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:178

> **Whomsoever God guides, he is rightly guided; and whomsoever He leads astray, it is they who are the losers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Guidance and the Leading Astray**

The verse states the statement of the guidance and the leading astray of God, and the statement is the statement that is the two parts: *Whomsoever God guides, he is rightly guided; and whomsoever He leads astray, it is they who are the losers*. That God guides, but may also "lead astray," is indicated in several verses, causing some of the theologians to hold that all the guidance and the leading astray is a matter of the will of God. The statement of the "whomsoever God guides, he is rightly guided" of the verse is the statement that means that the one whom God guides is said to be the rightly guided, so that none shall lead him astray (39:37), and the statement of the "whomsoever He leads astray, it is they who are the losers" of the verse is the statement that means that the ones whom He leads astray are the losers, in both this world and the Hereafter, and are elsewhere said to have no protector (17:97; 18:17).

The theological discussion of the verse is the discussion that is worth stating in full. Although this is presented as a matter of the will of God (6:125; 74:31), God is said, more specifically, to "lead astray" the iniquitous (2:26), the disbelievers (40:74), and the prodigal doubter (40:34), suggesting to many of the theologians that God leads the people astray only as a consequence or punishment for their actions. The two readings of the theologians are the two readings that are the teaching that the leading astray of God is the leading astray that is the leading astray the will of God, or the leading astray the consequence of the actions of the people, and the two forms of the leading astray of God are the two forms that are the teaching that the leading astray of God is the leading astray that is the consequence of the state of the people in the state of the iniquity and the disbelief. The logic of the teaching of the verse is the logic that is on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 146 and verse 155 of the sūrah, and the logic of the teaching of the verse is the logic that is worth stating: the people who are the objects of the guidance of God are the people who are in the state of the one who are the rightly guided, and the people who are the objects of the leading astray of God are the people who are in the state of the one who are the losers in both this world and the Hereafter, and the people who are the objects of the guidance of God and the people who are the objects of the leading astray of God are the two groups that are the teaching that the state of the people is the state that is the guidance or the state that is the leading astray. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the guidance and the leading astray, and the statement of the guidance and the leading astray are the statement of the guidance and the leading astray that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the creation for the Fire in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:179

> **We have indeed created for Hell many among jinn and men: they have hearts with which they understand not; they have eyes with which they see not; and they have ears with which they hear not. Such as these are like cattle. Nay, they are even further astray. It is they who are heedless.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Creation for the Fire and the Moral Choice**

The verse states the statement of God about the people who are created for the Fire, and the statement is the statement that is the heart of the issue of the relationship between the human and the divine control of the human moral choice and destiny: *We have indeed created for Hell many among jinn and men*. For many of the theologians who subscribed to one of the schools, the verse should be read literally to mean that God created certain human beings and jinn in such a way that they are innately inclined toward the acts that lead inevitably to their perdition, and the verse is consistent with the other verses indicating that God guides some and misleads others, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of the previous verse, and is also consistent with several of the reports, including the one that indicates that while the child is still unborn, God commands an angel to write down his or her worldly provision, life span, works, and fate as either the blissful or the wretched in the Hereafter. The other theologians, however, read the verse to mean that God has the foreknowledge of the moral choices and destinies of the individuals, but does not determine them. The statement that God created them for the Fire may simply be meant to indicate their final end, as if to say, "We have created many jinn and men only to have them end up in the Fire," or it may express the idea that some jinn and men are so deeply entrenched in the disbelief that it is as if they were created for the Fire. For the theologians who read the verse in this manner, interpreting this verse to mean that God literally creates some individuals to disobey His commands would be to assert that God has given certain individuals the moral commands that they are innately incapable of fulfilling, something that would be futile, unjust, and unmerciful and thus cannot be attributed to God, and the literal reading of this verse would conflict with the literal reading of the other verse (51:56), in which God states that He did not create the jinn and the mankind, save to worship Him.

**The Faculties That Do Not Function**

The verse states the state of the faculties of the people who are created for the Fire, and the state is the state that is the three faculties: the heart, the eyes, and the ears: *they have hearts with which they understand not; they have eyes with which they see not; and they have ears with which they hear not*. The eyes, the ears, and the heart, the last of which is considered the seat of the understanding, the intelligence, and the knowledge, comprise the faculties and the organs by which the individuals can be guided to the truth. But for some of the people, these faculties do not fulfill their divinely intended function (2:18; 18:101; 47:23), either because God has "sealed" or "covered" them (2:7; 47:16), or because the human beings have refused to employ them properly. Their hearts do not contemplate the various signs of God and His Oneness or the messages sent through His prophets, and their eyes do not see the obvious signs of God's creative power in the world around them, and their ears do not hear the teachings of the scriptures, but only the call of the temptation, on the form of the statement of the other verse (41:5), in which the disbelievers say to the Prophet ﷺ that their hearts are under the coverings from that to which he calls them, and in their ears is a deafness, and between them and him there is a veil.

**The Comparison to the Cattle**

The verse compares such persons to the cattle, and the comparison is the comparison that is on the form of the comparison of the other verse (25:44), for the cattle do not possess the intellect with which to understand the religious truth. Those who do not employ their intellect are thus no better than the beasts, in fact, they are even worse for squandering the gift of intellect, which they have been given precisely for the purpose of discerning the truth, and like the cattle, such persons are concerned only with fulfilling the base desires for the food and the comfort. The verse then asserts that they are even further astray than the cattle, since even the cattle know enough to flee from what is harmful for them and to seek out what nourishes them, while the disbelievers work toward their own spiritual destruction. Moreover, the Islamic tradition holds that the animals and all the natural phenomena recognize their Creator, but the disbelievers do not, and although the cattle can be guided by the call of their herdsman, even if they hear it only as the call and the shout (2:171), the disbelievers refuse to be guided at all. The logic of the comparison is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who are compared to the cattle are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who do not employ their intellect, and the people who do not employ their intellect are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are even further astray than the cattle, and the people who are even further astray than the cattle are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who work toward their own spiritual destruction, and the people who work toward their own spiritual destruction are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the statement of the "it is they who are heedless" of the verse, and the statement of the "it is they who are heedless" of the verse is the statement that is the heedlessness of the people, and the state of the heedlessness of the people is the state that is the introduction of the statement of the Most Beautiful Names of God in the next verse. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the creation for the Fire and the state of the faculties and the comparison to the cattle, and the statement of the creation for the Fire and the state of the faculties and the comparison to the cattle are the statement of the creation for the Fire and the state of the faculties and the comparison to the cattle that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the Most Beautiful Names of God in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:180

> **Unto God belong the Most Beautiful Names; so call Him by them, and leave those who deviate with regard to His Names. Soon they shall be recompensed for that which they used to do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Most Beautiful Names of God**

The verse states the command of God to the people, and the command is the command that is the calling upon God with His Most Beautiful Names: *Unto God belong the Most Beautiful Names; so call Him by them*. Following the mention of those who are heedless in verse 179 of the sūrah, this verse instructs the people to call upon God with His Most Beautiful Names, and the word that translates "call upon" also means "to supplicate," and the verse is here connected by the commentators with the practice of the remembering and the invoking of God, and the verse is thus one of the scriptural bases for the practice of the remembrance of God in the Islamic spirituality and especially the Sufism. The mention of the Most Beautiful Names of God is also found in the other verses (20:8; 59:24), and the similar command to call upon them is made in the other verse (17:110). The names of God are described as the Most Beautiful, a word also related to the goodness and the virtue, and are said to be beautiful to the ear as well as the heart. On the basis of the widely reported report, God is traditionally said to have the ninety-nine Beautiful Names, and the names attributed to God in the Islamic tradition are sanctified by the Islamic scripture in that they are based upon or derived from the many terms used to describe Him in the Qur'an, including those repeated frequently throughout the text such as the Compassionate, the Merciful, the Sovereign, the Holy, the One, the Hearer, the Seer, the Knower, and the Powerful.

**The Names and the Essence**
