That God has many Names is not considered to compromise His Oneness or to indicate that He has the parts or the qualities that would compromise His Oneness, rather, the Names are considered to represent the Attributes or the Qualities of His undifferentiated Essence, which are differentiated through the Self-manifestation of the Essence and then perceived in the mind of the one who is calling Him by them, and the Names also reveal the different Attributes of God, some pertaining to His Essence, and others to the different ways in which He relates to His creation. Nonetheless, each Name is understood to possess the real Divine Presence or Quality, for there are no empty titles for God, and in the Islamic metaphysics, the whole of the cosmos is considered to be in reality nothing but the reflections or theophanies of the Divine Names and Qualities. The discussion of the Names and the Qualities of God is the very important issue in the Islamic theology, philosophy, and Sufism, and the science of the Divine Names, which includes the science of the symbolic numerical significance of the Names and their constituent letters, is among the most important of the esoteric Islamic sciences. Most of the Islamic thinkers agree that the Names of God are His Attributes, which can be divided into the Attributes of Essence, such as the Knowing, the Powerful, the Living, the Hearing, the Seeing, and the Holy, and the Attributes of Action, such as the Creator, the Provider, the Originator, the Forgiver, and the Judge. In the Islamic esoterism, God and His Names are considered one, and God is understood to be present in His sanctified Names, each of which is a ladder leading to Him.

**The Deviation with Regard to the Names**

The verse closes with the command of the leaving of those who deviate with regard to the Names of God, and the command is the command that is the recompense: *and leave those who deviate with regard to His Names. Soon they shall be recompensed for that which they used to do*. The "those who deviate with regard to His Names" of the verse are said to be those who refer to God using specifically the human attributes, such as the "father" or the "son," or those who attribute the evil actions or intentions to God, or those who have the anthropomorphic understanding of the Divinity and thus describe Him in the terms that are ignorant or unworthy of Him. Several of the commentators assert that one should not refer to God using any name or description not found in the Qur'an or the Sunnah, even if it is a synonym for a known Name of God found in these sources. The deviation with regard to the Names of God can also refer to those who use His Names or the derivations from them to designate the false gods or the idols, such as the pagan people who worshipped the idols whose names appear to be the feminine forms of the name of God.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:181

> **And among those We have created, there is a community that guides by the truth and does justice thereby.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Community That Guides by the Truth**

The verse states the statement of the community among those God has created, and the statement is the statement that is on the form of the statement of verse 159 of the sūrah, in which the same description is applied to a community among the people of Moses: *And among those We have created, there is a community that guides by the truth and does justice thereby*. In the present verse, the community is widely considered to be the reference specifically to the Islamic community. Some of the commentators narrow the reference further on the basis of the report in which the Prophet ﷺ states that among his community is a group who will live according to the truth until the return of Jesus, and this group is sometimes identified as the religious scholars or those who call the others to the Islam, while some of the traditions consider it the reference to the community specifically. More generally, the verse can indicate that God will never leave the world without someone who calls to the truth, and the statement of the never leaving of the world without someone who calls to the truth is the statement that is on the form of the well-known report in which it is stated that the earth will never be left without a Proof of God. The logic of the teaching of the verse is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who are the objects of the statement of the verse are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the teaching that the community that guides by the truth and does the justice is the community that is the community that is never absent from the earth, and the community that is never absent from the earth is the community that is the proof of God on the earth, and the proof of God on the earth is the proof that is the guiding of the people by the truth and the doing of the justice by the truth. The man who guides by the truth and does the justice is the man who is in the one who is the object of the statement of the verse, and the man who is the object of the statement of the verse is the man who is in the one who is the example of the community that guides by the truth and does the justice. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the community that guides by the truth, and the statement of the community that guides by the truth is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the leading on little by little in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:182

> **And as for those who deny Our signs, We shall lead them on little by little, whence they know not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Little by Little**

The verse describes a manner rather than an event. *And as for those who deny Our signs, We shall lead them on little by little, whence they know not.* The phrase translated *little by little* is rendered by the commentators as *by degrees*, and the leading is gradual by definition — a thing done all at once could not be done by degrees.

The graduality is not incidental to the mechanism but constitutive of it. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the leading on by degrees refers to the various ways in which God leads astray those who disbelieve or do wrong, as punishment for their disbelief and wrongdoing. The punishment is thus administered in instalments, and the instalments are what make it a punishment rather than a catastrophe.

A catastrophe is survived or not. A series of small advantages is absorbed, and each one adjusts the person's estimate of his position slightly upward. The verse describes the second, and the reason it is worse is that the person undergoing it experiences improvement throughout.

The same phrase appears in Sūrah al-Qalam at 68:44, in a sentence otherwise identical to this one, which is the parallel the apparatus directs the reader to first. Its recurrence indicates that this is a named way of acting rather than a figure chosen for the occasion.

**Whence They Know Not**

The second clause is the verse's real subject. *Whence they know not* — the process is invisible to the people undergoing it, and the invisibility is stated as a fact about them rather than a limitation of the description.

This is what distinguishes the verse from an ordinary account of divine punishment. Elsewhere the Qur'an describes a people seized and knowing they are seized — the cry at the moment of the drowning, the recognition when the punishment arrives. Here the knowledge is absent throughout. There is no point in the sequence at which the person being led can look at his situation and identify it.

The clause also explains why the leading must be gradual. A sudden reversal would be visible; a slow one is not, because each step is measured against the step before it rather than against the beginning. The unknowing is produced by the little-by-littleness, and the two halves of the verse are therefore one mechanism described twice.

It follows that no amount of reflection on the part of the person being led would disclose the situation to him, since the disclosure would have to come from outside the sequence. This is why verse 99 of this sūrah poses its question in the third person — *Did they feel secure from God's plotting?* — rather than the second. The people concerned are not being addressed. They could not hear it.

**Making Wrongdoing Seem Fair**

The apparatus names two modes by which the leading is accomplished, and this verse is chiefly concerned with the second. The first is the granting of respite, which verse 183 states explicitly and which is treated there. The second is making their wrongdoing seem fair to them — see 3:14, 6:108 and 10:12 — so that they falsely assume they enjoy Divine favour and thus continue along this path toward their own punishment.

The two modes operate on different surfaces. Respite changes the world around the person: his affairs prosper, his enemies are delayed, his wealth increases. Making wrongdoing seem fair changes the person: what he is doing begins to look right to him. The first supplies evidence from outside, the second removes the capacity to read that evidence correctly.

Together they close the escape. A person whose circumstances are improving and whose conduct seems to him sound has no internal signal telling him that anything is wrong, and the verse's final clause says he has no external one either. *Whence they know not* is the summary of both.

It should be said plainly that the verse attributes the making-seem-fair to God rather than to the person's own reasoning. This is not a description of self-deception in the ordinary sense, where a person might be argued out of it by a better argument. It is a description of a condition imposed as punishment, and the punishment is precisely that the condition cannot be argued with from inside.

**A Punishment That Does Not Feel Like One**

Al-Ṭabarī's framing — leading astray *as punishment* for disbelief and wrongdoing — is the key to the verse, and it is easy to miss because the verse nowhere uses the word punishment. What it describes looks like favour from every available vantage point, including the vantage point of the person receiving it.

The Qur'an elsewhere names the specific error of inference this makes possible, at 23:55–56: *"Do they reckon that, [on account of] the wealth and the children that We have provided them, We hasten unto good for them? Nay, but they are unaware!"* The question is put as a reckoning — a calculation the people have performed and got wrong. The answer denies not the provision but the inference drawn from it, and closes by saying they are unaware. The prosperity is real and the reasoning is false, and the falseness is not corrigible by the person holding it, which is what verse 182's *whence they know not* states as a condition.

This is why the verse is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ and to the reader rather than to those it describes. Its function is not to warn the people being led — the mechanism excludes that — but to instruct the one watching. A community prospering is not thereby vindicated. A person succeeding is not thereby right.

The instruction cuts both ways and the verse does not soften the second direction. The reader is being told how to interpret his own prosperity as well as anyone else's, and the interpretive difficulty is identical: from inside a sequence of small advantages, nothing is visible. The only vantage the verse offers is the one it occupies, which is outside.

Verse 183 then supplies what this verse withholds — the first-person claim to the respite and the statement that God's scheme is firm. Read together the pair says that the invisibility of the process is not an accident of its gradualness but the design of it, and that the design has an author who announces Himself in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:183

> **And I will grant them respite; truly My scheme is firm.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Respite as the Instrument**

This verse is the second half of a pair and cannot be read without the first. Verse 182 describes God leading them on little by little — by degrees — and verse 183 names the mechanism: *And I will grant them respite; truly My scheme is firm.* The respite is not the absence of the scheme. It is the scheme.

Al-Ṭabarī explains the connection. That God shall lead them on by degrees refers to the various ways in which God leads astray those who disbelieve or do wrong as punishment for their disbelief and wrongdoing, and granting them respite is named first among them. The punishment is not postponed by the delay; the delay is how the punishment is administered.

The same pairing appears across the Qur'an and the commentators list the parallels: 13:32, where respite is granted so that a people may take their fill; 22:44 and 22:48, where the city is given respite and then seized; and 35:8. But the closest parallel is 68:44–45, the passage the note directs the reader to first, and it is closest by a wide margin — it repeats this pair almost word for word:

> *"So leave Me with those who deny this discourse. We shall lead them on little by little, whence they know not. And I shall grant them respite; truly My scheme is firm."* (68:44–45)

Two sūrahs, one sentence. The second half is identical and the first differs by a single phrase, and the difference is instructive: 68:44 opens with *leave Me with those who deny this discourse*, which turns what is here a description into a command addressed to the Prophet ﷺ. The mechanism is stated in the same words in both places; what surrounds it is not. That the note points to this passage first, ahead of the looser parallels, is a recognition of how exact the agreement is.

The word *respite* here carries that whole shape. It is not patience and it is not mercy. It is time given to a thing so that the thing may complete itself.

**Making Wrongdoing Seem Fair**

The apparatus records a second mode alongside respite, and the two work together. God leads astray by granting respite *or* by making their wrongdoing seem fair to them — see 3:14, 6:108 and 10:12 — so that they falsely assume they enjoy Divine favour and thus continue along this path toward their own punishment.

The two modes answer each other. Respite supplies the external evidence: things are going well, the crops came, the enemy was defeated, the wealth increased. Making wrongdoing seem fair supplies the internal evidence: what we are doing seems right to us. A person holding both will not question either.

That is the precision of the mechanism al-Ṭabarī describes. The punishment does not have to be felt as punishment while it operates. It has to be misread as favour, and the misreading is itself part of the operation. The verse's first person — *I will grant them respite* — is what makes this legible: the respite is claimed by God as His own act, not described as something that happened to occur.

The pastoral consequence is uncomfortable and unavoidable. Prosperity is not evidence of approval. The Qur'an says this often enough that it is a settled point rather than a local one, and this verse states it in its sharpest form: the longest respite is granted to the people whose punishment is most certain.

**My Scheme Is Firm**

The second clause — *truly My scheme is firm* — uses the language of plotting, and the Qur'an applies that language to God repeatedly. The note collects the references. That God schemes against the disbelievers and that His scheming is superior to theirs is alluded to at 7:99, 10:21, 52:42 and 86:15–17; the last opens with the human side of the same contest, *"Truly they are devising a scheme"*, before turning to the divine.

Two further formulations state the comparison outright. At 3:54: *"And they plotted, and God plotted. And God is the best of plotters."* At 8:30 the same sentence is spoken of the Quraysh's plan against the Prophet ﷺ. And at 13:42 plotting is assigned to God altogether — *unto God belongs plotting altogether* — with 14:46 in parallel and 27:50 also cited.

The word translated *firm* describes a scheme that is settled, executed in order, and not subject to interruption. That is what distinguishes it from human plotting, which the same verses describe as real but outrun. The people in 3:54 do plot, and their plot is not dismissed as nothing; it is simply placed beside a better one.

Read with verse 182 the two clauses form a single statement about time. Human plotting operates inside a short horizon and must hurry. Divine plotting does not hurry, because it has arranged for the other party to spend the interval confirming himself in error. The firmness of the scheme is what makes the respite safe to grant.

**Why the Verse Says This in the First Person**

Most of the surrounding passage speaks of God in the third person or addresses the Prophet ﷺ. This verse switches. *And I will grant them respite; truly My scheme is firm.* The first person is emphatic and it is doing specific work.

The work is to remove the ambiguity that respite otherwise carries. If prosperity were merely permitted, or merely unremarked, a person receiving it could suppose that nothing was being decided about him. The first person forecloses that. The respite is not a gap in divine attention; it is a declared act with a stated intention behind it.

It also relocates the danger. The threat in this verse is not addressed to the people being granted respite — they are not warned here, and by the logic of the passage they will not hear a warning in time. It is addressed to the reader, who is being told how to interpret the prosperity of others and, more uncomfortably, his own.

The prohibition the verse implies is stated explicitly elsewhere in this sūrah, at 7:99: *Did they feel secure from God's plotting? None feels secure from God's plotting, save the people who are losers.* That verse and this one are the two halves of one warning. Verse 99 forbids the feeling of security; verse 183 explains why the feeling arises at all and why it is dangerous. The scheme is not visible in its operation. It looks exactly like success.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:184

> **Have they not reflected? There is no madness in their companion. He is naught but a clear warner.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rebuttal of the Charge of the Madness**

The verse states the rebuttal of the charge of the Makkans, and the rebuttal is the rebuttal that is the two parts: the rhetorical question, and the statement of the non-madness: *Have they not reflected? There is no madness in their companion*. The "their companion" of the verse is the reference to the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, and the statement that there is no madness in him is the rebuttal to the claims of the Makkans that he was "mad" or, literally, "possessed by jinn," on the form of the statement of the other verses (15:6; 23:70; 34:8; 37:36; 44:14; 68:51; 81:22). The Qur'an also assures the Prophet ﷺ personally that he is not possessed in the other verses (52:29; 68:2). According to one report, the Prophet ﷺ had been atop the hill of Ṣafā calling to each clan among the Quraysh and warning them of the punishment of God, and someone in the crowd remarked that he seemed possessed, raising his voice in warning all night long, and the verse was then revealed in response. The other explanation of al-Rāzī suggests more generally that some of the Makkans dismissed the Prophet ﷺ as "possessed" because his religious practice was so different from their own, or because, when the revelation came upon him, his physical appearance and demeanor changed dramatically.

**The Reflection on the Character of the Companion**

The rhetorical question of the verse is intended to spur the pagan Makkans to consider the known character and the virtue of the Prophet ﷺ in assessing the truth and the soundness of the message he brings, and the consideration of the known character and the virtue of the Prophet ﷺ is the consideration that is on the form of the statement of the other verse (10:16), in which the Prophet ﷺ is told to say to his Makkan detractors that he tarried among them for a lifetime before it, meaning that they should know well his character. The statement of the "he is naught but a clear warner" of the verse is the statement that is the description of the Prophet ﷺ as the clear warner, and the description of the Prophet ﷺ as the clear warner is the description that is found in the other verses (15:89; 22:49; 29:50; 38:70; 46:9; 67:26), and Noah also describes himself as the clear warner in the other verses (11:25; 26:115; 71:2). The logic of the teaching of the verse is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who are the objects of the rebuttal of the verse are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are taught that the character of the prophet is the character that is the proof of the truth of the message, and the character of the prophet is the character that is the character that is known to the people before the message, and the character that is known to the people before the message is the character that is the teaching that the charge of the madness is the charge that is the charge that is refuted by the known character of the prophet. The man who is the object of the charge of the madness is the man who is in the one who is the object of the rebuttal of the verse, and the man who is the object of the rebuttal of the verse is the man who is in the one who is the object of the teaching that the prophet is the clear warner rather than the madman. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the rebuttal of the charge of the madness, and the statement of the rebuttal of the charge of the madness is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the contemplation of the dominion in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:185

> **Or have they not contemplated the dominion of the heavens and the earth, and what things God has created, and that their term may already have drawn nigh? So in what discourse after this will they believe?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Directed Attention as Evidence**

The verse asks three things of its audience at once: whether they have looked into the dominion of the heavens and the earth and into what God has created within it, and whether they have reckoned that their own appointed term may already be close. The first two are outward, the third inward, and the rhetorical force depends on the third arriving after the other two have been conceded.

This is one of a family of passages in which people — here the Makkans in particular — are asked to consider the wonders of creation as evidence of God's Reality and Power. The parallels are numerous: 14:19, 17:99, 21:30, 22:65, 26:7, 27:86, 29:19, 31:20, and 88:17–20. The recurrence across both Makkan and Madinan revelation shows this is a settled method of argument, not a passing rhetorical device.

The verb *yanẓurū* — contemplate, look into — asks for examination rather than acknowledgement. Nobody disputes that the heavens and the earth exist. The charge is that they have not actually looked at what exists, and the verse treats that as a culpable failure rather than an innocent oversight.

Three objects are set before the reader: the dominion (*malakūt*) of the heavens and the earth, the created things within it, and the nearness of their own term. The movement from cosmic order to created particulars to personal mortality is deliberate — the argument ends where the listener is.

**The Term That May Already Have Drawn Near**

Al-Zamakhsharī records that *their term* (ajaluhum) refers either to their individual deaths or to the end of the world and their impending judgment before God, or to both. The verse says it *may already have drawn nigh* — an imminence that is not scheduled.

The uncertainty is the instrument. A person who knows a deadline is distant behaves differently from one who cannot locate it, and the verse declines to locate it. Death and judgment are always imminent in the sense that nothing guarantees another day, and the argument asks the reader to hold that.

Placing mortality inside a cosmological argument changes what the cosmology is for. The heavens and the earth are not offered as an aesthetic object or an abstract proof of a First Cause. They are offered to someone whose time in them is short and unmeasured, which makes the question of what is true about them urgent rather than academic.

**In What Discourse After This**

> *So in what discourse after this will they believe?*

The rhetorical question — with parallels at 45:6 and 77:50 — indicates that believing the Qur'an ought not to have been difficult for them. It was conveyed in their own language by a man already known to them as a person of good, sound, and truthful character. The audience had none of the obstacles that make belief hard for a stranger to the speaker.

Commentators add a specifically literary ground. Al-Ṭabarsī records that the Divine provenance of the Qur'an should have been obvious to the Makkans, since its language has a literary quality and clarity none could imitate or challenge — the point pressed in the inimitability challenges at 2:23, 10:38 and 11:13. These were people whose highest cultural achievement was Arabic eloquence, addressing a text that outclassed them in it.

The question is therefore not a lament about general human unbelief. It is aimed at a specific audience with specific advantages: their own idiom, a speaker whose character they had verified over decades, and a professional standard by which they could assess the text and could not meet it. The implied answer is that if this discourse does not produce belief, nothing will — not because the evidence is exhausted but because the disposition required to weigh it is absent.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:186

> **Whomsoever God leads astray, no guide has he. And He leaves them to wander confused in their rebellion.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Non-Existence of the Guide**

The verse states the statement of the non-existence of the guide of the one whom God leads astray, and the statement is the statement that is on the form of the statement of the previous verse: *Whomsoever God leads astray, no guide has he*. The statement of the non-existence of the guide of the one whom God leads astray is the statement that is on the form of the statement of the other verse (3:73), in which the only guidance is the guidance of God, on the form of the statement of the other verses (13:33; 39:23, 36; 40:33). The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating: the one whom God leads astray is the one who is in the one who has no guide, and the one who has no guide is the one who is in the one who is the object of the teaching that the only guidance is the guidance of God, and the only guidance of God is the guidance that is the guidance that is not replaced by the guidance of the others.

**The Wandering Confused in the Rebellion**

The verse states the act of God with the people whom He leads astray, and the act is the act that is the leaving of the people to wander confused in their rebellion: *And He leaves them to wander confused in their rebellion*. The statement of the leaving of the people to wander confused in their rebellion refers to the respite that God gives the disbelievers and the wrongdoers in this life, and the respite of God is the respite that may allow for their repentance, but may also give them the opportunity to persist in the wrongdoing that assures their punishment, on the form of the teaching of the other verses (2:15; 6:110; 10:11; 23:76; 27:4). The logic of the teaching is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who are the objects of the leaving of God to wander confused in their rebellion are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are given the respite of God, and the people who are given the respite of God are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the two possibilities, and the two possibilities of the people are the two possibilities that are the possibility of the repentance and the possibility of the persistence in the wrongdoing, and the possibility of the repentance and the possibility of the persistence in the wrongdoing are the two possibilities that are the teaching that the respite of God is the respite that is the respite that is the test of the people, and the respite that is the test of the people is the respite that is the respite that is the object of the repentance of the people or the respite that is the object of the persistence of the people in the wrongdoing. The man who is the object of the leaving of God to wander confused in his rebellion is the man who is in the one who is the object of the teaching that the rebellion of the man is the rebellion that is the wandering confused in the rebellion, and the wandering confused in the rebellion is the wandering that is the persistence in the rebellion. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the non-existence of the guide and the statement of the wandering confused in the rebellion, and the statement of the non-existence of the guide and the statement of the wandering confused in the rebellion are the statement of the non-existence of the guide and the statement of the wandering confused in the rebellion that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the question about the Hour in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:187

> **They question thee about the Hour, when it will set in. Say, “Knowledge thereof lies only with my Lord. None save He shall manifest it at its proper time. Heavy shall it weigh upon the heavens and the earth. It shall not come upon you but suddenly.” They question thee as if thou knew it well. Say, “Knowledge thereof lies only with God, but most of mankind know not.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question about the Hour**

The verse states the question of the Makkans about the Hour, and the question is the question that is the asking about the time of the setting in of the Hour: *They question thee about the Hour, when it will set in*. The "Hour" of the verse refers to the onset of the apocalypse and the final end of the world, and the statement of the "knowledge thereof lies only with my Lord" of the verse indicates that the coming of the Hour is among the "unseen" matters known to God alone, on the form of the statement of the other verses (31:34; 33:63; 41:47; 43:85; 79:42–44). In the well-known report, when the Archangel Gabriel questions the Prophet ﷺ about when the Hour will arrive, he responds, "The one who is questioned knows no more than the questioner." According to some of the commentators, not knowing when the Hour will arrive, like not knowing the moment of one's own death, serves to encourage the good deeds and discourage the evil ones, since one never knows when one may be called to account. That the Hour will come upon the people suddenly indicates that the time of its coming will always remain beyond the knowledge of the human beings, even the prophets, and the verse has the great importance, therefore, for the understanding of the Islamic eschatology, and although a number of the people have made the predictions about the coming of the Hour or even claimed to be the rightly-guided leader who will set the matters right on earth before the final apocalypse, the Qur'an and the Prophet ﷺ himself explicitly reject the idea that the Prophet ﷺ, or indeed anyone but God, had the knowledge of when the Hour and the events leading up to it would arrive.

**The Weight of the Hour upon the Heavens and the Earth**

The verse states the statement of the weight of the Hour, and the statement is the statement that is the heaviness of the Hour upon the heavens and the earth: *Heavy shall it weigh upon the heavens and the earth*. The statement of the weight of the Hour upon the heavens and the earth may refer to the destructive events associated with the apocalypse as described in the Qur'an, including the splitting of the heavens, the convulsing of the earth, the destruction of the mountains, and the boiling of the seas, and the destructive events of the apocalypse are the destructive events that are the weight of the Hour upon the heavens and the earth. The other reading of the statement is the reading that the coming of the Hour will weigh heavily upon those in the heavens and the earth, that is, upon the angels, the jinn, and the human beings, because the uncertainty about the time of its arrival is difficult for them to bear, or because they dread its coming. The two readings of the statement are the two readings that are the teaching that the weight of the Hour is the weight that is the weight of the events of the apocalypse or the weight of the uncertainty of the time of the apocalypse, and the two forms of the weight of the Hour are the two forms that are the teaching that the Hour is the Hour that is the hour that is the object of the dread of the people.

**The Questioning as if He Knew It Well**

The verse states the state of the Makkans in their questioning of the Prophet ﷺ, and the state is the state that is the questioning as if the Prophet ﷺ knew it well: *They question thee as if thou knew it well*. The Makkans questioned the Prophet ﷺ about the Hour as if he knew it well, meaning they assumed that the Prophet ﷺ had acquired the knowledge of the coming of the Hour, and that he welcomed the questions about it and was willing to discuss it, and moreover, the open and the welcoming demeanor of the Prophet ﷺ gave them the confidence that he would disclose this secret matter to them because of their close relationship to him. The verse concludes by again asserting the exclusive knowledge of God of the Hour, and the assertion of the exclusive knowledge of God of the Hour is the assertion that is the statement of the "but most of mankind know not," and the statement of the "but most of mankind know not" of the verse is the statement that is on the form of the statement of the other verses (34:28, 36; 45:26), and the statement is the statement that is the teaching that the knowledge of the Hour is the knowledge that is the knowledge that is the object of the non-knowledge of most of the mankind, on the form of the teaching of the commentary of verse 102 of the sūrah. The logic of the teaching of the verse is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who question the prophet about the Hour as if he knew it well are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the teaching that the knowledge of the Hour is the knowledge that is the knowledge of God alone, and the people who are the objects of the teaching are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the statement of the "knowledge thereof lies only with God," and the people who are the objects of the statement of the "knowledge thereof lies only with God" are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are taught that the knowledge of the Hour is the knowledge that is the knowledge that is not the object of the disclosure by the prophet. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the question about the Hour and the statement of the weight of the Hour and the statement of the questioning as if he knew it well, and the statement of the question about the Hour and the statement of the weight of the Hour and the statement of the questioning as if he knew it well are the statement of the question about the Hour and the statement of the weight of the Hour and the statement of the questioning as if he knew it well that are in the form of the introduction of the statement of the non-power of the prophet in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:188

> **Say, “I have no power over what benefit or harm may come to me, save as God wills. Had I knowledge of the unseen, I would have acquired much good, and no evil would have touched me. I am naught save a warner and a bearer of glad tidings unto a people who believe.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**No Power Over Benefit or Harm**

The verse opens with a command to disclaim, and the disclaimer is as complete as language allows. *Say: I have no power over what benefit or harm may come to me, save as God wills.* The Prophet ﷺ is instructed to state that he cannot secure a good for himself or deflect an evil from himself, except as God wills it.

The restriction to himself is what gives the statement its force. He is not denying the ability to help others while retaining some private power; he is denying it in the case where every person would most expect to have it. If a man cannot guarantee his own benefit or ward off his own harm, the question of his doing so for anyone else does not arise.

The same disclaimer is put in the Prophet's ﷺ mouth twice more. 10:49: *"Say: I have no power over what harm or benefit may come to me, save as God wills. For every community there is a term."* And 72:21–22, where the denial is extended to what he can do for others: *"Say: I have no power over what harm or guidance may come to you."* The three together close the matter from both sides — nothing for himself, nothing for them.

The theological purpose is plain and it is protective. A prophet who could benefit or harm independently would be an object of petition, and petition directed to him would be indistinguishable from worship. The disclaimer removes the ground on which such a practice could be built, and it removes it in the prophet's own reported words rather than in a third-person statement about him.

**Had I Knowledge of the Unseen**

The second clause argues the same point from the other direction, as a counterfactual. *Had I knowledge of the unseen, I would have acquired much good, and no evil would have touched me.* The reasoning is an open syllogism whose conclusion is visibly false, which is what makes it persuasive: he has not acquired unlimited good and evil has touched him, therefore the premise fails.

The unseen — *al-ghayb* — is defined broadly by the commentators. It covers every realm of reality beyond ordinary human reach: the celestial realm, the inner intentions and thoughts and spiritual destiny of other people, the Hour which verse 187 has just been asked about, and all future events. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the argument here turns particularly on the future, which is the part of the unseen that would have been commercially and practically useful.

The counterfactual's two halves answer the two things a person would want foreknowledge for. *I would have acquired much good* is the offensive use — knowing where advantage lies. *No evil would have touched me* is the defensive use — knowing where danger lies. The verse claims neither, and by claiming neither it makes the same disclaimer as the first clause, but now grounded in an argument rather than an assertion.

**What "Much Good" Means**

The commentators differ on the phrase *much good*, and both readings are recorded. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī take it primarily as the accumulation of good works — a religious gain, measured in the register that survives death. The same authorities note that in theory it could also mean the acquisition of worldly benefits, and al-Zamakhsharī allows that reading as well.

The ambiguity is not carelessness. The counterfactual works on either reading, and the report attached to the verse shows why the worldly one was live for the original audience. Al-Rāzī records that some of the Quraysh asked the Prophet ﷺ whether God had informed him of the times of rising and falling prices, so that they might profit from the information in their trading.

That request is the verse's real occasion, and it explains the choice of example. They were not asking for prophecy about the Hour or for knowledge of the heart. They were asking for a market forecast, on the assumption that a man in contact with God would have an advantage in commerce. The answer given is that he has no such advantage and could not have one, because he does not know the future at all.

It is worth noting what the verse does not do with this. It does not rebuke the question as impious. It answers it as a factual claim that happens to be false, and in answering it separates prophethood from advantage in the world so completely that a man who wanted a stock tip has been told, in the same breath, that the prophet cannot protect himself from harm.

**Naught but a Warner and a Bearer of Glad Tidings**

The verse closes by stating what remains once power and foreknowledge have been disclaimed. *I am naught save a warner and a bearer of glad tidings unto a people who believe.* Two functions, and both are functions of speech.

The pairing is one of the Qur'an's most repeated descriptions of the prophetic office — at 2:119 and 2:213, 4:165, 5:19, 6:48, 11:2, 17:105, 18:56, 25:56, 33:45, 34:28 and 48:8. Its recurrence across Madinan and Makkan sūrahs alike indicates that it is not a local answer to the Quraysh but the settled definition of the role.

The definition is limiting in both directions. A warner cannot compel; a bearer of glad tidings cannot confer. Neither office includes the power to benefit or harm, which is what the first clause denied, nor the knowledge of outcomes, which is what the second denied. What is left is transmission, and the verse is content to leave exactly that.

The final qualification — *unto a people who believe* — attaches to the glad tidings rather than to the warning, and the asymmetry is real. The warning is general; the good news has an addressee. The one who believes receives the tidings as tidings, and the one who does not hears the same words as a threat. The verse does not soften this. It says that the message is one thing and its reception is another, and that the second is not in the messenger's hands either.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:189

> **He it is Who created you from a single soul, and made from it its mate, that he might find rest in her. Then, when he covered her, she bore a light burden, and carried it about. But when she had grown heavy, they called upon God, their Lord, “If Thou giveth us a healthy child, we shall surely be among the thankful.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From a Single Soul**

The verse traces the whole human race to one created soul, then to the mate made from it, and states the purpose of that making: rest. Only then does it turn to the couple's own story — conception, a burden carried lightly and then heavily, and a petition made under that weight.

The *single soul* (nafs wāḥidah) is commonly identified with Adam and its mate with Eve by al-Jawzī, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī, as at 4:1; see also 39:6. The commentators note, however, that the terms may describe the generation of human beings from male and female spouses generally — a reading that makes the verse about the structure of the human pair rather than about one ancestral couple alone.

That generality is what allows the verse to move so quickly from creation to a specific married couple's prayer. The account is not a remote origin story. It is presented as the pattern still operating, which is why the sudden shift into the first-person plural of verse 189's second half does not read as an inconsistency.

**Rest as the Purpose of the Pair**

The stated purpose of making the mate is *that he might find rest in her* — sakana, from s-k-n, the root of stillness, settling, dwelling. The same word and the same purpose appear at 30:21: "And among His signs is that He created mates for you from among yourselves, that you might find rest in them, and He established affection and mercy between you."

The commentators draw a substantive conclusion from this. Among the most important benefits of marriage are the emotional and physical comforts each spouse provides the other (see also 2:187), not merely the opportunity for procreation, which 16:72 mentions separately. The two goods are distinguished, and rest is named as the purpose here.

Al-Zamakhsharī explains why rest between spouses is natural in a particular sense: because they were created from a single soul, to find rest in one's spouse is to enjoy love, peace and intimacy together with what is, in origin, not wholly other. He goes further and compares the love for a spouse to the love for a child, insofar as both derive from the sense that the beloved is part of one's own being.

That comparison is doing real work. Love for a child is usually treated as the paradigm case of non-transactional attachment — nobody argues that a parent loves a child because the child is useful. Al-Zamakhsharī places spousal love in the same category and grounds both in the same fact about origin.

**The Light Burden and the Heavy**

> *Then, when he covered her, she bore a light burden, and carried it about. But when she had grown heavy, they called upon God, their Lord.*

The verse describes early pregnancy without naming it. The *light burden* (ḥaml khafīf) is carried about — she goes on moving, working, living — and only later does it become heavy. The shift from light to heavy is the whole of the description, and it is given from inside the experience rather than observed from outside.

What the weight produces is prayer. *They called upon God, their Lord* — the plural is notable, since the carrying has been described in the singular. At the point of need both spouses are in the sentence, and the petition is joint.

The wording of that petition is a conditional promise: *"If Thou giveth us a healthy child, we shall surely be among the thankful."* It is the ordinary bargain of a frightened person, and the verse records it without comment. Verse 190 supplies the comment, and it is severe: when the healthy child is given, they ascribe partners to God with regard to what He gave them. The sūrah sets the sincere-sounding vow beside its outcome and lets the distance between them carry the warning.

**Why the Sequence Is Arranged This Way**

Coming after two hundred verses on prophetic rejection, this passage relocates the problem. The failure it describes is not Pharaoh's open defiance but a private transaction in which gratitude was promised under pressure and withdrawn under relief.

That is a more searching indictment, and it explains why the verse begins with creation and the making of the pair. The goodness on display — rest, companionship, a child — is the very thing that gets misattributed once it arrives. Verse 190's charge of *shirk* is attached to a blessing, not to a hardship, which is the harder case for a reader to recognise in himself.

The verse's practical force lies in that recognition. The petition quoted here is one most people have made in some form, and the sūrah's point is not that making it is wrong but that keeping it is the test. Relief is where gratitude is most easily lost, because the need that produced the promise has passed.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:190

> **Then, when He gave them a healthy child, they ascribed partners unto Him with regard to that which He had given them. Exalted is God above the partners they ascribe.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From a Single Soul**

The verse is the second half of a pair, and the first half supplies its premise. Verse 189 describes the creation of humanity from a single soul and the making of its mate so that he might find rest in her, and the commentators commonly identify the soul as Adam and the mate as Eve — al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī all record that reading — while noting that the same terms can describe the generation of human beings from male and female spouses generally.

The purpose of the pairing is stated in a word: *li-yaskuna ilayhā*, that he might find rest in her. The Qur'an says the same thing of marriage as one of God's signs at 30:21, *"And among His signs is that He created mates for you from among yourselves, that you might find rest in them, and He established affection and mercy between you"*, and see also 2:187. What is being described is emotional and physical comfort, and the source note is explicit that this is among the most important benefits of marriage — not merely the opportunity for procreation, which is named separately at 16:72.

Al-Zamakhsharī draws the connection that makes verse 190 possible. To find rest in one's spouse means to enjoy love, peace and intimacy together, and this is natural in a specific sense: the spouses were made from a single soul. He maintains that love for a spouse is therefore similar to love for a child, insofar as both derive from the sense that the beloved is part of one's own being.

That observation is the hinge of the pair. Verse 189 establishes a love grounded in shared substance; verse 190 records what happened when that love was given what it asked for.

**A Difficulty in the Reading**

Taken as a story about Adam and Eve, verse 190 presents a problem the commentators face directly. Adam is a prophet in the Islamic tradition, and the verse says that when the healthy child was given, *they ascribed partners unto Him with regard to that which He had given them*. Ascribing partners is shirk. It is difficult to see how this narrative can concern Adam.

Two solutions are recorded. Some commentators hold that when the verse turns to the ascription of partners it is no longer speaking of Adam and Eve but of their offspring — human beings generally — and that the shift is marked only by context. The difficulty with this is grammatical and the note records it: the verb tenses continue in the dual. The sentence does not change number where the reading requires it to.

The second solution accepts the dual and changes the genre. On this reading the pair is not two named individuals but a type: a husband and a wife, any husband and any wife, and the narrative is a parable about what married people do when a prayer is answered.

Given the considerations on both sides, this seems the most reasonable reading, and it costs nothing exegetically. The parable loses no force by being general; if anything it gains, because a story about one couple in the distant past can be set aside while a story about every couple cannot.

**The Parable of the Answered Prayer**

What the parable describes is a specific and common failure, and it has a precise sequence. The couple, having been given a light burden and then grown heavy with it, call upon God that the child be healthy and sound. They are helpless and afraid, and in that state they address God alone. The prayer is answered. The child is born healthy. And then — in the verse's own transition, *fa-lammā ātāhumā ṣāliḥan*, then when He gave them a healthy child — they ascribe partners to Him with regard to the very thing He gave.

The ascription need not be a statue. It is the attribution of an answered prayer to some other cause: the physician, the timing, the family's merit, the remedy, one's own management of the pregnancy. The blessing arrives and the account of how it arrived quietly stops ending with God.

The Qur'an describes this movement often enough that the commentators treat it as a pattern rather than an incident. See 6:63–64, where God is called upon in distress and then forgotten when the distress is lifted; 10:22–23, the same sequence at sea; 17:67, harm at sea and turning away on reaching land; 29:65, sincerity in the crisis and shirk in the safety; 30:33 and 31:32, both turning to God under pressure and then associating once delivered.

The last two are worth reading alongside this verse because they name the mechanism. In 31:32 the wave passes over them and they call upon God sincerely; when He brings them to shore, some of them take a middle course. Sincerity under threat is easy and is not credited as virtue. The test is what the same person says when the threat has gone and the good has arrived.

**Exalted Above What They Ascribe**

The verse ends by stepping out of the narrative and saying something about God directly. *Exalted is God above the partners they ascribe.* The formula is a declaration of transcendence — *taʿālā* — and it does not argue with the ascription. It states that God is above it.

The same closing formula recurs at 9:31, where it answers the taking of rabbis and monks as lords beside God; at 10:18, where it answers the intercessors the idolaters claim; and at 16:1 and 16:3. In each case it follows a description of a specific ascription and refuses to engage with it on its own terms.

That refusal is the verse's final word on the parable. The couple's error is not answered by a proof that no partner exists, or by a listing of what God did and the causes did not. It is answered by a statement about God's height, which makes the ascription not false so much as beneath the subject. The partners are not rivals who lost; they are not in the same order of being as the thing they were set beside.

Read with verse 189 the pair forms a complete arc. Love grounded in a shared soul asks God for a child; God gives the child; the receiving of the gift becomes the occasion for misdescribing its source; and the verse closes by placing God above the misdescription. Nothing in the arc is unusual, which is why it is told of everyone.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:191

> **Do they ascribe as partners those who created naught and are themselves created?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument of the Creation and the Created**

The verse states the rhetorical question of the argument, and the argument is the argument that is the demonstration of the foolishness of the worship of anything other than God: *Do they ascribe as partners those who created naught and are themselves created?* The rhetorical question of the verse is on the form of the common Quranic argument of the other verses (6:100; 13:16; 16:20; 25:3; 31:11; 35:40; 46:4; 52:35–36), and the argument of the verse is the argument that is the teaching that God is the only Creator, and anything other than Him is itself created. The logic of the argument is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who ascribe the partners to God are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who worship the created beings, and the people who worship the created beings are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the argument of the verse, and the people who are the objects of the argument of the verse are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are taught that the object of the worship is the object that is the creator rather than the object that is the created, and the object of the worship that is the creator is the object that is God alone. The man who worships the created being is the man who is in the one who makes the error of the worship the worship of the created rather than the worship of the creator, and the man who makes the error of the worship in the form of the worship of the created rather than the worship of the creator is the man who is in the one who is the object of the argument of the verse. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the first part of the argument of the creation and the created, and the statement of the first part of the argument is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the second part of the argument in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:192

> **Those who can neither help them, nor help themselves?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument of the Helplessness**

The verse is the second part of the argument of the creation and the created, and the second part is the second part that is the rhetorical question of the helplessness: *Those who can neither help them, nor help themselves?* The all the objects of worship other than God are therefore the created beings themselves, and they are ultimately incapable of helping the others or even themselves outside of the will of God, and they are thus unable to bring the benefit or the harm to themselves or the others, on the form of the statement of verse 188 of the sūrah, in which the Prophet ﷺ indicates that he too is incapable in this regard, and the statement is on the form of the statement of the other verses (10:49; 5:76; 6:71; 10:18; 13:16; 21:43; 36:74–75; 20:88–89; 21:66; 22:12; 25:3, 55; 26:71–73), which relate to all the idols and the false deities. The logic of the argument of the helplessness is the logic that is worth stating in full: the people who worship the idols are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who worship the objects that cannot help them or themselves, and the people who worship the objects that cannot help them or themselves are the people who are in the state of the one who are the ones who are the objects of the teaching that the object of the worship is the object that is the object that is the object of the help rather than the object that is the object that is the object of the helplessness, and the object of the worship that is the object of the help is the object that is God alone, and the object of the worship that is the object of the helplessness is the object that is the idol. The man who worships the idol that cannot help him or itself is the man who is in the one who makes the error of the worship the worship of the helpless rather than the worship of the helping, and the man who makes the error of the worship in the form of the worship of the helpless rather than the worship of the helping is the man who is in the one who is the object of the argument of the verse. The two parts of the argument of the verse and the previous verse are the two parts that are the teaching that the object of the worship is the object that is the creator and the helping, and the object of the worship that is the creator and the helping is the object that is God alone. The verse, in its form, is the form of the statement of the second part of the argument of the helplessness, and the statement of the second part of the argument of the helplessness is the statement that is in the form of the introduction of the statement of the calling to the guidance in the next verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:193

> **And if you call them to guidance, they follow you not. It is the same for you whether you call them or whether you remain silent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Called to Guidance, or Called for Guidance**

The verse's first clause is grammatically ambiguous in a way the commentators take seriously rather than resolve. Al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Ṭabarī and al-Ṭūsī read it as calling the idols *to* guidance: if you call them to guidance, they follow you not — they do not respond, they do not come along, nothing happens on their side.

Al-Zamakhsharī records the other direction. The call is not *to* guidance but *for* it: when one calls upon the idols for guidance, they do not respond to the request. The preposition carries the difference, and the difference is between asking them to walk and asking them to lead.

The two readings converge on the same fact, which is why the note holds both. An object that cannot be called to guidance cannot supply it either. The verse is not distinguishing between two failures but describing one failure from the two positions in which a worshipper can stand — the one who would follow and the one who would be led.

**Why They Cannot Answer**

The apparatus grounds the non-response in physical terms, referring back to verse 195. They cannot respond in any way because they do not have ears with which they hear; nor do they have faculties of intellect and speech. The inventory in that verse — feet to walk on, hands to grasp with, eyes to perceive, ears to hear — is not decorative. Each absent organ corresponds to a function a supplicant needs from the one addressed.

Feet would allow the god to come when called. Hands would allow it to act. Eyes would allow it to see the condition of the one calling. Ears would allow it to hear the call at all. Verse 195 removes the organs; verse 193 states the consequence, which is that the call goes nowhere.

The addition of intellect and speech goes beyond the physical list and closes the last gap. Even an idol granted ears would need understanding to grasp a request and voice to answer it. The commentators supply both absences together, so that no intermediate case remains — nothing that hears but cannot understand, nothing that understands but cannot reply.

**The Third Reading: Calling the Idolaters**

Al-Ṭūsī records a further possibility, in which the pronoun shifts referent. *If you call them to guidance* may refer not to the idols but to the attempt to call the idolaters — those who ascribe partners to God, described in verses 190 through 192 — to guidance. On this reading the verse is about people, and the second half of the verse makes better sense of them than of stone.

The apparatus then draws the two cases together with a phrase that does the exegetical work: *in either case, because of their inability or unwillingness to respond to this call*. Al-Ṭabarī is credited with the observation that it does not matter whether you call them or remain silent, and the reason given covers both objects at once. The idols fail from inability. The people fail from unwillingness. The outcome for the caller is identical, which is what the verse is about.

That the two readings can be held simultaneously is a mark of the verse's construction rather than of loose commentary. The Qur'an frequently addresses an object of worship and its worshipper in the same breath, because the description of one is a description of the other. A people who cannot be called to guidance have already become like the things they call upon.

**Call, or Remain Silent**

The second half of the verse states the indifference plainly: *It is the same for you whether you call them or whether you remain silent.* The formula is one the Qur'an uses of a fixed condition rather than a passing mood, and its recurrence shows it is a technical statement about a category of people.

At 2:6 the same construction opens the description of those for whom warning is futile: *"Truly it is the same for the disbelievers whether thou warnest them or warnest them not; they do not believe."* At 36:10 it is repeated almost word for word: *"It is the same for them whether thou warnest them or warnest them not; they do not believe."* In both the addressee is the Prophet ﷺ and in both the point is that his effort does not change the outcome.

Verse 179 of this sūrah supplies the metaphor the note points to. Such people are likened to cattle — hearts with which they do not understand, eyes with which they do not see, ears with which they do not hear. The comparison is the same one verse 195 makes of the idols, and its application to human beings is what makes the third reading available at all.

It should be said clearly what this verse is not. It is not a licence to stop calling. The command to warn is given repeatedly and is not withdrawn here. What the verse removes is the expectation that the calling produces the result, and it removes that expectation so that the one calling does not mistake a lack of effect for a defect in the message or in himself.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:194

> **Truly those whom they call upon apart from God are servants like you. So call upon them! Let them answer you, if you are truthful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Servants Like You**

The verse opens by reclassifying the objects of worship. They are not rivals to God, nor independent powers, nor intermediaries holding a share of the divine authority. They are *ʿibād* — servants, owned beings — and the qualification that follows is the one that stings: *like you*. *Amthālukum*. The worshipper and the thing worshipped belong to the same category.

The point is not that the idols are evil or false in some dramatic sense but that they are junior. They belong to God and stand under His sovereignty, in the same standing as the people bowing to them. A servant who petitions another servant, over the head of the master who owns them both, has misdescribed the situation he is in.

This is the sūrah's sustained argument against shirk reduced to a single word. Elsewhere the Qur'an disputes the *power* of the false deities; here it disputes their *status*, and the dispute over status is the more damaging of the two, because a being with no power might still be pitied or honoured while a being with the same rank as its worshipper cannot be addressed at all.

**The Challenge to Call**

The verse then does something unusual: it grants the request. *So call upon them.* There is no prohibition issued and no argument offered. The idolaters are told to do exactly what they were doing, and to see what happens.

> *"Let them answer you, if you are truthful."*

The conditional *in kuntum ṣādiqīn* — if you are truthful — is the hinge. Truthfulness here is not sincerity but accuracy: the claim that these beings can hear and respond. The verse proposes an empirical test of a metaphysical claim, and it proposes it in the imperative, which removes the excuse of not having tried.

The challenge is structurally identical to the one the Qur'an puts to those who deny the resurrection — produce your proof, if you are truthful — and it works the same way. A claim that cannot survive being acted on was never a claim about the world. The verse does not need to refute the idolater; it needs him to make one phone call.

**Less Capable Than the Worshipper**

Verse 195 presses the same advantage with a sequence of rhetorical questions that Ibn Kathīr and al-Māwardī treat as the core of the argument. Do the idols have feet to walk on? Hands to grasp with? Eyes to see by? Ears to hear with?

The list is deliberately physical and deliberately descending. Feet, hands, eyes, ears — the equipment of an agent. The idols are not described as weak gods but as insensate objects, and the comparison being forced is not between them and God but between them and the person addressing them.

That is the argument's real edge. How is it that a being with feet and hands and eyes and ears worships a thing with none? The worshipper outranks his god in every capacity the questions name. The verse is not asking the idolater to think more highly of God; it is asking him to notice that he is already, by a wide margin, the more capable of the two.

**Scheme Against Me, and Grant Me No Respite**

The passage closes by turning the challenge from the idols to their worshippers, and the turn is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ himself. He is told to invite the full conspiracy: let them scheme against him together, and let them not give him a moment's respite.

The formula is not new to this sūrah. Noah issues it at 10:71 and Hūd at 11:55, in both cases to a people who have the numbers and the intention to kill him, and in both cases the invitation is genuine rather than rhetorical bravado. At 77:39 the same challenge is put to the deniers on the Day of Judgment itself: *So if you have a scheme, then scheme against Me.*

The reason a prophet can issue it is given elsewhere and does not depend on his own strength. Nothing in the heavens or the earth can thwart God — 35:44 — while God's own scheme holds even where He grants respite, which is the subject of verses 182 and 183 of this sūrah. The respite the disbeliever is enjoying is not evidence that the scheme against him has failed; it is the mechanism by which it is being completed.

Read in sequence the two challenges are one argument. The idols cannot answer when they are called, and their worshippers cannot harm the one who calls them. The verse removes both the object of worship and the threat that protected it, and leaves the idolater with nothing to do and nothing to fear from.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:195

> **Have they feet with which they walk? Have they hands with which they grasp? Have they eyes with which they perceive? Have they ears with which they hear? Say, “Call upon your partners, then scheme against me, and grant me no respite.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Four Questions About the Senses**

The four questions run down the body: feet, hands, eyes, ears. Each is phrased identically and each expects the same answer — no. Ibn Kathīr and al-Māturīdī note the argument's shape: the capabilities of these idols fall short even of those enjoyed by the human beings who worship them, so how can one worship what is less capable than oneself?

The sequence is not random. Walking and grasping are powers of acting on the world; seeing and hearing are powers of receiving it. The idols have neither. They cannot do anything and they cannot register anything, which between them excludes every function a person might want from a deity — intervention and attention.

The preceding verse has already established the ground for the mockery. These false deities are *servants like* their worshippers (7:194), belonging to God and subject to His Sovereignty. The questions in verse 195 are the working-out of that claim: not a metaphysical demonstration but an inventory.

**The Challenge Issued**

> *Say, "Call upon your partners, then scheme against me, and grant me no respite."*

The Prophet is told to invite his opponents to their best effort — to call on the partners, plot against him, and give him no quarter. The formulation is identical or closely similar to challenges issued by earlier prophets: Noah at 10:71 and Hūd at 11:55 both tell their peoples to scheme against them and not hold back.

That repetition places this verse inside a prophetic pattern rather than treating it as a personal act of defiance. The same words are put in the mouth of God Himself at 77:39, addressing the deniers on the Day of Judgment: "So if you have a scheme, then scheme against Me!" The challenge scales from prophet to God without changing form.

The phrase *grant me no respite* (lā tunẓirūn) is the sharpest part. The challengers are asked not to delay, not to show restraint, not to give warning. Everything that would normally make an attack more likely to succeed is waived in advance, which is how the verse signals confidence that success is impossible regardless.

**Why the Scheme Is Known to Fail**

The invitation is understood to be safe because the schemes will ultimately fail: nothing in the heavens or on earth can thwart God (35:44). By contrast God's own scheme is firm, even while He grants them respite — see 7:182–83, where He extends respite to those who deny His signs while His plan proceeds.

The contrast with verse 15 in this same sūrah is exact and worth holding together. Iblīs asks for respite and receives it; the Prophet tells his opponents to give him none, because their refusal of respite would change nothing. Respite in the first case is a real grant with a real term; in the second it is irrelevant to the outcome. The same word does opposite work depending on who holds the power.

The verse is therefore not a taunt resting on personal courage. It rests on an assessment of what the opponents actually have: objects that cannot walk, grasp, see or hear, and whose worshipper is more capable than they are. The challenge is calibrated to that inventory.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:196

> **Truly my Protector is God, Who sent down the Book, and He protects the righteous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer to the Challenge**

This verse cannot be read apart from the one before it, because it is the answer to a challenge the Prophet ﷺ has just been told to issue. Verse 195 ends with the command *Say: Call upon your partners* — and then, in the same verse, with the invitation *scheme against me, and grant me no respite*.

The apparatus explains the challenge. It is that they try their best to thwart God and the mission of His Prophet. The invitation is issued to people who have just been shown that their gods have no feet to walk on, no hands to grasp, no eyes to perceive, no ears to hear — and who are then told to bring those gods and do their worst, immediately and without delay.

Verse 196 is what the Prophet ﷺ says after issuing it, and its position is the point. He has invited the combined hostility of the city and its gods. The next sentence is not a calculation of the odds but a statement of who is on his side.

The same challenge is put in the mouths of two earlier prophets, and the apparatus cites both. Noah at 10:71, addressing a people to whom his presence and his reminding have become grievous. And Hūd at 11:55, in words nearly identical to this verse's: *"So scheme against me then, all together, and grant me no respite."* Three prophets, three peoples, one formula — and in each case the formula is followed by a statement of trust rather than by a withdrawal.

**My Protector Is God**

The answer opens in the first person and names a single relationship. *Truly my Protector is God.* The word translated *Protector* is *waliyy*, carrying the senses of guardian, patron, ally and one who is close — the same root that governs the Qur'anic language of alliance and of God's nearness to the believer.

The emphatic particle *truly* at the head of the sentence is doing work against an implied objection. The challenge of verse 195 invited the idolaters to act; the natural human response to having issued such an invitation is to look around for support. The verse begins by saying where the support is, and by saying it with emphasis.

What the claim excludes is as important as what it asserts. It does not say *God is my Protector and so are my tribe, my companions, my followers*. The protection is named in the singular and attributed to one source, immediately after a challenge that would have made any human ally's contribution decisive.

That the Prophet ﷺ is instructed to say this in his own voice, rather than having it said about him, is characteristic of the passage. Verses 194 to 196 are built as a series of things he is to say — call upon them, ask them whether they have ears, scheme against me, my Protector is God — and the sequence makes the final statement part of the challenge rather than a retreat from it.

**Who Sent Down the Book**

The verse does not leave the claim bare. It qualifies the Protector with a relative clause: *God, Who sent down the Book.* The qualification identifies God by an act, and the act chosen is revelation.

This is not the only act by which God could have been identified here. The surrounding passage has available to it creation, sovereignty, the sending of prophets, the destruction of prior peoples. The verse selects the sending down of the Book, and the selection is an argument.

The argument is that the protection claimed is of a piece with the mission that provoked the challenge. The Prophet ﷺ has been threatened because of what he brought. He answers by naming as his Protector the one who sent him with it, so that the threat and the protection are referred to the same source. To scheme against him is to scheme against the sender, and verse 195 has already said what the idols can do about that.

The Qur'an makes the same point directly at 35:44, cited by the apparatus in this connection: *naught in the heavens or upon the earth can thwart God*. The verse there follows a description of God's respite to a people who would have been seized for their deeds, and the conclusion drawn is that no scheme succeeds against Him. The protection claimed in verse 196 rests on that impossibility.

It also answers 77:39, where God Himself issues the same challenge on the Day of Judgment — *"So if you have a scheme, then scheme against Me!"* — to those who have been given every provision and denied it. The invitation the Prophet ﷺ extends in verse 195 is an extension of one God will extend finally, and verse 196 states why both can be extended safely.

**He Protects the Righteous**

The verse's last clause moves from the singular to the general. *And He protects the righteous.* Having named his own Protector, the Prophet ﷺ states a rule that includes him rather than a privilege that sets him apart.

The shift is deliberate and it changes the character of the claim. A statement that God protects this prophet would be a report about one man's situation, which his opponents could accept while continuing to oppose him. A statement that God protects the righteous makes the protection conditional on a quality available to anyone, and places the Prophet ﷺ among those who have it rather than above them.

It also relocates the ground of the contest. The idolaters' scheme is aimed at a person; the verse answers that the person is an instance of a class, and that what protects him protects the class. To prevail against him would require prevailing against the rule, and the rule is God's.

The apparatus draws the contrast with verses 182 and 183, where God grants respite to those who deny His signs and His scheme is firm. The two passages describe the same divine acting from opposite sides. There, the disbelievers are given time and led on whence they know not. Here, the righteous are protected and know it, and say so. The difference is not in God's power but in who is being described and what they are aware of.

That awareness is the verse's last word on the challenge. The idolaters are invited to scheme and to grant no respite; the Prophet ﷺ states that his Protector is the one who sent down the Book and who protects the righteous. He is not claiming to be untouchable. He is claiming to be on the side of a rule that does not depend on his being untouchable, and the claim is offered in the same breath as the invitation, which is what makes it a challenge rather than a comfort.

---
## Sūrah al-Aʿrāf 7:197

> **And those whom you call upon apart from Him can neither help you, nor help themselves.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Statement of the Same Fact**

This verse says what verses 191 and 192 have already said, and the saying again is the point. Those verses put the matter as two questions — *Do they ascribe as partners those who created naught and are themselves created? Those who can neither help them, nor help themselves?* — and this verse puts it as a statement, in the indicative, addressed directly.

The shift from question to declaration is a shift in what is being asked of the hearer. A rhetorical question invites agreement; a statement requires only that it be heard. By the third telling the argument is no longer being made but registered, which is what a passage does when it considers a point closed.

It is also worth noting what the reiteration adds. The questions were about *them* — the partners the idolaters ascribe. This verse is about *you*: those whom *you* call upon. The general proposition has been turned into a second-person address, and the person being described as helpless is no longer a hypothetical but the hearer's own god.

**Neither You Nor Themselves**

The two negations are ordered, and the order is the argument. *Can neither help you, nor help themselves.* The first is what the worshipper needs and does not have. The second is what removes any remaining excuse for supplying it.

A being unable to help its worshipper might still be worth petitioning if it were powerful in itself and merely constrained — a friend at court, an intermediary with access. The second negation closes that possibility. These objects cannot help themselves. Whatever is happening to them, they cannot alter; whatever is done to them, they cannot prevent.

The comparison the verse forces is therefore not between the idols and God but between the idols and the people worshipping them, and it comes out against the idols. The worshipper can act on his own behalf. The thing he worships cannot.
